Multipole and Berezinskii-Kosterlitz-Thouless Transitions in the Two-component Plasma Thanks: S.S. was supported by NSF grant DMS-2000205 and by the Simons Foundation through the Simons Investigator program.
Jeanne Boursier, Sylvia Serfaty

Abstract
. We study the two-dimensional two-component Coulomb gas in the canonical ensemble and at inverse temperature β > 2 \beta>2 . In that regime, the partition function diverges and the interaction needs to be cut off at a length scale λ > 0 \lambda>0 . Particles of opposite charges tend to pair into dipoles of length scale comparable to λ \lambda , which themselves can aggregate into multipoles. Despite the slow decay of dipole–dipole interactions, we construct a convergent cluster expansion around a hierarchical reference model that retains only intra-multipole interactions. This yields a large deviation result for the number of 2 ​ p 2p -poles as well as a sharp free energy expansion as N → ∞ N\to\infty and λ → 0 \lambda\to 0 with three contributions: (i) the free energy of N N independent dipoles, (ii) a perturbative correction, and (iii) the contribution of a non-dilute subsystem. The perturbative term has two equivalent characterizations: (a) a convergent Mayer series obtained by expanding around an i.i.d. dipole model; and (b) a variational formula as the minimum of a large-deviation rate function for the empirical counts of 2 ​ p 2p -poles. The Mayer coefficients exhibit transitions at β p = 4 − 2 p \beta_{p}=4-\tfrac{2}{p} , that accumulate at β = 4 \beta=4 , which corresponds to the Berezinskii-Kosterlitz-Thouless transition in the low-dipole-density limit. At β = β p \beta=\beta_{p} the p p -dipole cluster integrals switch from non-integrable to integrable tails. The non-dilute system corresponds to the contribution of large dipoles: we exhibit a new critical length scale R β , λ R_{\beta,\lambda} which transitions from λ − ( β − 2 ) / ( 4 − β ) \lambda^{-(\beta-2)/(4-\beta)} to + ∞ +\infty as β \beta crosses the critical inverse temperature β = 4 \beta=4 , and which can be interpreted as the maximal scale such that the dipoles of that scale form a dilute set.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Setting of the problem and objectives

We consider the canonical continuum Coulomb gas given as the ensemble with probability distribution proportional to

(1.1)

\exp\left(-\beta\mathsf{F}(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})\right)\mathrm{d}\vec{X}_{N}\mathrm{d}\vec{Y}_{N},

for configurations
(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})
, with
\vec{X}_{N}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in\Lambda^{N}
and
\vec{Y}_{N}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{N})\in\Lambda^{N}
of
N
positive and
N
negative particles in the square
\Lambda=[0,\sqrt{N}]^{2}
of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, with logarithmic interaction energy

(1.2)

\mathsf{F}(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{i\neq j}-\log|x_{i}-x_{j}|-\log|y_{i}-y_{j}|+2\sum_{i,j}\log|x_{i}-y_{j}|\right),

and
where
\mathrm{d}\vec{X}_{N}
and
\mathrm{d}\vec{Y}_{N}
denote the Lebesgue measure on
\Lambda^{N}
. Here, the parameter
\beta>0
is the inverse temperature.

This model is known to exhibit a phase transition at
\beta=4
, called the Berezinskii-Kosterlitz-Thouless (BKT) transition, discovered independently in
the 1970s in
[
Kos74
,
KT73
]
and in
[
Ber71
]
. The integral of the expression in (
1.1
) (called partition function) diverges as soon as
\beta\geq 2
, so we introduce a small‑scale cutoff
\lambda>0
. This divergence corresponds to the dipole transition that occurs at
\beta=2
, between a situation with “free charges” for
\beta<2
and a situation with pairing of opposite-sign particles (dipoles) of length scale
\lambda
for
\beta\geq 2
.
It was predicted in
[
BGN82
,
GN85
]
that such a system also has an infinite sequence of transitions at

(1.3)

\beta_{p}\coloneqq 4-\frac{2}{p},

with
p\geq 1
an integer,
which corresponds to the divergence (at small scale) of the free energy of
2p
-poles (i.e. sets of
p
dipoles clustered together).
Indeed, formal computations indicate that the Gibbs weight of a dipole of size
\lambda
is
\sim\lambda^{2-\beta}
which diverges if and only if
\beta>\beta_{1}\coloneqq 2
. Similarly, the Gibbs weight of a
2p
-pole of size
\lambda
is
\sim\lambda^{p(2-\beta)+2(p-1)}
which diverges if and only if
\beta>\beta_{p}\coloneqq 4-\frac{2}{p}
.

Most of the related works have analyzed the grand canonical lattice Coulomb gas, via the sine-Gordon transformation. The existence of the BKT phase transition was proven in the pioneering paper of Fröhlich and Spencer
[
FS81
]
.
In that paper, the authors write:

“We believe
that the techniques of Section 5 will eventually permit us to prove convergence of an expansion of the two-dimensional Coulomb gas in terms of
neutral multipole configurations, at low density and low temperature,
designed to imply the existence of the Kosterlitz-Thouless transition. But
the required combinatorial and refined electrostatic estimates are still
missing.”

Moreover, the status of the transitions at
\beta_{p}
predicted by
[
BGN82
,
GN85
]
has remained debated, as footnotes in
[
FLL95
]
reveal.

In this paper, we rigorously establish for the first time the multipole transitions at
\beta_{p}
, as well as aspects of the BKT transition at
\beta=4
(in the low-dipole-density limit), and we go further by providing a new free energy expansion and large deviations estimates in the asymptotics of
\lambda\to 0
, by bringing the missing pieces of the puzzle alluded to in
[
FS81
]
: new electrostatic estimates combined with diagrammatic expansions (more precisely, cluster expansions).

We also introduce a new
critical length scale

(1.4)

R_{\beta,\lambda}=\begin{cases}\lambda^{-\frac{\beta-2}{4-\beta}}&\text{ if $\beta<4$ }\\
+\infty&\text{ if $\beta\geq 4$}\end{cases}

that changes as
\beta
crosses the critical inverse temperature
4
, and which can be interpreted as the maximal scale such that the dipoles of that scale form a dilute set, i.e. the dipole size below which dipoles are typically far from other dipoles of the same size.

Our first main result is an expansion of the partition function
Z_{N,\beta}^{\lambda}
(of the model suitably truncated at
\lambda
)
as we let
N\to\infty
then
\lambda\to 0
: roughly, we show that for
\beta\in(\beta_{p},\beta_{p+1}]
,

(1.5)

\log Z_{N,\beta}^{\lambda}=\log N!+\text{free energy of $N$ i.i.d.~dipoles}+\text{Mayer series}+\text{non-dilute contribution}.

This formula is a perturbative expansion around an i.i.d. dipole model. The exact statement we prove is given in Theorem
1
below. The Mayer series terms (named after J. E. Mayer, who introduced the cluster expansion in statistical mechanics
[
May37
]
) are the non-divergent perturbative terms, corresponding to the contributions of clusters of
k
dipoles with
k\leq p
. The non-dilute contribution is an error term which we show is, for
\beta\leq 4
, of order
NR_{\beta,\lambda}^{-2}
(with logarithmic corrections at each
\beta_{p}
), which is sharp. In the regime
\beta\geq 4
, we can keep the Mayer series terms up to an arbitrary order
p_{0}
, and obtain an error of order
\lambda^{2p_{0}}
.
The transition at
\beta_{p}
corresponds to the transition from the
p
-dipole clusters at large scales being non-integrable to them being integrable (as expected from the way
\beta_{p}
was introduced), and dominant over the non-dilute contribution
NR_{\beta,\lambda}^{-2}
.

We also provide a probabilistic interpretation of the multipole transitions: we show a large deviation result on
the fraction of
2p
-poles, which transitions from
R_{\beta,\lambda}^{-2}
to
\lambda^{2(p-1)}
at
\beta_{p}
. The Mayer series term in (
1.5
) is interpreted as the minimum of the rate function associated with this large deviation result. The precise statements are given in Theorem
2
below.

The paper develops a framework tailored to long-range dipole interactions. To our knowledge, this is the first non-iterated cluster expansion with long-range interactions. The key novelties are:

•

Matching compatible with cluster expansion.
We use a matching between positive and negative charges (forming dipoles) which is compatible with the cluster expansion, i.e. which enjoys a nice separation of variables property.

•

Hierarchical multipole model.
A direct perturbative expansion around an i.i.d. dipole model fails to converge. Instead, we introduce a hierarchical multipole reference model that retains only intra-multipole interactions and we perform a convergent perturbative (cluster) expansion around it. The non-divergent diagrams from this multipole Mayer series are shown to agree term-by-term with those of the dipole Mayer series.

•

Removing divergences by energy truncation.
For
\beta\in(\beta_{p},\beta_{p+1})
, the
k
-dipole clusters with
k>p
are divergent, so a naïve multipole cluster expansion fails. We “sandwich” the system between two auxiliary models whose interaction ranges lie strictly below the critical scale
R_{\beta,\lambda}
, thereby removing large-scale divergences before running the expansion; the two auxiliary models have identical Mayer series up to a sharp error term. They are obtained via new electrostatic estimates which provide effective upper and lower bounds
for the energy.

•

Extracting an ideal dilute subsystem.
We isolate an “ideal” subsystem (dipoles of length smaller than the critical scale and multipoles of bounded cardinality, subject to additional constraints) and perform the cluster expansion solely on this part; we then show that the complement has negligible density.

•

Exploiting cancellations.
Even after truncation, the dipole-dipole interaction remains long-range: it decays in inverse distance squared. However it exhibits a cancellation by averaging over the angle between the dipoles. Thus, the Mayer bond splits into a non-integrable odd contribution and an even integrable contribution. Hence, by a symmetry argument, we reduce to Eulerian graphs with odd weight (every vertex has even degree), supplemented by summable corrections from the even part. This Eulerian structure yields convergent integrals and a summable cluster series, with optimal bounds on the contribution of large diagrams.

1.2.
Physical background and literature

1.2.1.
BKT transition

The celebrated BKT transition (see, e.g.,
[
BG16
]
and the recent comprehensive text
[
AM25
]
for a description and review) is expected to occur in a broad class of two‑dimensional models that support quantized vortices in a neutral ensemble. The prototypical example is the XY (rotator) spin model. The phenomenon has far‑reaching implications in condensed‑matter physics; early motivations came from models of dislocations and superfluids.

A hallmark of the transition is the change in correlation decay: above the critical temperature, correlations decay exponentially, whereas below it they decay algebraically (power‑law); see
[
MS77
,
BFL77
,
BF80
,
FS81
,
MKP90
,
MK91
,
JKKN77
,
GS24
,
GS23
]
.

In the XY model (probably the one that has attracted the most attention in the mathematical physics community) unit spins are sampled on a lattice, constituting a
\mathbb{U}(1)
analogue of the Ising model. The transition is then explained by the formation of topological vortices, which are points around which the spin field has a nonzero degree or winding number. Below the transition, vortices bind into dipole pairs of opposite charge (no free charge at macroscopic scale); above it, they unbind (at mesoscopic scale) and behave essentially as free particles.

The Coulomb or vortex gas version of the problem corresponds to the situation where only the (logarithmic) interactions between the vortices are retained while the spin-wave contribution, corresponding to the fluctuations around the vortex configurations
[
Kos74
,
KT73
,
KLS88
]
, has been removed. The Coulomb gas is thus a fundamental model on which to understand the BKT transition, as seen in the original paper of Kosterlitz and Thouless
[
KT73
]
.

1.2.2.
Approaches to the transitions

The lattice Coulomb gas (i.e. the version where the particles or vortices can only sit on lattice points) was studied in the seminal work of Fröhlich-Spencer
[
FS81
]
via the sine-Gordon representation, allowing for the analysis of the decay rate of correlation functions, thus giving the first proof of the BKT transition, see also
[
MS77
,
MKP90
,
MK91
]
.

The role of multipoles was put forward in
[
FS81
]
which expands the grand canonical lattice Coulomb gas in sine-Gordon representation as a convex combination of “neutral multipole gases” with small effective activities when
\beta
is large, see
[
KP17
]
for a modern account.
The same model was also analyzed more precisely in
[
BGN82
,
GN85
]
, also proceeding by the sine-Gordon transformation and using field theory techniques.
These two papers are the first to derive, via a multiscale Mayer (or cluster) expansion,
the sequence of critical temperatures
\beta_{p}

which appear as the transition temperature for
2p
-neutral clusters in the Mayer expansion.
The cancellations that make the series converge correspond to a renormalizability of the field theory.

Note that the model that
[
BGN82
]
and
[
GN85
]
work on is a model with a specific ultraviolet cutoff of the interaction, effectively equivalent to cutting off at
\lambda=1
, which blurs the asymptotic effects of
\lambda\to 0
that we observe. In particular,
[
BGN82
,
GN85
]
did not predict the ordering of the coefficients in (
1.5
) or the precise proportion of multipoles, but only the transition temperatures.

There is also a substantial line of work on the primal grand‑canonical Coulomb gas itself (as opposed to the dual sine–Gordon formulation)
[
AC92
,
AC97a
,
AC97b
,
AF95b
,
AF95a
,
ABCM03
]
, which studies the BKT transition via cluster expansions and develops non‑rigorous resummations that are consistent with renormalization group (RG) predictions for the dual problem.

Despite this progress, a complete mathematical picture remains elusive. Most rigorous approaches rely on the sine-Gordon transformation together with sophisticated RG techniques
[
JKKN77
,
BF80
,
Fal12
,
LRV15
,
KM19
,
BB21
,
BPR24a
,
BPR24b
]
.
Note that in
[
KM19
,
BB21
]
, the proof works only up to
\beta=6\pi
(the equivalent of
\beta=3
for us) while they expect the result to hold up to
\beta=8\pi
(corresponding to the BKT transition
\beta=4
for us), but the combinatorics in the Mayer series becomes more complex for
\beta\in(6\pi,8\pi)
. We encounter an analogous dichotomy: when
\beta<3
no cancellations in the activities are needed and the argument could be considerably simplified, whereas when
\beta\geq 3
a finer analysis exploiting delicate cancellations is required, see below.

1.2.3.
Prior results on our model

Prior to our work, the ensemble (
1.1
) was studied in the regime
\beta<2
in
[
GP77
,
DL74
,
LSZ17
]
.
The results of
[
LSZ17
]
, building on important insights from
[
GP77
]
and techniques developed for the study of the one-component Coulomb gas in
[
SS15
,
RS15
,
LS17
,
LS18
]
, show an expansion of
\log Z_{N,\beta}^{0}
as
N\to\infty
up to
o(N)
,
with the order-
N
coefficient identified as the minimum of a large deviation rate function, improving on
[
DL74
,
GP77
]
, which captured only the leading
N\log N
term. This was complemented by
a large deviations principle on point processes, which characterizes a situation with free interacting particles, with competition between the attraction of opposite charges and the entropic repulsion. This corresponds to the situation of “free charges” for
\beta<2
.

In our prior paper
[
BS24
]
, using large deviations techniques, a description of dipoles based on nearest-neighbor graphs inspired by
[
GP77
]
as well as electrostatic estimates, we showed that for
\beta\geq 2
the system concentrates on configurations with a large proportion of dipoles of size
O(\lambda)
, and the expansion

\log Z_{N,\beta}^{\lambda}=\log N!+\text{free energy of $N$ i.i.d. dipoles}+No_{\lambda}(1),

where
o_{\lambda}(1)
was a positive power of
\lambda
for
\beta>2
and a positive power of
|\log\lambda|^{-1}
for
\beta=2
.

Acknowledgments:
The authors are particularly indebted to Ofer Zeitouni for many invaluable discussions from which this work benefited very much.
They also thank Christophe Garban, Tom Spencer, Angel Alastuey, Eyal Lubetzky and Ahmed Bou-Rabee for very helpful discussions.

2.
Definitions, method of proof and main results

2.1.
Model

We now describe the precise model we study, by providing the definition of the interaction truncation introduced in
[
BS24
]
and adopted here.

Let us for shortcut always denote

(2.1)

\mathsf{g}(x)=-\log|x|,

and we will abuse notation by considering
\mathsf{g}
as either a function of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
or of
\mathbb{R}
depending on the context.

Truncating the interaction involves introducing a small length scale
\lambda
and
renormalizing
the divergent part of the energy as
\lambda\to 0
. A natural way is to truncate the energy at a distance
\lambda
and consider

(2.2)

\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j}\min(\mathsf{g}(x_{i}-x_{j}),\mathsf{g}(\lambda))+\min(\mathsf{g}(y_{i}-y_{j}),\mathsf{g}(\lambda))-2\min(\mathsf{g}(x_{i}-y_{j}),\mathsf{g}(\lambda)).

The precise method of truncation of the interaction is not really important, and here we propose a variant of this which is convenient for our techniques: instead of truncating
\mathsf{g}
we consider charges smeared on discs of radius
\lambda
, with
\lambda
small, interacting otherwise in the normal Coulomb fashion. Let
\chi
denote a radial nonnegative function, supported in the unit ball
B(0,1)
and such that
\int\chi=1
. We will only assume that
\chi
is bounded, for instance
\chi
could be the normalized indicator function of
B(0,1)
.
We let
\delta_{z}^{(\lambda)}=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\chi(\frac{\cdot-z}{\lambda})
, it is a measure of mass
1
supported on
B(z,\lambda)
. We then let

(2.3)

\kappa\coloneqq\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\delta_{0}^{(1)}(x)\delta_{0}^{(1)}(y),

and observe, by scaling, that

(2.4)

\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\delta_{0}^{(\lambda)}(x)\delta_{0}^{(\lambda)}(y)=\mathsf{g}(\lambda)+\kappa.

The energy we consider is

(2.5)

\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})=\frac{1}{2}\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\mathrm{d}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\lambda)}-\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\lambda)}\right)(x)\mathrm{d}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\lambda)}-\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\lambda)}\right)(y)-N(\mathsf{g}(\lambda)+\kappa).

Here, compared to (
1.2
) we have reinserted the self-interaction terms which are no longer infinite but equal to
\mathsf{g}(\lambda)+\kappa
,
and then subtracted them off.

We will denote by

(2.6)

\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(z)=\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\delta_{0}^{(\lambda)}(x)\delta_{z}^{(\lambda)}(y)=\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\lambda)}*\delta_{0}^{(\lambda)}(z),

the effective interaction between two points at distance
|z|
. The function
\delta_{0}^{(\lambda)}*\delta_{0}^{(\lambda)}
is radial and supported in
B(0,2\lambda)
, hence from the mean-value theorem or Newton’s theorem,

(2.7)

\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(z)=\mathsf{g}(z)\quad\text{for }|z|\geq 2\lambda.

Thus we see that
\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}
is the same as (
2.2
) except with

\min(\mathsf{g}(x_{i}-x_{j}),\mathsf{g}(\lambda))
replaced by
\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-x_{j})
, and if the distances between points are larger than
\lambda
, the interactions coincide and
\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}
coincides with
\mathsf{F}
in (
1.2
).
If
\lambda=0
then the definition in (
2.5
) coincides with
\mathsf{F}
, as proved in
[
LSZ17
]
– this is essentially Newton’s theorem and Green’s formula.

We will thus work with (
2.5
) and study

(2.8)

\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{N,\beta}^{\lambda}=\frac{1}{Z_{N,\beta}^{\lambda}}\exp\left(-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})\right)\mathrm{d}\vec{X}_{N}\mathrm{d}\vec{Y}_{N}

in the limit where
\lambda
tends to zero, where

(2.9)

Z_{N,\beta}^{\lambda}=\int_{\Lambda^{2N}}\exp\left(-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})\right)\mathrm{d}\vec{X}_{N}\mathrm{d}\vec{Y}_{N}.

When
\beta<2
one can immediately set
\lambda=0
and recover the model studied in
[
LSZ17
]
, but when
\beta>2
,
\log Z_{N,\beta}^{\lambda}
diverges as
\lambda\to 0
.

2.2.
Dipoles and multipoles

Throughout the paper, for any integer
n
, we write
[n]\coloneqq\{1,\dots,n\}
.

The first question that arises is to properly define dipoles, i.e. how one pairs the positive charges with the negative ones.
We present a notion of dipoles based on a stable matching algorithm. This is a different definition from
[
BS24
]
in which dipoles were defined based on the nearest-neighbor graph of the particles (irrespective of their sign), as in
[
GP77
]
.

We introduce a matching between the positive charges
(x_{i})_{i\in[N]}
and the negative charges
(y_{i})_{i\in[N]}
. We use the stable matching
\sigma_{N}
defined as follows: starting from
r=0
, grow discs of radius
r
around each
x_{i}
. If
B(x_{i},r)
is the first disc to touch a negative charge, say
y_{j}
, (if several, pick the one with the smallest index) then we set
\sigma_{N}(i)=j
. The particles
x_{i}
and
y_{j}
are then removed from the family of particles, and we repeat the operation. This procedure defines a random permutation of
[N]
.

Definition 2.1
.

We call dipoles the pairs
(x_{i},y_{\sigma_{N}(i)})
, where
\sigma_{N}
is the stable matching as above.
We denote by
r_{i}=|x_{i}-y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}|
the dipole size.

Figure 1
.
A stable matching. The positive charges are represented as red dots and negative charges as blue dots.

Without loss of generality, up to the multiplicative factor
N!
, one can reduce to the situation where
\sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Id}
.
The stable matching has the nice property (see Section
4.1
) that

(2.10)

\{\sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Id}\}=\bigcap_{1\leq i,j\leq N:i\neq j}\mathcal{A}_{ij},

where
\mathcal{A}_{ij}
is an event depending only on
x_{i},y_{i},x_{j},y_{j}
: it is the geometric condition that says that
x_{i}
is matched to
y_{i}
and
x_{j}
to
y_{j}
when performing the stable matching with
x_{i},x_{j}
and
y_{i},y_{j}
only. This decomposition of the geometric constraint of the matching
\sigma_{N}
into the pair conditions
\mathcal{A}_{ij}
is crucial.

We now define the notion of
2p
-poles or multipoles which are groups of dipoles. Two dipoles belong to the same multipole if the distance between these two sets of points is, roughly speaking, of the same order as the smallest dipole size. This construction involves an arbitrary choice of the large parameter
M
.

Definition 2.2

(Multipoles)
.

Let
\sigma_{N}
be the stable matching introduced in Definition
2.1
. Let
M>10
be a fixed number. For each
i=1,\ldots,N
, denote
r_{i}\coloneqq|x_{i}-y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}|
.

(1)

For every
i,j\in[N]
with
i\neq j
, define the event

(2.11)

\mathcal{B}_{ij}\coloneqq\{i\leftrightarrow j\},

where
i\leftrightarrow j
if

\mathrm{dist}(\{x_{i},y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}\},\{x_{j},y_{\sigma_{N}(j)}\})\leq M\min(\max(r_{i},\lambda),\max(r_{j},\lambda)).

(2)

The relation
\leftrightarrow
defines an undirected graph on
[N]
.
The connected components of that graph are called multipoles. Those of size
k
are called
2k
-poles or multipoles of size
k
(or pure dipoles for
k=1
).

(3)

Let
S
be a subset of
[N]
. Define the event

(2.12)

\mathcal{B}_{S}\coloneqq\{S\text{ is connected in }([N],\leftrightarrow)\},

which means that the elements of
S
are in the same multipole.

Note that to have a convergent cluster expansion, we will take
M
large enough with respect to
\beta
.

Figure 2
.
Three multipoles:
\{1,2\}
,
\{4\}
,
\{3,5\}

These definitions are motivated by the fact that the free energy of a
k
-pole is a true
2k
-body integral and cannot be Taylor-expanded. Note that a large dipole (say of size
\gg 1
) which is not a multipole is still considered in a “dipole state” exactly like a small dipole that is well-separated from other dipoles. Such a large dipole may be surrounded by small dipoles that are very close to it, but that is still not considered a multipole.

2.3.
Starting point of the method

Let us give a simplified sketch of the strategy. Details will be given over the course of the paper.

2.3.1.
Cluster expansion

We begin by introducing a dipole expansion (which we in fact do not perform), which will allow us to introduce the main quantities.

The starting point is to separate out the leading
part of the interactions, which is the interaction within dipoles, and consider the other pair interactions, which are the dipolar interactions (and are weaker), as small perturbations by writing:

(2.13)

Z_{N,\beta}^{\lambda}=\int_{\Lambda^{2N}}e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}}=N!\int_{\Lambda^{2N}}e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}}\mathbf{1}_{\sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Id}}=N!\int_{\Lambda^{2N}}\prod_{i<j}e^{-\beta v_{ij}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{ij}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}e^{\beta\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-y_{i})}\mathrm{d}x_{i}\mathrm{d}y_{i},

where
v_{ij}
is the interaction between the dipole
i
and the dipole
j
and
\mathcal{A}_{ij}
are the sets appearing in (
2.10
).

The principle of a cluster expansion is then to write

(2.14)

e^{-\beta v_{ij}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{ij}}=1+f_{ij}^{v}

where
f_{ij}^{v}
are the so-called Mayer bonds,
and expand the product over
i,j\in[N]
,
i<j
. This yields

(2.15)

Z_{N,\beta}^{\lambda}=N!\sum_{E}\int_{\Lambda^{2N}}\prod_{ij\in E}f_{ij}^{v}\prod_{i=1}^{N}e^{\beta\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-y_{i})}\mathrm{d}x_{i}\mathrm{d}y_{i},

where the sum is over sets of pairs
E
in
[N]
.

Cluster expansions allow us to use combinatorial graph expansions to rewrite the
logarithm
of expressions like (
2.15
) as a sum over subsets of
[N]
, involving certain integral terms called
activities
. We refer for instance to
[
Far10
,
FV18
]
for an introduction. It is important to note that an activity is not an energetic quantity.

A first important observation is that for any connected graph
(V,E)
with
p
vertices,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2p}}\prod_{ij\in E}f_{ij}^{v}\prod_{i\in V}e^{\beta\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-y_{i})}\delta_{0}(x_{1})\mathrm{d}y_{1}\prod_{i=2}^{p}\mathrm{d}x_{i}\mathrm{d}y_{i}\text{ converges}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\beta>\beta_{p}=4-\frac{2}{p}

where
\delta_{0}
is the Dirac measure at
0
, meaning the point
x_{1}
in the integral is frozen. Thus, the clusters (i.e. connected components of the graph
([N],E)
in (
2.15
)) of cardinality strictly larger than
p
will diverge for
\beta\in[\beta_{p},\beta_{p+1})
.

2.3.2.
Critical scale

The expression (
2.15
) can be read as

(2.16)

\frac{Z_{N,\beta}^{\lambda}}{Z^{\mathrm{dip}}}=N!\sum_{E}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{dip}}\left[\prod_{ij\in E}f_{ij}^{v}\right]

where the expectation is taken with respect to the i.i.d. dipole model, i.e. the measure with density proportional to

\prod_{i=1}^{N}e^{\beta\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-y_{i})}\mathrm{d}x_{i}\mathrm{d}y_{i},

where only the interactions between dipoles (as identified by the stable matching) are counted.
We will denote by

(2.17)

\mathcal{Z}_{\beta}\coloneqq 2\pi\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{\beta\mathsf{g}_{1}(r)}r\mathrm{d}r,

the rescaled (limiting) normalization constant for this model.

The cluster expansion corresponding to (
2.16
) leads to a series that is not convergent. To obtain a convergent expansion, we first need to remove the divergent part of the model. Let us see at what scale the i.i.d. dipole approximation breaks. Under the i.i.d. dipole model, the number of dipoles with size
R>\lambda
is typically of order
N(\frac{\lambda}{R})^{\beta-2}.
Therefore, under the i.i.d. dipole measure, the typical distance between dipoles of size
R
is of the order
(\frac{R}{\lambda})^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1}.
Observe that the independent dipole approximation is valid only if the inter-dipole distance is at least of the same order as the dipole length, i.e. if

(2.18)

\left(\frac{R}{\lambda}\right)^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1}\geq R\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad R\leq R_{\beta,\lambda}=\lambda^{-\frac{\beta-2}{4-\beta}}\quad\text{or $\beta\geq 4$}.

Indeed, if the inter-dipole spacing is much smaller than the dipole length, the matching forces an overcrowding of same-signed particles, which is heavily energetically penalized.

2.3.3.
Expansion around the hierarchical model

To have a convergent expansion, we should expand only the interactions that are small enough, i.e. the interactions between dipoles in distinct multipoles. Equivalently, we perform a perturbative expansion around a hierarchical multipole model in which only intra-multipole interactions (as defined in Definition
2.2
) are retained.

For every subpartition
X
of
[N]
, the hierarchical model, denoted by
\mathsf{P}_{\beta,\lambda}^{X}
, is defined by

\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{\beta,\lambda}^{X}\propto\prod_{S\in X}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{S}}\prod_{i,j\in S:i<j}e^{-\beta v_{ij}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{ij}}\prod_{i\in S}e^{\beta\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-y_{i})}\mathrm{d}x_{i}\mathrm{d}y_{i},

which can be compared to (
2.13
).

Denote by
\Pi^{\mathrm{mult}}
the (random) partition into multipoles and let
\pi
be a partition of
[N]
. Expanding around the hierarchical model by using (
2.14
) allows one to expand
\mathbb{P}_{N,\beta}^{\lambda}(\sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Id},\Pi^{\mathrm{mult}}=\pi)
as the multipole partition function multiplied by the perturbative term

(2.19)

\sum_{E\subset\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(\pi)}\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{P}_{\pi}^{\beta,\lambda}}\left[\prod_{ij\in E}(e^{-\beta v_{ij}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}}-1)\right],

where
\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(\pi)
represents the set of edges joining pairs of dipoles in
distinct
multipoles, defined by

(2.20)

\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(\pi)\coloneqq\bigcup_{S\neq S^{\prime}\in\pi}\bigcup_{i\in S,j\in S^{\prime}}\{ij\}.

Let us also introduce
\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{intra}}(\pi)
to be the set of edges between dipoles in the same multipole:

\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{intra}}(\pi)\coloneqq\bigcup_{S\in\pi}\bigcup_{i,j\in S:i\neq j}\{ij\}.

Following the cluster expansion roadmap, we resum (
2.19
) over the connected components of the augmented graph

(2.21)

([N],E\cup\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{intra}}(\pi)).

Let
X
be a subpartition of
[N]
. We will denote

(2.22)

V_{X}\coloneqq\bigcup_{S\in X}S,

and will say that a set of edges
E\subset\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(X)
is connected relative to
X
and write
E\in\mathsf{E}^{X}
if the augmented graph
(V_{X},E\cup\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{intra}}(X))
is connected.

This allows one to rewrite (
2.19
) as

(2.23)

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\pi)\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}K(X_{1})\cdots K(X_{n}),

where
K(X)
is the
activity
of the subpartition
X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi)
defined by

(2.24)

K(X)=\sum_{E\in\mathsf{E}^{X}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{P}_{\pi}^{\beta,\lambda}}\left[\prod_{ij\in E}(e^{-\beta v_{ij}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}}-1)\right].

One of the main advantages of such a representation is that,
if the series is absolutely convergent
, one has a formula for the logarithm of (
2.23
) as another series: by a simple combinatorial argument, as formal series,

(2.25)

\log\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\pi)\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}K(X_{1})\dots K(X_{n})=\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\pi)\\
\text{ connected}\end{subarray}}K(X_{1})\cdots K(X_{n})\mathrm{I}(G(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})),

where
G(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
denotes the connection graph of
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
(the graph on
[n]
with an edge
ij
if
X_{i}
and
X_{j}
intersect) and where
\mathrm{I}
is the Ursell function whose definition we now recall.

Definition 2.3
.

The Ursell function of a connected graph
G
is defined by

(2.26)

\mathrm{I}(G)\coloneqq\sum_{H\subset G}(-1)^{|E(H)|},

where the sum over
H
runs over all spanning subgraphs of
G
(
H
connected and with edges included in those of
G
) and where
|E(H)|
is the number of edges of
H
.

Note that we will in fact expand the restricted partition function in a different way, which will eventually give the same activity by Möbius inversion. The main challenge is to establish the absolute convergence of the series (
2.25
). Because of the long-range nature of the interaction, the usual cluster‑expansion criteria do
not apply in their standard form.

2.4.
Statement of the main results

We begin by defining the dipole activity for families of
k
dipoles in
\Lambda
.

Definition 2.4

(Dipole activity)
.

We define the dipole measure on
\Lambda^{2}
as given by

(2.27)

\mathrm{d}\mu_{\beta,\lambda}(x,y)=\frac{1}{NC_{\beta,\lambda}}e^{\beta\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x-y)}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y,

where

(2.28)

C_{\beta,\lambda}=\frac{1}{N}\int_{\Lambda^{2}}e^{\beta\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x-y)}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

For every
V\subset[N]
, we let
\mathcal{G}_{c}(V)
be the set of edges
E
on
V
such that
(V,E)
is connected. Moreover, we let

\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{dip}}(V)=\sum_{E\in\mathcal{G}_{c}(V)}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\beta,\lambda}^{\otimes|V|}}\left[\prod_{ij\in E}(e^{-\beta v_{ij}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{ij}}-1)\right].

Notice that
\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{dip}}
still depends on
N
, and that for
|V|\in\{0,1\}
, we have
\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{dip}}(V)=0
.

Since
\beta>2
, the integrand is integrable and

(2.29)

\lim_{N\to\infty}C_{\beta,\lambda}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{\beta\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x)}\mathrm{d}x=\lambda^{2-\beta}\mathcal{Z}_{\beta},

where
\mathcal{Z}_{\beta}
is as in (
2.17
).

Next, we introduce the limiting activity associated with
\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{dip}}
. For
\beta>2
, set

(2.30)

p^{*}(\beta)\coloneqq\max\{q\geq 2:\ \beta>\beta_{q}\}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\cup\{+\infty\}\cup\{-\infty\}.

Note that
p^{*}(\beta)=-\infty
for
\beta\in(2,3]
and
p^{*}(\beta)=+\infty
for
\beta\geq 4
. We will show that whenever
|V|=k\in\{2,\ldots,p^{*}(\beta)\}
, the limit

(2.31)

\lim_{N\to\infty}N^{k-1}\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{dip}}(V)\eqqcolon\mathsf{k}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{dip}}(k)

exists. For
k\in\{0,1\}
, we set
\mathsf{k}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{dip}}(k)=0
.

We next introduce the truncated Mayer series that arises in (
1.5
).

Definition 2.5

(Hypertrees)
.

For every set
A
, define the set of hypertrees
\mathrm{Htrees}_{n}(A)
on
A
with
n
parts to be

(2.32)

\mathrm{Htrees}_{n}(A)\coloneqq\\
\Bigl\{(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})\in A^{n}\;\Bigm|\;G(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})\text{ is a tree and }|X_{i}\cap X_{j}|=1\ \ \forall\,ij\in E\bigl(G(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})\bigr)\Bigr\},

where
G(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
is the connection graph of
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
.

Definition 2.6

(Truncated Mayer series)
.

Let
\beta\in(2,\infty)
. Let
p\leq p^{*}(\beta)
with
p^{*}(\beta)
as in (
2.30
).

Define the Mayer series truncated at
p
to be

(2.33)

\mathrm{f}_{\beta,p,\lambda}\coloneqq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n!}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(V_{1},\ldots,V_{n})\in\mathrm{Htrees}_{n}([k]):\\
V_{1}\cup\cdots\cup V_{n}=[k]\end{subarray}}\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{dip}}_{\beta,\lambda}(|V_{1}|)\mathbf{1}_{|V_{1}|\leq p}\cdots\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{dip}}_{\beta,\lambda}(|V_{n}|)\mathbf{1}_{|V_{n}|\leq p}.

Note that when
\beta\in(2,3]
,
\mathrm{f}_{\beta,p,\lambda}=0
.

When
\beta\in(2,4)
, the Mayer series will be truncated at
p=p^{*}(\beta)
, while for
\beta\geq 4
at an arbitrary number that will be fixed throughout the paper.

Definition 2.7

(Series truncation parameter)
.

Fix
p_{0}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
for the rest of the paper. Set

p(\beta)=\begin{cases}p^{*}(\beta)&\text{if $\beta\in(2,4)$}\\
p_{0}&\text{if $\beta\geq 4$}.\end{cases}

We next quantify the remainder in (
1.5
), which corresponds to the contribution of the non-dilute subsystem for
\beta\in(2,4)
and to large diagrams for
\beta\geq 4
in the expansion (
1.5
).

Definition 2.8

(
\lambda
-optimal error rate)
.

We let

(2.34)

\delta_{\beta,\lambda}\coloneqq R_{\beta,\lambda}^{-2}\mathbf{1}_{\beta\in(2,4)}\left(1+\sum_{p=2}^{\infty}|\log\lambda|\mathbf{1}_{\beta=\beta_{p}}\right)+\lambda^{2p_{0}}\mathbf{1}_{\beta\geq 4}.

Note that
\delta_{\beta,\lambda}\to 0
as
\lambda\to 0
.

The following result decomposes the free energy of the Coulomb gas as the sum of the free energy of
N
i.i.d. dipoles, the truncated Mayer series of non-divergent clusters of Definition
2.6
and the optimal error rate from Definition
2.8
.

Theorem 1

(Free energy expansion)
.

Let
\beta\in(2,\infty)
and let
p(\beta)
be as in Definition
2.7
. Let
\mathcal{Z}_{\beta}
be as in (
2.17
) and
\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{dip}}
be as in Definition
2.4
. Recall the Ursell function
\mathrm{I}
from Definition
2.3
.

(1)

We have

(2.35)

\log Z_{N,\beta}^{\lambda}=2N\log N+N((2-\beta)\log\lambda+\log\mathcal{Z}_{\beta}-1)\\
+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}V_{1},\ldots,V_{n}\subset[N]\\
\mathrm{disjoint},\forall i,|V_{i}|\leq p(\beta)\end{subarray}}\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{dip}}(V_{1})\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{dip}}(V_{n})\mathrm{I}(G(V_{1},\ldots,V_{n}))+O_{\beta,p(\beta)}(N\delta_{\beta,\lambda}).

(2)

The limit
\mathsf{k}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{dip}}(k)
in (
2.31
) exists and is finite if
k\leq p^{*}(\beta)
, i.e. if
\beta>\beta_{k}
. Moreover, for every
k\leq p^{*}(\beta)
, there exists a constant
C>0
depending on
\beta
and
k
such that

(2.36)

|\mathsf{k}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{dip}}(k)|\leq C\lambda^{2(k-1)}.

(3)

Recalling
\mathrm{f}_{\beta,p,\lambda}
from Definition
2.6
, we have

(2.37)

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}V_{1},\ldots,V_{n}\subset[N]\\
\mathrm{disjoint},\forall i,|V_{i}|\leq p(\beta)\end{subarray}}\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{dip}}(V_{1})\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{dip}}(V_{n})\mathrm{I}(G(V_{1},\ldots,V_{n}))=-N\mathrm{f}_{\beta,p(\beta),\lambda}+O_{\beta,p(\beta)}(N\delta_{\beta,\lambda}).

Remark 2.9

(Link with the multipole transitions)
.

For
\beta>\beta_{p}
, the smallest contribution to (
2.33
) comes from clusters of cardinality
p
and scales like
\lambda^{2(p-1)}
, while

R_{\beta,\lambda}^{-2}<\lambda^{-\frac{\beta_{p}-2}{4-\beta_{p}}}=\lambda^{2(p-1)},

with equality for
\beta=\beta_{p}
.
In particular, for
\beta<\beta_{p}
the non-dilute term dominates over the
p
-cluster term, whereas for
\beta>\beta_{p}
the
p
-cluster contribution becomes the larger of the two. Equivalently, this critical point marks the change from divergence to convergence at infinity of the activity of
p
-clusters: they diverge when
\beta<\beta_{p}
but converge when
\beta>\beta_{p}
. Note that here
p
-clusters are not the same as
2p
-poles.

Remark 2.10

(On the optimality of the error rate)
.

Recall from (
2.18
) that
R_{\beta,\lambda}
is the maximal scale
R
such that dipoles of size
R
are dilute.
Hence, we expect there are about
NR_{\beta,\lambda}^{-2}
dipoles of size
R_{\beta,\lambda}
. The free energy of that subsystem should then be proportional to the number of points as in the regime
\beta\in(0,2)
in
[
LSZ17
]
, hence the error that they contribute should be in
NR_{\beta,\lambda}^{-2}
, giving us that this is a sharp error rate when
\beta\in(2,4)
. In contrast, when
\beta\geq 4
, the error rate we obtain is not sharp. An important remaining question is the dependence in
p_{0}
of the error term, equivalent to the convergence of the cluster expansion series in that regime.

We now state our large deviation result for the number of
2k
-poles. Let us recall the hierarchical multipole model.

Definition 2.11

(Hierarchical multipole model and multipole partition function)
.

For every
S\subset[N]
, define

(2.38)

\mathsf{M}_{\beta,\lambda}(S)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\beta,\lambda}^{\otimes|S|}}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{S}}\prod_{i,j\in S:i<j}e^{-\beta v_{ij}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{ij}}\right].

We also define the probability measure
\mathsf{P}_{\beta,\lambda}^{S}
on
\Lambda^{2|S|}
by

(2.39)

\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{\beta,\lambda}^{S}\coloneqq\frac{1}{\mathsf{M}_{\beta,\lambda}(S)}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{S}}\prod_{i,j\in S:i<j}e^{-\beta v_{ij}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{ij}}\prod_{i\in S}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\beta,\lambda}(x_{i},y_{i}).

When
X
is a subpartition of
[N]
, we define

\mathsf{P}_{\beta,\lambda}^{X}=\bigotimes_{S\in X}\mathsf{P}_{\beta,\lambda}^{S}

to be the hierarchical multipole model on
X
.

Definition 2.12

(Multipole activity)
.

For any

X
subpartition of
[N]
, we denote

(2.40)

V_{X}\coloneqq\bigcup_{S\in X}S.

Recall (
2.20
) and
\mathsf{E}^{X}
as the set of
E\subset\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(X)
such that
(V_{X},E\cup\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{intra}}(X))
is connected. We define

(2.41)

\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X)\coloneqq\sum_{E\in\mathsf{E}^{X}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{P}_{\beta,\lambda}^{X}}\left[\prod_{ij\in E}(e^{-\beta v_{ij}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}}-1)\right].

Notice that for
|X|\in\{0,1\}
, we have
\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X)=0
.

We will show that for
|S|\leq p^{*}(\beta)
, there exists
\mathsf{m}_{\beta,\lambda}(|S|)
such that

(2.42)

\lim_{N\to\infty}N^{|S|-1}\mathsf{M}_{\beta,\lambda}(S)=\mathsf{m}_{\beta,\lambda}(|S|).

Notice that for
|S|=1
, we have
\mathsf{m}_{\beta,\lambda}(|S|)=1
. Moreover, for every subpartition
X
of
[N]
such that
k\coloneqq\sum_{S\in X}|S|\leq p^{*}(\beta)
, we will show that there exists
\mathsf{k}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}
such that

(2.43)

\lim_{N\to\infty}N^{|X|-1}\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X)=\mathsf{k}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(n_{1},\ldots,n_{p}),

where
n_{i}
stands for the number of elements of
X
of cardinality
i
. Notice that for
|X|\in\{0,1\}
with
n_{i}
elements of cardinality
i
for every
i\in[p]
, we have
\mathsf{k}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(n_{1},\ldots,n_{p})=0
.

We now define the multipole rate function.

Definition 2.13

(Canonical partition with given block profile)
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{N}
and
m_{1},\ldots,m_{p}\in\mathbb{N}
, and set
k=m_{1}+2m_{2}+\cdots+pm_{p}
. For any set partition
Y
, define

\#_{i}(Y)\coloneqq\bigl|\{S\in Y:|S|=i\}\bigr|

for the number of blocks of size
i
in
Y
.

We define
Y(m_{1},\ldots,m_{p})
to be the partition of
[k]
obtained by taking the ordered list

1,2,\ldots,k
and cutting it, in order, into blocks as follows: first form
m_{1}
singletons from
the first
m_{1}
elements; next form
m_{2}
consecutive pairs from the following
2m_{2}
elements; and
continue in this way until
m_{p}
consecutive
p
-tuples have been formed from the last
pm_{p}

elements. By construction,

\#_{i}\bigl(Y(m_{1},\ldots,m_{p})\bigr)=m_{i}\quad\text{for every }i=1,\ldots,p,\quad\#_{i}\bigl(Y(m_{1},\ldots,m_{p})\bigr)=0\quad\text{for every }i>p.

Definition 2.14

(Multipole rate function)
.

Let
\beta\in(2,\infty)
and
p\leq p^{*}(\beta)
. Recall
Y(m_{1},\ldots,m_{p})
from Definition
2.13
.

For every
x\in[0,1]
, let
\triangle_{p,x}\coloneqq\{(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{p})\in[0,1]^{p}:\gamma_{1}+2\gamma_{2}+\cdots+p\gamma_{p}=1-x\}
. Define

\mathcal{I}_{\beta,p,\lambda}:(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{p})\in\cup_{x\in[0,1]}\triangle_{p,x}\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{p}\gamma_{i}(\log i!-1+\log\gamma_{i}-\log\mathsf{m}_{\beta,\lambda}(i))+1\\
+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n!}\sum_{m_{1},\ldots,m_{p}\in\mathbb{N}}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\frac{\gamma_{i}^{m_{i}}}{m_{i}!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\\
\in\mathrm{Htrees}_{n}(Y(m_{1},\ldots,m_{p}))\\
X_{1}\cup\cdots\cup X_{n}=Y(m_{1},\ldots,m_{p})\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\mathsf{k}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}((\#_{k}X_{i})_{k})\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{i}}|\leq p}.

The rate function
\mathcal{I}_{\beta,p,\lambda}
includes an entropy term, which counts the number of partitions, the free energy of the hierarchical model, and the correction to the hierarchical model (Mayer series).

We can now state our large deviation result.

Theorem 2

(Multipole distribution)
.

Let
\beta\in(2,\infty)
and let
p(\beta)
be as in Definition
2.7
. For every
k\geq 1
, denote by
\mathcal{N}_{k}
the number of multipoles (as defined in Definition
2.2
) of cardinality
k
. Then, for
M
large enough with respect to
\beta
and
p(\beta)
, the following hold:

(1)

(Coefficients of the rate function)
The limits (
2.42
) and (
2.43
) exist and are finite. Moreover, for every
k\in\{2,\ldots,p(\beta)\}
, there exists
C>0
depending on
\beta,k
and
M
such that

(2.44)

\frac{1}{C}\lambda^{2(k-1)}\leq\mathsf{m}_{\beta,\lambda}(k)\leq C\lambda^{2(k-1)}.

For every subpartition
X
of
\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that
|V_{X}|\leq p(\beta)
, there exists
C>0
depending on
\beta,k
and
M
such that

(2.45)

|\mathsf{k}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(\{\#_{m}X_{i}\}_{m})|\leq C\lambda^{2(|X|-1)}.

(2)

(Free energy expansion in terms of the rate function)

We have

(2.46)

\log Z_{N,\beta}^{\lambda}=2N\log N+N\left((2-\beta)\log\lambda+\log\mathcal{Z}_{\beta}-1\right)-N\inf_{\triangle_{p,0}}\mathcal{I}_{\beta,p,\lambda}+O_{\beta,p(\beta),M}(N\delta_{\beta,\lambda}).

(3)

(Control of large multipoles and large dipoles)
For every
n\geq 1
,

(2.47)

\log\mathbb{P}_{N,\beta}^{\lambda}\left(|\mathcal{N}_{1}+2\mathcal{N}_{2}+\cdots+p(\beta)\mathcal{N}_{p(\beta)}-N|\geq n\right)\leq-\tfrac{1}{2}n+O_{\beta,p(\beta),M}(N\delta_{\beta,\lambda}).

Moreover, for every
n\geq 1
,

(2.48)

\log\mathbb{P}_{N,\beta}^{\lambda}\left(\left|\left\{i\in[N]:|x_{i}-y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}|\geq R_{\beta,\lambda}\right\}\right|\geq n\right)\leq-\tfrac{1}{2}n+O_{\beta,p(\beta),M}(N\delta_{\beta,\lambda}).

(4)

(Large deviations for multipole count)
Let
\gamma=(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{p(\beta)})\in\triangle_{p(\beta),0}
and suppose that there exists
C_{0}>0
such that for every
k\in\{2,\ldots,p(\beta)\}
,

\gamma_{k}\leq C_{0}\,\lambda^{2(k-1)}.

Then,

(2.49)

\log\mathbb{P}_{N,\beta}^{\lambda}\left(\max_{i\in[p(\beta)]}|\mathcal{N}_{i}-N\gamma_{i}|\leq C_{0}N\delta_{\beta,\lambda}\right)\\
=-N\left(\mathcal{I}_{\beta,p(\beta),\lambda}(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{p(\beta)})-\inf_{\triangle_{p(\beta),0}}\mathcal{I}_{\beta,p(\beta),\lambda}\right)+O_{\beta,p(\beta),M,C_{0}}(N\delta_{\beta,\lambda}).

(5)

(Minimizer of the rate function)
The infimum of
\mathcal{I}_{\beta,p(\beta),\lambda}|_{\triangle_{p,0}}
is attained at a unique
\gamma^{*}\in\triangle_{p(\beta),0}
and for every
k\in\{2,\ldots,p(\beta)\}
, there exists a constant
C>0
depending on
\beta
,
M
and
k
such that

(2.50)

\frac{1}{C}\lambda^{2(k-1)}\leq\gamma_{k}^{*}\leq C\lambda^{2(k-1)}.

(6)

(Typical number of multipoles)
Let
k\in\{2,\ldots,p(\beta)\}
. There exists
C>0
depending on
\beta,p(\beta)
and
M
such that for
C_{0}
large enough,

(2.51)

\log\mathbb{P}_{N,\beta}^{\lambda}\left(\mathcal{N}_{k}\notin\Bigr(\frac{1}{C_{0}}N\lambda^{2(k-1)},C_{0}N\lambda^{2(k-1)}\Bigr)\right)\leq-CN\lambda^{2(k-1)}+O_{\beta,p(\beta),M}(N\delta_{\beta,\lambda}).

Remark 2.15

(On the large deviations assumption)
.

In the grand canonical setting, an expansion around the i.i.d. dipole model of the partition function conditional on the multipoles fractions
would yield a simpler rate function and make the assumption
\gamma_{k}\leq C_{0}\,\lambda^{2(k-1)}
in (4) unnecessary.

Remark 2.16

(On the number of multipoles of given size and cardinality)
.

We believe that with our method one could easily prove the following. Let
\beta\in(2,\infty)
and
k\in\{2,\ldots,p(\beta)\}
. For every
R>0
, denote by
\mathcal{N}_{k,R}
the number of multipoles of cardinality
k
with the largest dipole of size between
R
and
2R
and set
n_{k,R}\coloneqq NR^{2(k-1)}(\frac{\lambda}{R})^{k(\beta-2)}
. Then, (we expect that) there exists
C>0
depending on
\beta,p(\beta)
and
M
such that for
C_{0}
large enough,

\log\mathbb{P}_{N,\beta}^{\lambda}\Bigr(\mathcal{N}_{k,R}\notin\Bigr(\frac{1}{C_{0}}n_{k,R},C_{0}n_{k,R}\Bigr)\Bigr)\leq-Cn_{k,R}+O_{\beta,p(\beta),M}(N\delta_{\beta,\lambda}).

Notice that since
k\leq p(\beta)\leq p^{*}(\beta)
, the quantity
n_{k,R}
is decreasing in
R
. This is at the heart of the multipole transition: conditionally on seeing a multipole of cardinality
k
, one should bet that it is of small size whenever
k\leq p^{*}(\beta)
and of large size whenever
k>p^{*}(\beta)
.

2.5.
More details about the proof and plan of the paper

We assume here that we have restricted to the event where the stable matching introduced in
2.2
is given by
\sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Id}
.

2.5.1.
Truncation scale

In order to get a convergent Mayer series, we need to restrict the cluster expansion to dipoles of length smaller than the critical scale. However for
\beta\geq 4
, this critical scale is infinite; hence we define the following truncation scale:

Definition 2.17
.

We let

\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}\coloneqq\begin{cases}R_{\beta,\lambda}&\text{if $\beta\in(2,4)$}\\
\lambda^{-2p_{0}}&\text{if $\beta\geq 4$},\end{cases}

where
R_{\beta,\lambda}
is as in (
1.4
).

We will restrict the cluster expansion to dipoles of length smaller than
\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}
where
\varepsilon_{0}
is a small number.

The next task is to approximate the dipole-dipole interaction
v_{ij}
(and hence
f_{ij}^{v}
) by two
short-range
interactions, one denoted by
\tilde{v}_{ij}
which corresponds to a bound from above on the energy, and one denoted by
v_{ij}^{\prime}
which corresponds to a bound from below. These approximations will have ranges smaller than
\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}
and will yield errors of size
N\delta_{\beta,\lambda}
(see Definition
2.8
), which rely on new electrostatic estimates that are given in Section
3
. These two approximate models allow us to discard delicate interactions, corresponding to dipoles at large distances from each other or to highly packed dipoles, while still providing a good approximation of the true interaction
v_{ij}
.

2.5.2.
Electric representation and electrostatic estimates

Our electrostatic estimates rely on the electric formulation of the energy, as developed for the one-component Coulomb gas in the second author’s prior works
[
SS15
,
RS15
,
Ser24
]
and in the two-component case in
[
LSZ17
,
BS24
]
. They provide effective upper and lower bounds
for the energy based on a method of increasing the radii of the smearing balls for each charge.

First, the
electric potential

h_{\lambda}
generated by the configuration
(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})
is defined as a function
over all

\mathbb{R}^{2}
by

(2.52)

h_{\lambda}[\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N}]\coloneqq\mathsf{g}*\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\lambda)}-\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\lambda)}\right),

where
*
denotes the convolution.
In the sequel, we will most often drop the
[\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N}]
dependence in the notation.

Note that by definition of
\mathsf{g}
,
h_{\lambda}
satisfies the Poisson equation

(2.53)

-\Delta h_{\lambda}[\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N}]=2\pi\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\lambda)}-\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\lambda)}\right).

A direct insertion into (
2.5
) and integration by parts using (
2.53
) yield the following rewriting of the energy

(2.54)

\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\nabla h_{\lambda}[\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N}]\right|^{2}-N(\mathsf{g}(\lambda)+\kappa).

Our electrostatic estimates rely on the ball-growth method,
see
[
Ser24
, Chap. 4]
and references therein,
which consists in expanding the spherical charges
\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\lambda)},\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\lambda)}
into charges
\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\tau_{i})},\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\tau_{i})}
of the same mass but supported in the discs
B(x_{i},\tau_{i})
,
B(y_{i},\tau_{i})
. By Newton’s theorem, whenever
B(x_{i},\tau_{i})
and
B(x_{j},\tau_{j})
are
disjoint
(resp. with
x_{i}
replaced by
y_{i}
or
x_{j}
replaced by
y_{j}
), then the interaction between the charge distributions
\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\tau_{i})}
and
\delta_{x_{j}}^{(\tau_{j})}
(and resp.) is equal to the interaction between
\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\lambda)}
and
\delta_{x_{j}}^{(\lambda)}
(and resp.). Moreover, if they are not disjoint, a trivial monotonicity allows us to bound from below
\int|\nabla h_{\lambda}|^{2}
by
the difference between the energies before ball growth and after ball growth, which, by Newton’s theorem
only counts interactions between non-disjoint larger balls
.
This way, we obtain a lower bound for the energy of the form

(2.55)

\begin{split}\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})\geq-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-y_{i})+\sum_{i<j}v_{ij}^{\prime}+\text{Error},\end{split}

where
v_{ij}^{\prime}
is a new
short range interaction
between
\{x_{i},y_{i}\}
and
\{x_{j},y_{j}\}
, which vanishes if the balls of radius
\tau_{i}
and
\tau_{j}

centered at dipoles
i
and
j
do not intersect.

The delicate task done in Section
5.2
is to choose the
\tau_{i}
’s appropriately. These radii should be small enough to suppress diverging interactions (from long dipoles or highly overcrowded multipoles) but large enough so that
\tilde{v}_{ij}
remains a good approximation of
v_{ij}
.

To bound the partition function from below, we restrict the phase space to the event where all dipoles are small. To each couple
(x_{i},y_{i})
we associate a
screened dipole electric field
obtained by solving

(2.56)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\Delta u_{i}=2\pi(\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\lambda)}-\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\lambda)})&\text{in}\ B(z_{i},\tau_{i}),\\
\frac{\partial u_{i}}{\partial\nu}=0&\text{on}\ \partial B(z_{i},\tau_{i}),\end{array}\right.

where
\nu
denotes the outer unit normal,
z_{i}
is the barycenter of
\{x_{i},y_{i}\}
and
\tau_{i}
’s are this time chosen equal to
\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}
, with
\varepsilon_{0}
a small parameter and
\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}
defined as follows.

Adding the vector fields
\nabla u_{i}
generated by all dipoles, one obtains a vector field
E=\sum_{i}\nabla u_{i}\mathbf{1}_{B(z_{i},\tau_{i})}
, which is not necessarily a gradient, but which, thanks to the vanishing Neumann boundary condition, satisfies

(2.57)

-\mathrm{div}\,E=2\pi\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\lambda)}-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\lambda)}\right)\quad\text{in }\ \mathbb{R}^{2}.

Using Green’s theorem (which reveals the
L^{2}
-minimality of gradients), we may then obtain an energy upper bound

(2.58)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla h_{\lambda}|^{2}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|E|^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\nabla u_{i})\mathbf{1}_{B(z_{i},\tau_{i})}\right|^{2}.

Expanding the square in the right-hand side gives pair-interactions which
only concern pairs of dipoles for which the balls
B(z_{i},\tau_{i})
and
B(z_{i},\tau_{j})
intersect
.
After some PDE estimates, we are led to an upper bound of the form

(2.59)

\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})\leq-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-y_{i})+\sum_{i<j}\tilde{v}_{ij}+\text{Error},

where
\tilde{v}_{ij}
represents the new interaction between the screened dipoles, which vanishes at distances larger than
2\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}
.

We will work with these two reduced models, the one with dipole-interactions
v_{ij}^{\prime}
as in (
2.55
), and the one with dipole interactions
\tilde{v}_{ij}
as in (
2.59
), and perform cluster expansions on each of them.

2.5.3.
Cancellation effects and integration

The dipole-dipole interaction
v_{ij}
between two dipoles of vectors
\vec{r}_{i}=x_{i}-y_{i}
and
\vec{r}_{j}=x_{j}-y_{\sigma_{N}(j)}
is well known to be, at leading order

(2.60)

v_{ij}\approx\frac{\vec{r}_{i}\cdot\vec{r}_{j}}{d_{ij}^{2}}-2\frac{(\vec{r}_{i}\cdot\vec{d_{ij}})(\vec{r}_{j}\cdot\vec{d_{ij}})}{d_{ij}^{4}}

where
\vec{d}_{ij}
is the vector joining the two dipoles and
d_{ij}
its norm, when
d_{ij}\geq\max(r_{i},r_{j})
. In general, one has the crude bound

(2.61)

|v_{ij}|\lesssim\frac{r_{i}r_{j}}{d_{ij}\max(d_{ij},r_{i},r_{j})},

which is long-range in 2D since the spatial integral of
d_{ij}^{-2}
diverges.

Applied to the Mayer bond
f_{ij}^{v},
this crude control produces divergent contributions in the series (
2.19
) when
\beta>3
. To obtain a convergent expansion, we must exploit the angular cancellation. We split
f_{ij}^{v}
into its odd part (essentially
v_{ij}
) and its even correction. A standard parity argument shows that products of odd bonds integrate to zero unless every vertex has an even number of incident odd bonds – that is, only Eulerian graphs contribute after integration. We therefore reduce to analyzing Eulerian subgraphs and the contributions obtained by combining the crude bound along their edges.

The key step is that, after integrating over inter-dipole positions/angles, cluster integrals associated with an Eulerian graph collapse to a quadratic dependence in the dipole lengths:

(2.62)

\int r_{2}^{2}r_{3}^{2}\dots r_{k}^{2}\prod_{i=1}^{N}e^{\beta\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(r_{i})}\mathbf{1}_{r_{i}\leq\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\mathrm{d}r_{i},

where
r_{i}=|\vec{r}_{i}|
is the length of the dipole.

For intuition, consider the triangle
(1,2,3)
and assume for simplicity that
d_{ij}\geq\max(r_{i},r_{j})
for each edge. If
r_{3}=\max(r_{1},r_{2},r_{3})
and
d_{13}=\max(d_{12},d_{23},d_{13})
, then

\prod_{i=1}^{3}\frac{r_{i}r_{j}}{\max(d_{ij},r_{i},r_{j})}=r_{1}^{2}r_{2}^{2}\cdot d_{12}^{-2}d_{23}^{-2}\cdot\Bigr(\frac{r_{3}^{2}}{d_{13}^{2}}\Bigr)\leq r_{1}^{2}r_{2}^{2}\cdot d_{12}^{-2}d_{23}^{-2}\cdot\Bigr(\frac{r_{3}^{2}}{\max(d_{12},d_{23}}\Bigr).

Integrating over
d_{12}
and
d_{23}
yields the desired quadratic factor
r_{1}^{2}r_{2}^{2}
. Notice that we have opened the triangle by taking off the variable
d_{13}
. For a general Eulerian graph, we extract a minimally 2-edge-connected spanning subgraph and perform a strict ear decomposition; opening each ear repeats the triangle mechanism and produces the same quadratic structure.

We then integrate the radii in (
2.62
): take
r_{2},\dots,r_{k}
from
0
up to
r_{1}

and use the approximation
e^{\beta\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(r)}\approx r^{-\beta}
on
[\lambda,\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}]
.
This leads to the one-dimensional integral

\int\max(r_{1},\lambda)^{(4-\beta)(k-1)}\max(r_{1},\lambda)^{1-\beta}\mathrm{d}r_{1},

which converges if and only if

(4-\beta)(k-1)+1-\beta>-1\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad k>\frac{2}{4-\beta}\Longleftrightarrow\quad\beta>\beta_{k}.

This is exactly the transition at
\beta_{p}
. For
\beta\in(\beta_{p},\beta_{p+1})
, all clusters with
k
dipoles with
k\leq p
are integrable.

Finally, the even part of the interaction is controlled via a Penrose resummation, reducing it to a convergent tree sum. Summing over partitions of
[N]
with prescribed multipole counts then yields the desired lower bound on the partition function (see Section
4
).

2.5.4.
Extracting a set of good points

The proof of the upper bound of the partition function, occupying Section
5
is significantly more difficult than the lower bound. Indeed, the absolute convergence of the cluster expansion in (
2.25
) crucially relies on the fact that dipoles are of length smaller than
\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}
and multipoles of bounded cardinality. The idea is to extract a set of good points and to perform a cluster expansion on them, where the frozen bad points act as an external force. We then control the number of bad points and show that their contribution to the partition function is negligible.

Two aspects are particularly delicate: (i) the clustering procedure that defines the radii
\tau_{i}
, where we must ensure that interactions among bad points are well controlled and that each bad point has attractive interactions with only a bounded number of other charges per scale; and (ii) the fact that the good points are not an isolated system—interactions with the bad points disrupt the structure underlying the lower bound. We address the second issue in the next section by recovering a quadratic estimate in the presence of bad points.

2.5.5.
Recovering the quadratic estimate

Freezing the bad points breaks the perfect odd–bond cancellations: on the set of good dipoles, the subgraph formed by odd Mayer bonds is not necessarily Eulerian or 2-edge-connected. To repair this, we analyze each connected component of the odd subgraph. It is made of a 2-core consisting of a tree of 2-edge-connected blocks, with pendant trees attached to it. Crucially, each vertex of odd degree in the odd graph interacts with bad points (in particular, the leaves of the pendant trees). We will restore the quadratic estimate (
2.62
) by taking advantage of the interactions with bad points.

With our radii choice and the good-point definition, for any good
i
,

\mathrm{Interaction}(i,\text{bad points})\lesssim\frac{r_{i}}{\mathrm{dist}(\{x_{i},y_{i}\},\text{bad points})}.

Using a key (sub)geometric growth property of
\mathrm{dist}(\{x_{i},y_{i}\},\text{bad points})
along the graph—forced by our choice of enlargement radii—allows us to replace the missing odd edges by these interactions with bad points and thereby recover the quadratic estimate (
2.62
).

2.5.6.
Identifying the infimum of the rate function

Combining the matching large deviation upper and lower bounds identifies the limiting free energy as the infimum of the multipole rate function of Definition
2.14
. The remaining step in Section
5
is to rewrite this infimum as the Mayer series of Theorem
1
. The idea is to use Theorem
2
to reduce to clusters and multipoles of cardinality less than
p(\beta)
and then sum over the multipole partition, in order to approximate
\log Z_{N,\beta}^{\lambda}
by a (convergent) series

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}V_{1},\ldots,V_{n}\subset[N]\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}K(V_{1})\cdots K(V_{n}),

where
K
denotes an intricate activity. Since a cluster expansion of the above form (with a variable total number of points) is essentially unique by Möbius inversion, we can identify this activity
K
with the simple dipole activity of Definition
2.4
(for the relevant clusters). This way, we complete the proof of Theorem
1
.

2.6.
Notation

Since the paper is quite long, we gather here some notation that has already been introduced in the introduction and/or that will be introduced later.

•

Recalling that
N
is a fixed integer, we denote

\Lambda=[0,\sqrt{N}]^{2}.

•

The Euclidian norm in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is denoted by
|\cdot|
.

•

For every set
A
, we denote by
|A|
its cardinality.

•

For every set
A
, we let
\mathcal{P}(A)
be the set of subsets of
A
.

•

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
[n]
the set
\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

•

For a set
V
, we let
\mathcal{G}_{c}(V)
be the set of collections of edges
E
on
V
such that
(V,E)
is connected. We also let
\mathcal{T}_{c}(V)
be the set of collections of edges
E
on
V
such that
(V,E)
is a tree.

•

For a graph
G
, we let
V(G)
be the set of vertices and

be the set of edges. For every
v\in V(G)

\deg_{G}(v)
for the degree of
v
in

(we will sometimes write this with an abuse of notation as
\deg_{E(G)}(v)
).

•

Let
A
be a set and
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subset A
. We denote by
G(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
the connection graph of
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
which is a graph on
[n]
with an edge between
i
and

if
X_{i}
and

intersect.

We denote by
\mathrm{I}
the Ursell function (see Definition
2.3

•

For every set
A
, recall
\mathrm{Htrees}_{n}(A)
from (
2.32
).

Let
A
be an ensemble. The set
\mathbf{\Pi}(A)
will denote the set of partitions of
A
and
\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathrm{sub}}(A)
the set of subpartitions of
[N]
.

•

For every subpartition
X
of
[N]
, let

V_{X}=\bigcup_{S\in X}S.

Also set

\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(X)=\bigcup_{S,S^{\prime}\in X:S\neq S^{\prime}}\bigcup_{i\in S,j\in S^{\prime}}\{ij\}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{intra}}(X)=\bigcup_{S\in X}\bigcup_{i\in S,j\in S:i\neq j}\{ij\}.

•

For every subpartition
X
of
[N]
, we let
\mathsf{E}^{X}
be the set of collections of edges
E\subset\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(X)
such that
(V_{X},E\cup\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{intra}}(X))
is connected.

•

For every subpartition
X
of
[N]
and every integer
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
\#_{k}(X)
be the number of parts of
X
of cardinality
k
.

•

Let
X
be a subpartition of
[N]
and let
E\subset\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(X)
. For every
S\in X
, we denote

\deg_{E}(S)=\sum_{v\in S}\deg_{(V_{X},E)}(v).

•

We denote by
\mathcal{Z}_{\beta}
the integral defined in (
2.17
):

\mathcal{Z}_{\beta}=2\pi\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{\beta\mathsf{g}_{1}(r)}r\mathrm{d}r.

•

We denote by
\sigma_{N}
the stable matching and
\mathcal{A}_{ij}
the event in from (
2.10
). The events
\mathcal{B}_{S}
(the elements of
S
are in the same multipole) and
\mathcal{B}_{ij}
(
i
and
j
are in the same multipole) are as in Definition
2.2
.

•

We denote
\vec{r}_{i}\coloneqq y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}-x_{i}
and
r_{i}=|y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}-x_{i}|
. Moreover for every
S\subset[N]
, we let

r_{S}\coloneqq\max_{i\in S}r_{i}.

•

For every
i,j\in[N]
with
i\neq j
, we will denote

d_{ij}\coloneqq\mathrm{dist}(\{x_{i},y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}\},\{x_{j},y_{\sigma_{N}(j)}\}).

•

Given
i,j\in[N]
with
i\neq j
, denote

v_{ij}\coloneqq\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-x_{j})+\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}-y_{\sigma_{N}(j)})-\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-y_{\sigma_{N}(j)})-\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{j}-y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}).

•

As in (
2.14
), we denote by
f_{ij}^{v}
the Mayer bond

f_{ij}^{v}=e^{-\beta v_{ij}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{ij}}-1.

•

We let
R_{\beta,\lambda}
be the critical scale introduced in (
1.4
), i.e.

R_{\beta,\lambda}=\begin{cases}\lambda^{-\frac{\beta-2}{4-\beta}}&\text{ if $\beta<4$ }\\
+\infty&\text{ if $\beta\geq 4$}\end{cases}

Recall that
p_{0}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
is fixed throughout the paper. Moreover, in Definition
2.17
, we set

\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}\coloneqq\begin{cases}R_{\beta,\lambda}&\text{if $\beta\in(2,4)$}\\
\lambda^{-2p_{0}}&\text{if $\beta\geq 4$}.\end{cases}

•

As in Definition
2.8
, we let

\delta_{\beta,\lambda}\coloneqq R_{\beta,\lambda}^{-2}\mathbf{1}_{\beta\in(2,4)}\left(1+\sum_{p=2}^{\infty}|\log\lambda|\mathbf{1}_{\beta=\beta_{p}}\right)+\lambda^{2p_{0}}\mathbf{1}_{\beta\geq 4}.

Notice that for
\beta\geq 4
,
\delta_{\beta,\lambda}\neq\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}^{-2}
.

•

Recall from (
2.30
)

p^{*}(\beta)\coloneqq\max\{q\geq 2:\ \beta>\beta_{q}\}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\cup\{+\infty\}\cup\{-\infty\}

and from (
2.7
),

p(\beta)=\begin{cases}p^{*}(\beta)&\text{if $\beta\in(2,4)$}\\
p_{0}&\text{if $\beta\geq 4$}.\end{cases}

•

We let

\alpha(\beta)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\frac{4-\beta}{2}&\text{if $\beta\in(2,4)$}\\
\frac{1}{2}&\text{if $\beta\geq 4$}.\end{cases}

•

Recall

C_{\beta,\lambda,\varepsilon_{0}}=\frac{1}{N}\int_{\Lambda^{2}}e^{\beta\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x-y)}\mathbf{1}_{|x-y|\leq\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

and
C_{\beta,\lambda}=C_{\beta,\lambda,\infty}
.

•

We will denote

q(\beta)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\frac{3}{2-\beta/2}-1&\text{if $\beta\in(2,4)$}\\
p_{0}&\text{if $\beta\geq 4$}.\end{cases}

•

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, the quantity
\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,k}
will stand for

\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,k}=\begin{cases}\lambda^{2(k-1)}&\text{if }k\leq p^{*}(\beta),\\[4.0pt]
\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}^{-2}&\begin{aligned} &\text{if }k>p^{*}(\beta)\ \text{and}\ \beta\in\big(\beta_{p^{*}(\beta)},\beta_{p^{*}(\beta)+1}\big)\\
&\text{or }\beta=\beta_{p^{*}(\beta)+1}\ \text{and}\ k>p^{*}(\beta)+1,\end{aligned}\\[8.0pt]
\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}^{-2}\lvert\log\lambda\rvert&\text{if }k=p^{*}(\beta)+1\ \text{and}\ \beta=\beta_{p^{*}(\beta)+1}.\end{cases}

•

Throughout the paper,
M
denotes the constant in Definition
2.2
(multipoles);
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
is a small parameter used to restrict to dipoles of length at most
\varepsilon_{0}\,\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}
and to interactions of range at most
C\varepsilon_{0}\,\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}
; and
p_{0}
denotes the (fixed) truncation parameter of the Mayer series when
\beta\geq 4
.

3.
Electric formulation and energy bounds

After establishing decay estimates for the exact dipole-dipole interaction in Section
3.4
, we control in Section
3.5
the error between the exact interaction and the approximations introduced in the lower and upper bounds.

3.1.
Electric formulation

This formulation is useful both for getting lower bounds and upper bounds on the energy.
Using the temporary shortcut
z_{1},\dots,z_{2N}
for
(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})
,
we are able to rewrite (
2.52
) as

h_{\lambda}=\mathsf{g}*\left(\sum_{i=1}^{2N}d_{i}\delta_{z_{i}}^{(\lambda)}\right)

and thus

(3.1)

-\Delta h_{\lambda}=2\pi\sum_{i=1}^{2N}d_{i}\delta_{z_{i}}^{(\lambda)}.

When increasing the discs we will also denote similarly
for any vector
\vec{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{2N})
in
\mathbb{R}^{2N}

(3.2)

h_{\vec{\alpha}}=\mathsf{g}*\left(\sum_{i=1}^{2N}d_{i}\delta_{z_{i}}^{(\alpha_{i})}\right).

As a preliminary, we will need the following.

Lemma 3.1
.

If
\chi=\delta_{0}^{(1)}
is bounded, the
functions
\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(1)}
and
\mathsf{g}_{1}=\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(1)}*\delta_{0}^{(1)}
have a uniformly bounded second derivative, i.e. are in the Sobolev space
W^{2,\infty}
.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{\mathsf{g}}_{1}\coloneqq\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(1)}
.
It suffices to use that
-\Delta\tilde{\mathsf{g}}_{1}=2\pi\chi
where the right-hand side is bounded and supported in
B(0,1)
and the fact that
\tilde{\mathsf{g}}_{1}
is radial.
Letting
f(t)=\int_{B(0,t)}\chi
, the function
f
is differentiable, bounded by
\pi\|\chi\|_{L^{\infty}}t^{2}
and its derivative is bounded by
2\pi t\|\chi\|_{L^{\infty}}
. On the other hand, Stokes’s formula gives that

\int_{\partial B(0,t)}\frac{\partial\tilde{\mathsf{g}}_{1}}{\partial\nu}=f(t)

where
\nu
denotes the outer unit normal to
B(0,t)
. Viewing
\tilde{\mathsf{g}}_{1}
as a function of one variable, we then find that

-\tilde{\mathsf{g}}_{1}^{\prime}(t)=\frac{f(t)}{2\pi t}
, and from the above estimates on
f
, we find that
|\tilde{\mathsf{g}}_{1}^{\prime}|\leq Ct
and
|\tilde{\mathsf{g}}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(t)|\leq C
for every
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and this easily implies that
D^{2}\tilde{\mathsf{g}}_{1}
, seen as a function of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, is bounded, implying the result for
\tilde{\mathsf{g}}_{1}
. The result for
\mathsf{g}_{1}
is then straightforward.
∎

We next state another preliminary result.

Lemma 3.2

(Monotonicity)
.

If
0<\alpha_{1}\leq\eta_{1}
and
0<\alpha_{2}\leq\eta_{2}
, then we have pointwise

(3.3)

\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\alpha_{1})}*\delta_{0}^{(\alpha_{2})}\geq\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\eta_{1})}*\delta_{0}^{(\eta_{2})}.

In particular, in view of (
2.6
), if
0<\alpha\leq\eta
, then

(3.4)

\mathsf{g}_{\alpha}\geq\mathsf{g}_{\eta}.

Proof.

We first show that if
\alpha\leq\eta
, then

(3.5)

\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\alpha)}\geq\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\eta)}.

Let
u_{\alpha,\eta}\coloneqq\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\alpha)}-\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\eta)}
.
By definition of
\delta_{0}^{(\eta)}
as
\frac{1}{\eta^{2}}\chi(\frac{\cdot}{\eta})
with
\chi\geq 0
supported in
B(0,1)
, we have

-\Delta u_{\alpha,\eta}=2\pi\Bigr(\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\chi\Bigr(\frac{x}{\alpha}\Bigr)-\frac{1}{\eta^{2}}\chi\Bigr(\frac{x}{\eta}\Bigr)\Bigr),

Thus, for any
r>0
,

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,r)}\Delta u_{\alpha,\eta}

\displaystyle=2\pi\int_{B(0,r)}\frac{1}{\eta^{2}}\chi\Bigr(\frac{x}{\eta}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}x-2\pi\int_{B(0,r)}\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\chi\Bigr(\frac{x}{\alpha}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=2\pi\int_{B(0,\frac{r}{\eta})}\chi(x)\mathrm{d}x-2\pi\int_{B(0,\frac{r}{\alpha})}\chi(x)\mathrm{d}x,

where we have used a change of variables. Since
\alpha\leq\eta
and
\chi\geq 0
, this is nonpositive.

By Stokes’s theorem, we deduce that for every
r>0
,

\int_{\partial B(0,r)}\frac{\partial u_{\alpha,\eta}}{\partial\nu}\leq 0,

where
\nu
is the outer unit normal to
B(0,r)
.
But
u_{\alpha,\eta}
is radial, hence
\frac{\partial u_{\alpha,\eta}}{\partial\nu}
is constant on
\partial B(0,r)
and also equal to
\frac{\partial u_{\alpha,\eta}}{\partial r}
, so we have found that
\frac{\partial u_{\alpha,\eta}}{\partial r}\leq 0
, that is
u_{\alpha,\eta}
is nonincreasing in
r
. On the other hand, by Newton’s theorem,
\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\alpha)}
and
\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\eta)}

both coincide with
\mathsf{g}
in
B(0,\eta)^{c}
, hence
u_{\alpha,\eta}=0
there. We deduce that
u_{\alpha,\eta}\geq 0
everywhere and (
3.5
) holds.

Next, we write

\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\alpha_{1})}*\delta_{0}^{(\alpha_{2})}-\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\eta_{1})}*\delta_{0}^{(\eta_{2})}=\Bigr(\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\alpha_{1})}*\delta_{0}^{(\alpha_{2})}-\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\alpha_{1})}*\delta_{0}^{(\eta_{2})}\Bigr)+\Bigr(\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\alpha_{1})}*\delta_{0}^{(\eta_{2})}-\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\eta_{1})}*\delta_{0}^{(\eta_{2})}\Bigr).

By (
3.5
),

\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\alpha_{1})}*\delta_{0}^{(\alpha_{2})}-\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\alpha_{1})}*\delta_{0}^{(\eta_{2})}=\Bigr(\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\alpha_{2})}-\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\eta_{2})}\Bigr)*\delta_{0}^{(\alpha_{1})}\geq 0.

Similarly,

\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\alpha_{1})}*\delta_{0}^{(\eta_{2})}-\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\eta_{1})}*\delta_{0}^{(\eta_{2})}\geq 0.

Combining the above two displays concludes the proof of (
3.3
).

∎

3.2.
Lower bound on the energy

Let us now describe how to deduce easy energy lower bounds via the method of growth of radii.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
\sigma_{N}:(\Lambda^{2N},\mathcal{B}(\Lambda^{2N}))\to\Sigma_{N}
be the stable matching. Let
\tau_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}^{-}\geq\lambda,1\leq i\leq N
be a set of positive variables. For each
i,j\in[N]
with
i\neq j
, denote

(3.6)

v_{ij}^{\prime}\coloneqq\Bigr(\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}-\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{i}^{+})}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{j}^{+})}\Bigr)(x_{i}-x_{j})+\Bigr(\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}-\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{i}^{-})}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{j}^{-})}\Bigr)(y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}-y_{\sigma_{N}(j)})\\
-\Bigr(\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}-\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{i}^{-})}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{j}^{+})}\Bigr)(y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}-x_{j})-\Bigr(\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}-\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{i}^{+})}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{j}^{-})}\Bigr)(x_{i}-y_{\sigma_{N}(j)}).

Assume that there are two sets
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
such that
[N]=I_{1}\sqcup I_{2}
and

(3.7)

\displaystyle\text{for every}\ i\in I_{1}\text{ we have }|x_{i}-y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}|\leq 2(\tau_{i}^{+}+\tau_{i}^{-})\text{ and }\tau_{i}^{+}=\tau_{i}^{-}

(3.8)

\displaystyle\text{for every}\ i\ \in I_{2}\text{ we have }|x_{i}-y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}|>2(\tau_{i}^{+}+\tau_{i}^{-}).

Then, there exists a constant
C>0
depending only on
\mathsf{g}_{1}
such that

(3.9)

\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})\geq-\sum_{i\in I_{1}}\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-y_{\sigma_{N}(i)})-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}(\mathsf{g}(\tau_{i}^{+})+\mathsf{g}(\tau_{i}^{-})+2\kappa)\\
+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq N:i\neq j}v_{ij}^{\prime}-C\sum_{i\in I_{1}}\Bigr(\frac{|x_{i}-y_{\sigma_{N}(i)|}}{\tau_{i}^{+}}\Bigr)^{2}.

The crucial point here is that the terms in
v_{ij}^{\prime}
vanish as soon as
B(x_{i},\tau_{i})\cap B(x_{j},\tau_{j})=\emptyset
(or respectively the same with
x_{i}
and
y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}
etc. Indeed, when
B(z_{i},\tau_{i})\cap B(z_{j},\tau_{j})=\emptyset
, then
B(z_{i},\lambda)\cap B(z_{j},\lambda)=\emptyset
and therefore by Newton’s theorem (or the mean value property),

\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\delta_{z_{i}}^{(\lambda)}(x)\delta_{z_{j}}^{(\lambda)}(y)-\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\delta_{z_{i}}^{(\tau_{i})}(x)\delta_{z_{j}}^{(\tau_{j})}(y)=0.

The above proposition thus allows us to bound from below the energy by the energy within dipole plus dipole pair interactions concerning a reduced set of pairs (that will depend on the choices of
\tau_{i}
), up to an error.

Proof.

For the proof, we will assume that
\sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Id}
, which we can do without loss of generality.
Here we introduce the notation
z_{i}
,
i=1,\cdots,2N
to denote any point in the collection, whether positively or negatively charged, and
d_{i}=\pm 1
its charge (positive if it belongs to
\vec{X}_{N}
, negative to
\vec{Y}_{N}
).

For
i=1,\ldots,N
, we write temporarily
\tau_{i}=\tau_{i}^{+}
and for
i=N+1,\ldots,2N
,
\tau_{i}=\tau_{i}^{-}
. In view of (
2.4
) and (
3.2
), one can express the variation of energy as

(3.10)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla h_{\lambda}|^{2}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla h_{\vec{\tau}}|^{2}

\displaystyle=2\pi\sum_{i,j}d_{i}d_{j}\left(\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\delta_{z_{i}}^{(\lambda)}(x)\delta_{z_{j}}^{(\lambda)}(y)-\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\delta_{z_{i}}^{(\tau_{i})}(x)\delta_{z_{j}}^{(\tau_{j})}(y)\right)

\displaystyle\geq 2\pi\sum_{i=1}^{2N}(\mathsf{g}(\lambda)-\mathsf{g}(\tau_{i}))

\displaystyle+2\pi\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq 2N}d_{i}d_{j}\left(\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\delta_{z_{i}}^{(\lambda)}(x)\delta_{z_{j}}^{(\lambda)}(y)-\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\delta_{z_{i}}^{(\tau_{i})}(x)\delta_{z_{j}}^{(\tau_{j})}(y)\right).

By (
2.54
),

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla h_{\lambda}|^{2}-N(\mathsf{g}(\lambda)+\kappa)\geq\frac{1}{4\pi}\Bigr(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla h_{\lambda}|^{2}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla h_{\vec{\tau}}|^{2}\Bigr)-N(\mathsf{g}(\lambda)+\kappa)

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{2N}\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\delta_{z_{i}}^{(\tau_{i})}(x)\delta_{z_{i}}^{(\tau_{i})}(y)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq 2N}d_{i}d_{j}\left(\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\delta_{z_{i}}^{(\lambda)}(x)\delta_{z_{j}}^{(\lambda)}(y)-\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\delta_{z_{i}}^{(\tau_{i})}(x)\delta_{z_{j}}^{(\tau_{j})}(y)\right).

One can perform a resummation over dipole indices. Recalling
v_{ij}^{\prime}
defined in (
3.6
) and spelling out
z_{i}=x_{i}
or
z_{i}=y_{i}
, one can write

\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq 2N}d_{i}d_{j}\left(\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\delta_{z_{i}}^{(\lambda)}(x)\delta_{z_{j}}^{(\lambda)}(y)-\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\delta_{z_{i}}^{(\tau_{i})}(x)\delta_{z_{j}}^{(\tau_{j})}(y)\right)\\
=\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq N:i\neq j}v_{ij}^{\prime}-2\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\lambda)}(x)\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\lambda)}(y)-\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{+})}(x)\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{-})}(y)\right).

It follows in view of (
2.4
) that

(3.11)

\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})\geq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\neq j}v_{ij}^{\prime}-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-y_{i})+A

where

A\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{+})}(x)\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{-})}(y)-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{+})}(x)\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{+})}(y)\\
-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{-})}(x)\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{-})}(y)=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\mathrm{d}(\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{+})}-\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{-})})(y)\mathrm{d}(\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{+})}-\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{-})})(y).

Let now
i\in I_{1}
. Then by assumption,
\tau_{i}^{+}=\tau_{i}^{-}
. We claim that

(3.12)

\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\mathrm{d}(\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{+})}-\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{+})})(x)\mathrm{d}(\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{+})}-\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{+})})(y)=O\Bigr(\Bigr(\frac{|x_{i}-y_{i}|}{\tau_{i}^{+}}\Bigr)^{2}\Bigr).

Indeed, by scaling, one can check that

(3.13)

\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\mathrm{d}(\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{+})}-\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{+})})(x)\mathrm{d}(\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{+})}-\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{+})})(y)=\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\mathrm{d}(\delta_{z}^{(1)}-\delta_{0}^{(1)})(x)\mathrm{d}(\delta_{z}^{(1)}-\delta_{0}^{(1)})(y),

where
z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
is such that
|z|=\frac{|x_{i}-y_{i}|}{\tau_{i}^{+}}
. First, note that

(3.14)

\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\mathrm{d}(\delta_{z}^{(1)}-\delta_{0}^{(1)})(x)\mathrm{d}(\delta_{z}^{(1)}-\delta_{0}^{(1)})(y)=-\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\mathrm{d}\delta_{0}^{(1)}(x)\mathrm{d}(\delta_{-z}^{(1)}-\delta_{0}^{(1)})(y)\\
-\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\mathrm{d}\delta_{0}^{(1)}(x)\mathrm{d}(\delta_{z}^{(1)}-\delta_{0}^{(1)})(y).

Set
\tilde{\mathsf{g}}_{1}=\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(1)}
. By Taylor expansion and Lemma
3.1
, we have

\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\mathrm{d}\delta_{0}^{(1)}(x)\mathrm{d}(\delta_{z}^{(1)}-\delta_{0}^{(1)})(y)=\int_{B(0,1)}(\tilde{\mathsf{g}}_{1}(x+z)-\tilde{\mathsf{g}}_{1}(x))\mathrm{d}x=\int_{B(0,1)}\nabla\tilde{\mathsf{g}}_{1}(x)\cdot z\,\mathrm{d}x+O(|z|^{2}).

Then by Stokes’s theorem,
