\int_{B(0,1)}\nabla\tilde{\mathsf{g}}_{1}(x)\cdot z\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\partial B(0,1)}\tilde{\mathsf{g}}_{1}(x)z\cdot\nu\,\mathrm{d}x=0,

where we have used the fact that
\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(1)}=\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}=0
on
\partial B(0,1)
by Newton’s theorem and from the properties of
\mathsf{g}=-\log|\cdot|
. We deduce from the above that

\int\mathsf{g}(x-y)\mathrm{d}\delta_{0}^{(1)}(x)\mathrm{d}(\delta_{z}^{(1)}-\delta_{0}^{(1)})(y)=O(|z|^{2}).

Replacing
z
by
-z
, it follows from (
3.14
) that

\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\mathrm{d}(\delta_{z}^{(1)}-\delta_{0}^{(1)})(x)\mathrm{d}(\delta_{z}^{(1)}-\delta_{0}^{(1)})(y)=O(|z|^{2}),

which proves (
3.12
) in view of (
3.13
).

Now suppose that
i\in I_{2}
so that
|x_{i}-y_{i}|>2(\tau_{i}^{+}+\tau_{i}^{-})\geq 2\lambda
. Then,
B(x_{i},\tau_{i}^{+})
and
B(y_{\sigma_{N}(i)},\tau_{i}^{-})
are disjoint, hence by Newton’s theorem we find

(3.15)

\begin{split}\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\mathrm{d}(\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{+})}-\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{-})})(x)\mathrm{d}(\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{+})}-\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{-})})(y)&=(\mathsf{g}(\tau_{i}^{+})+\mathsf{g}(\tau_{i}^{-})+2\kappa)-2\mathsf{g}(x_{i}-y_{i})\\
&=\mathsf{g}(\tau_{i}^{+})+\mathsf{g}(\tau_{i}^{-})+2\kappa-2\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-y_{i}),\end{split}

in view of (
2.4
) and the fact that
|x_{i}-y_{i}|>2\lambda
.

Inserting (
3.12
) and (
3.15
) into (
3.11
) shows the result.

∎

3.3.
Upper bound on the energy

The electric formulation is also useful in obtaining energy upper bounds by constructing electric potentials only over a union of balls of the domain. To do so, we consider electric fields
E=\nabla h_{\lambda}
and relax the condition that they be gradient fields. The Neumann boundary condition is crucial.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})
be a configuration with stable matching
\sigma_{N}
. For each
i\in[N]
let
z_{i}
be the barycenter of
\{x_{i},y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}\}
. Letting
\tau
be such that for any
i\in[N]
we have
\tau\geq 8\max(r_{i},\lambda)
(where we recall
r_{i}
from Definition
2.2
), we define for each
i
,
u_{i}
to be the solution to

(3.16)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\Delta u_{i}=2\pi(\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\lambda)}-\delta_{y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}}^{(\lambda)})&\text{in}\ B(z_{i},\tau)\\
\frac{\partial u_{i}}{\partial\nu}=0&\text{on}\ \partial B(z_{i},\tau).\end{array}\right.

Then

(3.17)

\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})\leq-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-y_{\sigma_{N}(i)})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\neq j}\tilde{v}_{ij}+O\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{r_{i}^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\right),

where

(3.18)

\tilde{v}_{ij}\coloneqq\int_{B(z_{i},\tau)\cap B(z_{j},\tau)}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}.

Although it is not really needed, the solution to (
3.16
) can be computed explicitly using the classical method of image charges, which is closely related to the Schwarz reflection principle from complex analysis. To this end, consider the Neumann Green’s function
G_{1}(x,y)
on the unit ball
B_{1}
, defined as the solution to

\begin{cases}-\Delta G_{1}(x,y)=2\pi(\delta_{y}-1)&\text{in }B_{1},\\
\frac{\partial G_{1}}{\partial\nu}(x,y)=0&\text{on }\partial B_{1},\quad\text{where $y\in B_{1}$.}\end{cases}

The method of image charges introduces a fictitious charge placed outside the domain to explicitly satisfy the Neumann boundary condition. For the unit disk
B_{1}
, the appropriate image charge for a point charge at
y\in B_{1}
is obtained by reflection across the boundary circle
\partial B_{1}
, specifically at the point

y^{*}=\frac{y}{|y|^{2}}.

The explicit expression for the Neumann Green’s function is given by

(3.19)

G_{1}(x,y)=-\log|x-y|-\log|x-y^{*}|+\frac{|x|^{2}}{2},\text{ for }|x|\leq 1.

This can be checked using that

|x-y^{*}|^{2}|y|^{2}=|x-y|^{2}\quad\text{for $|x|=1$}.

We will need the following lemma.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
u_{i}
be as in (
3.16
) and let
\tilde{u}_{i}\coloneqq u_{i}-\mathsf{g}*(\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\lambda)}-\delta_{y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}}^{(\lambda)})

We have

(3.20)

\displaystyle\|\nabla\tilde{u}_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(B(z_{i},\tau))}\leq C\frac{r_{i}}{\tau^{2}},

(3.21)

\displaystyle|\nabla u_{i}(x)|\leq C\frac{r_{i}}{|x-z_{i}|^{2}}\quad\text{if}\ x\in B(z_{i},\tau)\backslash B(z_{i},2\max(r_{i},\lambda)),

(3.22)

\displaystyle|\nabla u_{i}(x)|\leq\frac{C}{\mathrm{dist}(x,\{x_{i}\}\cup\{y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}\})}+C\frac{r_{i}}{\tau^{2}}\quad\text{for all }\ x\in B(z_{i},\tau),

where
C
is universal.

Proof.

For simplicity of notation, we assume that
\sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Id}
. First of all, by scaling and translation invariance, it suffices to prove the result for
\tau=1
and
z_{i}=0
, noting that
\nabla\tilde{u}_{i}(x)
equals
\frac{1}{\tau}
times the gradient of its rescaled function that solves the equation on
B(0,1)
.
The function
\tilde{u}_{i}
solves

(3.23)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\Delta\tilde{u}_{i}=0&\text{in}\ B(0,1)\\
\frac{\partial\tilde{u}_{i}}{\partial\nu}=\left(-\frac{x-x_{i}}{|x-x_{i}|^{2}}+\frac{x-y_{i}}{|x-y_{i}|^{2}}\right)\cdot\nu&\text{on}\ \partial B(0,1).\end{array}\right.

The relation (
3.20
) follows from (
3.23
), the fact that
\|\frac{\partial\tilde{u}_{i}}{\partial\nu}\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial B(0,1))}\leq Cr_{i}
and elliptic regularity estimates, then scaling. Alternatively,
by (
3.19
), for
x\in B(0,1)
, we have

(3.24)

u_{i}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathsf{g}(x-y)\mathrm{d}\left(\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\lambda)}-\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\lambda)}\right)(y)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathsf{g}(x-y^{*})\mathrm{d}\left(\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\lambda)}-\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\lambda)}\right)(y),

so that

\tilde{u}_{i}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathsf{g}(x-y^{*})\mathrm{d}\left(\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\lambda)}-\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\lambda)}\right)(y)

from which we can also deduce (
3.20
), after scaling. The relations (
3.21
) and (
3.22
) are then straightforward from the properties of
\mathsf{g}
.

∎

Proof.

Without loss of generality we assume that
\sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Id}
. Following
[
SS15
]
and subsequent works,
we define a global “electric field”
E
by pasting together the electric fields defined over these non-disjoint balls:

E\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{B(z_{i},\tau)}\nabla u_{i}.

Thanks to the crucial choice of zero Neumann boundary conditions on the boundary of the disjoint balls, this vector field satisfies

(3.25)

-\mathrm{div}\,E=2\pi\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\lambda)}-\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\lambda)}\right)=-\Delta h_{\lambda}

where
h_{\lambda}
is the electric potential of the configuration as in (
2.52
).
The trick is then to take advantage of the
L^{2}
projection property onto gradients to show that the energy can be estimated from above by the
L^{2}
norm of
E
:
indeed

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|E|^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla h_{\lambda}|^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|E-\nabla h_{\lambda}|^{2}+2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(E-\nabla h_{\lambda})\cdot\nabla h_{\lambda}

and the last term vanishes after integration by parts, in view of (
3.25
).
It follows from (
2.54
) that

(3.26)

\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})\leq\frac{1}{4\pi}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{B(z_{i},\tau)}|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}+\sum_{i\neq j}\int_{B(z_{i},\tau)\cap B(z_{j},\tau)}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}-N(\mathsf{g}(\lambda)+\kappa).

Integrating by parts and using (
3.16
), we have

\int_{B(z_{i},\tau)}|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}=2\pi\int_{B(z_{i},\tau)}u_{i}\,\mathrm{d}(\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\lambda)}-\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\lambda)})

and decomposing
u_{i}
as
\mathsf{g}*(\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\lambda)}-\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\lambda)}))+\tilde{u}_{i}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{B(z_{i},\tau)}|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}

\displaystyle=4\pi(\mathsf{g}(\lambda)+\kappa)-4\pi\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-y_{i})+2\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\tilde{u}_{i}\,\mathrm{d}(\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\lambda)}-\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\lambda)})

\displaystyle=4\pi(\mathsf{g}(\lambda)+\kappa)-4\pi\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-y_{i})+O\Bigr(\frac{r_{i}^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\Bigr)

in view of (
3.20
). The result follows.
∎

3.4.
Decay of the true dipole interaction

We denote by
v_{ij}
the true dipole interaction.

Definition 3.6

(Non-truncated interaction)
.

For every
i,j\in[N]
with
i\neq j
, let

v_{ij}\coloneqq\iint\mathsf{g}(x-y)\mathrm{d}(\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\lambda)}-\delta_{y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}}^{(\lambda)})(x)\mathrm{d}(\delta_{x_{j}}^{(\lambda)}-\delta_{y_{\sigma_{N}(j)}}^{(\lambda)})(y).

Also let

(3.27)

\displaystyle v_{i,j,+}\coloneqq\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-x_{j})-\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}-x_{j})

(3.28)

\displaystyle v_{i,j,-}\coloneqq\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-y_{\sigma_{N}(j)})-\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}-y_{\sigma_{N}(j)}),

so that

v_{ij}=v_{i,j,+}-v_{i,j,-}.

We next estimate precisely the dipole-dipole interaction, and for reference we also record in item (1) in the lemma below a more precise result than we will need that holds under a stronger regularity assumption on the truncation.

Lemma 3.7

(Estimating the dipole-dipole interaction)
.

Let
x_{i},x_{j},y_{\sigma_{N}(i)},y_{\sigma_{N}(j)}\in\Lambda
be in stable matching. Let
z_{i},z_{j}
be the barycenters of
\{x_{i},y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}\}
and
\{x_{j},y_{\sigma_{N}(j)}\}
. Let
\vec{\rho}_{ij}\coloneqq z_{j}-z_{i}
,
\rho_{ij}\coloneqq|z_{i}-z_{j}|
,
\vec{r}_{i}\coloneqq y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}-x_{i}
,
\vec{r}_{j}=y_{\sigma_{N}(j)}-x_{j}
and
d_{ij}=\mathrm{dist}(\{x_{i},y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}\},\{x_{j},y_{\sigma_{N}(j)}\})
. Let
\vec{R}_{ij}=z_{j}-x_{i}
and
\vec{R}^{\prime}_{ij}=z_{j}-y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}
.
Let
v_{ij}
be as in Definition
3.6
. Viewing
\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}
as a function of
\mathbb{R}
, the following holds.

(1)

If we assume that
\mathsf{g}_{1}=\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(1)}
is of class
W^{3,\infty}
(i.e. its second derivative is Lipschitz), then if
d_{ij}\geq\min(r_{i},r_{j})
, we have

(3.29)

\displaystyle v_{ij}=-\frac{\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}^{\prime}(|\vec{R}_{ij}|)}{|\vec{R}_{ij}|}\vec{R}_{ij}\cdot\vec{r}_{j}+\frac{\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}^{\prime}(|\vec{R}^{\prime}_{ij}|)}{|\vec{R}^{\prime}_{ij}|}\vec{R}^{\prime}_{ij}\cdot\vec{r}_{j}+O\left(\frac{\min(r_{i},r_{j})^{3}}{\max(|\vec{R}_{ij}|,\lambda)^{3}}+\frac{\min(r_{i},r_{j})^{3}}{\max(|\vec{R}^{\prime}_{ij}|,\lambda)^{3}}\right),

and if in addition
d_{ij}\geq 4\max(r_{i},r_{j})
, we have

(3.30)

\displaystyle v_{ij}=-\frac{\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}^{\prime}(\rho_{ij})}{\rho_{ij}}(\vec{r}_{i}\cdot\vec{r}_{j})+\left(\frac{\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}^{\prime}(\rho_{ij})}{\rho_{ij}^{3}}-\frac{\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}(\rho_{ij})}{\rho_{ij}^{2}}\right)(\vec{\rho}_{ij}\cdot\vec{r}_{i})(\vec{\rho}_{ij}\cdot\vec{r}_{j})+O\left(\frac{\min(r_{i},r_{j})(r_{i}^{2}+r_{j}^{2})}{\max(\rho_{ij},\lambda)^{3}}\right),

with

(3.31)

\begin{cases}\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}^{\prime}(t)=-\frac{1}{t}\ \text{and }\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}(t)=\frac{1}{t^{2}}&\ \text{if}\ t\geq 2\lambda\\
|\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}^{\prime}(t)|\leq\frac{C}{\max(t,\lambda)}\quad|\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}(t)|\leq\frac{C}{(\max(t,\lambda))^{2}}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(2)

If we assume
\mathsf{g}_{1}
is only in
W^{2,\infty}
(which follows from our assumption here), then, if
d_{ij}\geq\min(r_{i},r_{j})
, we have

(3.32)

|v_{ij}|\leq C\frac{r_{i}r_{j}}{\max(d_{ij},\lambda)\max(d_{ij},\max(r_{i},r_{j}))}.

(3)

Let

(3.33)

d_{i,j}^{+}\coloneqq\mathrm{dist}(\{x_{i},y_{i}\},x_{j}),\quad d_{i,j}^{-}\coloneqq\mathrm{dist}(\{x_{i},y_{i}\},y_{j}).

If
d_{i,j}^{\pm}\geq r_{i}
, then,
v_{i,j,\pm}
being as in Definition
3.6
, under the assumption that
\mathsf{g}_{1}\in W^{2,\infty}
, we have

(3.34)

|v_{i,j,\pm}|\leq C\frac{r_{i}}{\max(d_{i,j}^{\pm},\lambda)}.

Here the constant
C>0
and the
O
’s depend only on
\|\mathsf{g}_{1}\|_{W^{2,\infty}}
, respectively
\|\mathsf{g}_{1}\|_{W^{3,\infty}}
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi_{\lambda}(t)=\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(\sqrt{t})
, considering
\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}
as a function of
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Without loss of generality, let us assume that
r_{j}\leq r_{i}
, and denote
\vec{\rho}
for
\vec{\rho}_{ij}
.
We will need to evaluate
\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}
and
\varphi_{\lambda}^{(3)}
.
First we note that by scaling and the definition of
\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}
(
2.6
), we have

(3.35)

\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(z)=\mathsf{g}(\lambda)+\mathsf{g}_{1}\Bigr(\frac{z}{\lambda}\Bigr).

Moreover, by assumption and Lemma
3.1
, the function
\mathsf{g}_{1}
has two or three uniformly bounded derivatives in
B(0,2)
, while for
|z|\geq 2
, we have without further assumption that
\mathsf{g}_{1}(z)=\mathsf{g}(z)
, whose derivatives equal those of
-\log|z|
. It follows that for every
0\leq k\leq 2
(resp.
\leq 3
for the first item), we have
|\mathsf{g}_{1}^{(k)}(z)|\leq\frac{C}{|\max(z,1)|^{k}}
. Note that this and (
3.35
) proves (
3.31
).

Since
\varphi_{\lambda}(t)=\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(\sqrt{t})=\mathsf{g}(\lambda)+\mathsf{g}_{1}(\frac{\sqrt{t}}{\lambda})

we obtain with the Faa-di-Bruno formula that for every
k\geq 1
,

(3.36)

\varphi_{\lambda}^{(k)}(t)\leq\frac{C}{|\max(t,\lambda^{2})|^{k}},

where
C>0
depends only on the derivatives of
\mathsf{g}_{1}
.

Let us now turn to the proof of the first item and recall that we assume
\min(r_{i},r_{j})=r_{j}\leq d_{ij}
.
Let us set
\vec{R}=\vec{R_{ij}}=\vec{\rho}+\frac{1}{2}\vec{r}_{i}
, which is the vector
\vec{x_{i}z_{j}}
and let
R
denote its norm. By property of the barycenter, we must have
R\geq d_{ij}\geq r_{j}
.

We then consider

\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-x_{j})-\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-y_{j})
and expand it to find

\displaystyle\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-x_{j})-\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-y_{j})

\displaystyle=\varphi_{\lambda}\left(\left|\vec{\rho}+\frac{\vec{r}_{i}-\vec{r}_{j}}{2}\right|^{2}\right)-\varphi_{\lambda}\left(\left|\vec{\rho}+\frac{\vec{r}_{i}+\vec{r}_{j}}{2}\right|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{\lambda}\left(\left|\vec{R}-\frac{\vec{r}_{j}}{2}\right|^{2}\right)-\varphi_{\lambda}\left(\left|\vec{R}+\frac{\vec{r}_{j}}{2}\right|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{\lambda}\left(R^{2}+\frac{1}{4}r_{j}^{2}-\vec{R}\cdot\vec{r}_{j}\right)-\varphi_{\lambda}\left(R^{2}+\frac{1}{4}r_{j}^{2}+\vec{R}\cdot\vec{r}_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=-2\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime}(R^{2}+\frac{1}{4}r_{j}^{2})\vec{R}\cdot\vec{r}_{j}+O\left(\sup_{\sqrt{t}\in[|\vec{R}-\frac{\vec{r}_{j}}{2}|,|\vec{R}+\frac{\vec{r}_{j}}{2}|]}|\varphi_{\lambda}^{(3)}(t)|R^{3}r_{j}^{3}\right)

(3.37)

\displaystyle=-2\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime}(R^{2})\vec{R}\cdot\vec{r}_{j}+O\Bigg(\sup_{t\in[R^{2},R^{2}+\frac{1}{4}r_{j}^{2}]}|\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}(t)|Rr_{j}^{3}+\sup_{\sqrt{t}\in[|\vec{R}-\frac{\vec{r}_{j}}{2}|,|\vec{R}+\frac{\vec{r}_{j}}{2}|]}|\varphi_{\lambda}^{(3)}(t)|R^{3}r_{j}^{3}\Bigg).

Alternatively, using only two derivatives of
\varphi_{\lambda}
, we can write

(3.38)

\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-x_{j})-\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-y_{j})=-2\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime}(R^{2})\vec{R}\cdot\vec{r}_{j}\\
+O\Bigg(\sup_{\sqrt{t}\in[|\vec{R}-\frac{\vec{r}_{j}}{2}|,|\vec{R}+\frac{\vec{r}_{j}}{2}|]}|\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}(t)|R^{2}r_{j}^{2}+\sup_{t\in[R^{2},R^{2}+\frac{1}{4}r_{j}^{2}]}|\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}(t)|Rr_{j}^{3}\Bigg).

In view of (
3.36
), we have thus obtained that

(3.39)

\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-x_{j})-\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-y_{j})=-2\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime}(R^{2})\vec{R}\cdot\vec{r}_{j}+O\left(\frac{r_{j}^{3}}{\max(R,\lambda)^{3}}\right)

if
\mathsf{g}_{1}
has three bounded derivatives, or alternatively

(3.40)

\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-x_{j})-\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-y_{j})=-2\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime}(R^{2})\vec{R}\cdot\vec{r}_{2}+O\left(\frac{r_{j}^{2}}{\max(R,\lambda)^{2}}\right)

if
\mathsf{g}_{1}
has only two bounded derivatives.

We next turn to

\displaystyle\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(y_{i}-x_{j})-\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(y_{i}-y_{j})=\varphi_{\lambda}\left(\left|\vec{\rho}-\frac{\vec{r}_{i}+\vec{r}_{j}}{2}\right|^{2}\right)-\varphi_{\lambda}\left(\left|\vec{\rho}+\frac{\vec{r}_{j}-\vec{r}_{i}}{2}\right|^{2}\right).

This is then exactly the same computation with
\vec{R}
replaced by
\vec{R}^{\prime}=\vec{R}^{\prime}_{ij}=\vec{\rho}-\frac{1}{2}\vec{r}_{i}
, the vector
\vec{y_{i}z_{j}}
, and in the same way, we have
R^{\prime}\geq d_{ij}\geq r_{j}
.
Thus, we find

\displaystyle\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(y_{i}-x_{j})-\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(y_{i}-y_{j})=-2\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime}((R^{\prime})^{2})(\vec{R^{\prime}}\cdot\vec{r}_{j})+O\left(\frac{r_{j}^{3}}{(\max(R^{\prime},\lambda))^{3}}\right).

Subtracting this from (
3.39
), if
\mathsf{g}_{1}
has three bounded derivatives, the total interaction is then

\displaystyle v_{ij}

\displaystyle=-2\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime}(R^{2})\vec{R}\cdot\vec{r}_{j}+2\varphi^{\prime}((R^{\prime})^{2})\vec{R^{\prime}}\cdot\vec{r}_{j}+O\left(\frac{r_{j}^{3}}{\max(R,\lambda)^{3}}+\frac{r_{j}^{3}}{(\max(R^{\prime},\lambda))^{3}}\right)

\displaystyle=-2\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime}\left(|\vec{\rho}+\frac{1}{2}\vec{r}_{i}|^{2}\right)(\vec{\rho}\cdot\vec{r}_{j}+\frac{1}{2}\vec{r}_{i}\cdot\vec{r}_{j})+2\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime}\left(|\vec{\rho}-\frac{1}{2}\vec{r}_{i}|^{2}\right)(\vec{\rho}\cdot\vec{r}_{j}-\frac{1}{2}\vec{r}_{i}\cdot\vec{r}_{j})

\displaystyle+O\left(\frac{r_{j}^{3}}{\max(|\vec{\rho}+\frac{1}{2}\vec{r}_{i}|,\lambda)^{3}}+\frac{r_{j}^{3}}{\max(|\vec{\rho}-\frac{1}{2}\vec{r}_{i}|,\lambda)^{3}}\right).

If in addition, we have
r_{i}\leq\frac{1}{4}d_{ij}
then
r_{i}\leq\frac{1}{2}\rho
and using (
3.36
) we can expand this further into

\displaystyle v_{ij}

\displaystyle=-2\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime}(\rho^{2})(\vec{r}_{i}\cdot\vec{r}_{j})

\displaystyle-2\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}(\rho^{2})\left((\vec{\rho}\cdot\vec{r}_{i}+\frac{1}{4}r_{i}^{2})(\vec{\rho}\cdot\vec{r}_{j}+\frac{1}{2}\vec{r}_{i}\cdot\vec{r}_{j})+(\vec{\rho}\cdot\vec{r}_{i}-\frac{1}{4}r_{i}^{2})(\vec{\rho}\cdot\vec{r}_{j}-\frac{1}{2}\vec{r}_{i}\cdot\vec{r}_{j})\right)

\displaystyle+O\left(\frac{r_{i}^{2}r_{j}}{\max(\rho,\lambda)^{3}}+\frac{r_{j}^{3}}{\max(\rho,\lambda)^{3}}\right)

\displaystyle=-2\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime}(\rho^{2})(\vec{r}_{i}\cdot\vec{r}_{j})-4\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}(\rho^{2})(\vec{\rho}\cdot\vec{r}_{i})(\vec{\rho}\cdot\vec{r}_{j})+O\left(\frac{r_{i}^{2}r_{j}}{(\max(\rho,\lambda)^{3}}\right).

Reexpressing things in terms of
\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}
, we obtain the result.

Let us now turn to the proof of the second item.
If we assume that
\mathsf{g}_{1}
has only two bounded derivatives and
d_{ij}\geq r_{j}
,
we can subtract (
3.40
) and the corresponding result with
\vec{R^{\prime}}
and find

(3.41)

\displaystyle v_{ij}=-2\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime}(R^{2})\vec{R}\cdot\vec{r}_{j}+2\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime}((R^{\prime})^{2})\vec{R^{\prime}}\cdot\vec{r}_{j}+O\left(\frac{r_{j}^{2}}{\max(R,\lambda)^{2}}+\frac{r_{j}^{2}}{\max(R^{\prime},\lambda)^{2}}\right).

In the case where
r_{j}\leq d_{ij}\leq r_{i}
, we may bound this using (
3.36
) and
\min(R,R^{\prime})\geq d_{ij}
to obtain

|v_{ij}|\leq Cr_{j}\left(\frac{R}{\max(R,\lambda)^{2}}+\frac{R^{\prime}}{\max(R^{\prime},\lambda)^{2}}+\frac{r_{j}}{\max(R,\lambda)^{2}}+\frac{r_{j}}{\max(R^{\prime},\lambda)^{2}}\right)\leq\frac{r_{j}}{\max(d_{ij},\lambda)}.

In the case
d_{ij}\geq r_{i}\geq r_{j}
, we can expand (
3.41
) further to find

\displaystyle v_{ij}=

\displaystyle-2\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime}\left(|\vec{\rho}+\frac{1}{2}\vec{r}_{i}|^{2}\right)(\vec{\rho}\cdot\vec{r}_{j}+\frac{1}{2}\vec{r}_{i}\cdot\vec{r}_{j})+2\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime}\left(|\vec{\rho}-\frac{1}{2}\vec{r}_{i}|^{2}\right)(\vec{\rho}\cdot\vec{r}_{j}-\frac{1}{2}\vec{r}_{i}\cdot\vec{r}_{j})

\displaystyle+O\left(\frac{r_{j}^{2}}{\max(R,\lambda)^{2}}+\frac{r_{j}^{2}}{\max(R^{\prime},\lambda)^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(\sup_{\sqrt{t}\in[R,R^{\prime}]]}|\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}(t)|\rho^{2}r_{i}r_{j}+\sup_{\sqrt{t}\in[R,R^{\prime}]}|\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime}(t)|r_{i}r_{j}+\frac{r_{j}^{2}}{\max(R,\lambda)^{2}}+\frac{r_{j}^{2}}{\max(R^{\prime},\lambda)^{2}}\right).

Using
\min(R,R^{\prime})\geq d_{ij}
and (
3.36
) again, we then obtain

|v_{ij}|\leq C\frac{r_{i}r_{j}}{\max(d_{ij},\lambda)^{2}}.

In both cases, (
3.32
) follows.

For the proof of the third item, we treat the positive case, the negative one is analogous. Let
\vec{R}=\vec{x_{j}z_{i}}
. We have
R\geq d_{i,j}^{+}\geq r_{i}
.
In view of (
3.38
) and (
3.36
), we have

|v_{i,j,+}|\leq 2\sup_{\sqrt{t}\in[|\vec{R}-\frac{1}{2}\vec{r_{i}}|,|\vec{R}+\frac{1}{2}\vec{r_{i}}|]}|\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime}(t)|Rr_{i}\leq C\frac{1}{\max(R,\lambda)^{2}}Rr_{i}

and since
d_{i,j}^{+}-\frac{1}{2}r_{i}\leq R\leq d_{i,j}^{+}+\frac{1}{2}r_{i}
, the result follows.
∎

Remark 3.8
.

If we have
d_{ij}\geq 4\lambda
(resp.
d_{i,j}^{\pm}\geq 4\lambda
), then the calculations only involve derivatives of
\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(t)
in the region
t\geq 2\lambda
where it coincides with
\mathsf{g}
, and then the results hold without any assumption on the regularization
\mathsf{g}_{1}
.

3.5.
Reduced models and control on error terms

Lemma 3.9

(Errors from the lower bound)
.

Let
\{x_{i},y_{i}\}_{i\in[N]}\in\Lambda
. Denote as above
r_{i}\coloneqq|x_{i}-y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}|
, and
d_{ij}\coloneqq d(\{x_{i},y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}\},\{x_{j},y_{\sigma_{N}(j)}\})
.
For
i\in[N]
, let
\tau_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}^{-}\geq\lambda
be a set of positive variables.

Let
v_{ij},v_{i,j,\pm},d_{i,j}^{\pm}
be as in Definition
3.6
and (
3.33
), and
v_{ij}^{\prime}
as in (
3.6
).
Also denote

(3.42)

\displaystyle v_{i,j,+}^{\prime}\coloneqq\Bigr(\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}-\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{i}^{+})}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{j}^{+})}\Bigr)(x_{i}-x_{j})-\Bigr(\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}-\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{i}^{-})}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{j}^{+})}\Bigr)(y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}-x_{j})

(3.43)

\displaystyle v_{i,j,-}^{\prime}\coloneqq\Bigr(\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}-\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{i}^{+})}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{j}^{-})}\Bigr)(x_{i}-y_{\sigma_{N}(j)})-\Bigr(\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}-\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{i}^{-})}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{j}^{-})}\Bigr)(y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}-y_{\sigma_{N}(j)}).

Then, the following holds.

(1)

We have

(3.44)

|v_{i,j,\pm}^{\prime}|\leq C\left(\frac{r_{i}}{\max(d_{i,j}^{\pm},\lambda)}+\frac{r_{i}}{\tau_{j}^{\pm}}+\frac{\max(\tau_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}^{-})^{2}}{(\tau_{j}^{\pm})^{2}}\right)\mathbf{1}_{d_{i,j}^{\pm}\leq\max(\tau_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}^{-})+\tau_{j}^{\pm}}

and

(3.45)

|v_{i,j,\pm}-v_{i,j,\pm}^{\prime}|\leq C\left(\frac{r_{i}}{\tau_{j}^{\pm}}+\frac{\max(\tau_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}^{-})^{2}}{(\tau_{j}^{\pm})^{2}}\right)\mathbf{1}_{d_{i,j}^{\pm}\leq\max(\tau_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}^{-})+\tau_{j}^{\pm}}+C\frac{r_{i}}{\max(d_{i,j}^{\pm},\lambda)}\mathbf{1}_{d_{i,j}^{\pm}>\max(\tau_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}^{-})+\tau_{j}^{\pm}}.

(2)

If
d_{i,j}^{\pm}\geq r_{i}
and
\tau_{i}^{+}=\tau_{i}^{-}=\tau_{i}
, we have

(3.46)

|v_{i,j,\pm}^{\prime}|\leq C\frac{r_{i}}{\max(d_{i,j}^{\pm},\lambda)}\mathbf{1}_{d_{i,j}^{\pm}\leq\tau_{i}+\tau_{j}^{\pm}}

and

(3.47)

|v_{i,j,\pm}-v_{i,j,\pm}^{\prime}|\leq C\frac{r_{i}}{\max(\tau_{i},\tau_{j}^{\pm})}\mathbf{1}_{d_{i,j}^{\pm}\leq\tau_{i}+\tau_{j}^{\pm}}+C\frac{r_{i}}{\max(d_{i,j}^{\pm},\lambda)}\mathbf{1}_{d_{i,j}^{\pm}>\tau_{i}+\tau_{j}^{\pm}}.

(3)

Assume that
r_{i}\leq 2(\tau_{i}^{+}+\tau_{i}^{-})
,
\tau_{i}^{+}=\tau_{i}^{-}=\tau_{i}
, the same for
j
, and
d_{ij}\geq\min(r_{i},r_{j})
. Then we have

(3.48)

|v_{ij}^{\prime}|\leq C\frac{r_{i}r_{j}}{\max(d_{ij},\lambda)\max(r_{i},r_{j},d_{ij})}\mathbf{1}_{d_{ij}\leq\tau_{i}+\tau_{j}}

and

(3.49)

|v_{ij}^{\prime}-v_{ij}|\leq C\frac{r_{i}r_{j}}{\max(\tau_{i},\tau_{j})^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{d_{ij}\leq\tau_{i}+\tau_{j}}+C\frac{r_{i}r_{j}}{\max(d_{ij},\lambda)\max(r_{i},r_{j},d_{ij})}\mathbf{1}_{d_{ij}>\tau_{i}+\tau_{j}}.

Proof.

Assume without loss of generality that
\sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Id}
.
Let us consider the
+
case, the other one is analogous.
For the first item, we note that if
d_{i,j,+}>\max(\tau_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}^{-})+\tau_{j}^{+}
, then

B(x_{i},\tau_{i}^{+})\cap B(x_{j},\tau_{j}^{+})=\emptyset
and
B(y_{i},\tau_{i}^{-})\cap B(x_{j},\tau_{j}^{+})=\emptyset
, which implies by Newton’s theorem, that
v_{i,j,+}^{\prime}=0
. The results then follow in view of Lemma
3.7
.

Let us turn to the case where
d_{i,j,+}\leq\max(\tau_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}^{-})+\tau_{j}^{+}
.
By the definitions, we have

(3.50)

v_{i,j,+}^{\prime}-v_{i,j,+}=-\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{i}^{+})}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{j}^{+})}(x_{i}-x_{j})+\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{i}^{-})}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{j}^{+})}(y_{i}-x_{j}).

Setting
f=\mathsf{g}*\delta_{x_{j}}^{(\tau_{j}^{+})}
, we may rewrite
this as

\displaystyle v_{i,j,+}^{\prime}-v_{i,j,+}

\displaystyle=\int f(\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{+})}-\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{-})})

\displaystyle=\int f(\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{+})}-\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{+})})+\int f(\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{+})}-\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{-})}).

The first term on the right-hand side is bounded by
|x_{i}-y_{i}|\|\nabla f\|_{L^{\infty}}
, moreover one can check in view of Lemma
3.1
and scaling that
f
is Lipschitz with constant
C(\tau_{j}^{+})^{-1}
. Thus the first term is bounded by
C\frac{r_{i}}{\tau_{j}^{+}}
. For the second term, Taylor-expanding
f
to order
2
near
y_{i}
, we obtain

\int f(\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{+})}-\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\tau_{i}^{-})})\leq C\|D^{2}f\|_{L^{\infty}}\max(\tau_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}^{-})^{2}\leq\frac{\max(\tau_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}^{-})^{2}}{(\tau_{j}^{+})^{2}}.

This proves (
3.45
) and (
3.44
) follows from Lemma
3.7
.

Let us turn to the second item, considering again the
+
case.
If
d_{i,j}^{+}>\tau_{i}+\tau_{j}^{+}
then
B(x_{i},\tau_{i})\cap B(x_{j},\tau_{j}^{+})=\emptyset
and
B(y_{i},\tau_{i})\cap B(x_{j},\tau_{j}^{+})=\emptyset
, hence by Newton’s theorem

v_{i,j,+}^{\prime}=0
. The results then follow from Lemma
3.7
.

Let us thus consider the case
d_{i,j,+}\leq\tau_{i}+\tau_{j}^{+}
.
Letting

f\coloneqq\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{i}^{+})}*\delta_{x_{j}}^{(\tau_{j}^{+})}=\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{i}^{-})}*\delta_{x_{j}}^{(\tau_{j}^{+})},

we may thus write in view of (
3.50
) that

|v_{i,j,+}^{\prime}-v_{i,j,+}|=|f(x_{i})-f(y_{i})|\leq\|\nabla f\|_{L^{\infty}}r_{i}.

Moreover,
\nabla f
is as regular as
\nabla\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\max(\tau_{i},\tau_{j}^{+}))}
, from which we deduce
\|\nabla f\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\frac{C}{\max(\tau_{i},\tau_{j}^{+})}
.
This proves (
3.47
), and from (
3.34
), (
3.46
) follows by using
d_{i,j,+}\leq\tau_{i}+\tau_{j}^{+}\leq 2\max(\tau_{i},\tau_{j}^{+})
to absorb terms.

Let us turn to the third item. If
d_{ij}>\tau_{i}+\tau_{j}
then

B(x_{i},\tau_{i})\cap B(x_{j},\tau_{j})=\emptyset
and the same with
(y_{i},y_{j})
,
(x_{i},y_{j})
, and
(y_{i},x_{j})
. By Newton’s theorem, this implies that
v_{ij}^{\prime}=0
, and the results follow in view of (
3.32
). Let us now turn to the case
d_{ij}\leq\tau_{i}+\tau_{j}\leq 2\max(\tau_{i},\tau_{j})
.
We have

(3.51)

v_{ij}^{\prime}-v_{ij}=-\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{i})}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{j})}(x_{i}-x_{j})+\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{i})}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{j})}(x_{i}-y_{j})\\
+\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{i})}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{j})}(x_{j}-y_{i})-\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{i})}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{j})}(y_{i}-y_{j}).

Letting
f\coloneqq\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{i})}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{j})}
and
h=f(y_{i}-\cdot)-f(x_{i}-\cdot)
, we can rewrite the above as

v_{ij}^{\prime}-v_{ij}=h(x_{j})-h(y_{j})

hence

(3.52)

|v_{ij}^{\prime}-v_{ij}|\leq|x_{j}-y_{j}|\|\nabla h\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq|x_{j}-y_{j}||x_{i}-y_{i}|\|D^{2}f\|_{L^{\infty}}.

But
f
is at least as regular as
\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\max(\tau_{i},\tau_{j}))}
, while by Lemma
3.1
and scaling, we have

|D^{2}\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\max(\tau_{i},\tau_{j}))}|\leq\frac{C}{\max(\tau_{i},\tau_{j})^{2}}.

Inserting into (
3.52
), the result (
3.49
) follows. The relation (
3.48
)
is then simply a combination of (
3.49
) and (
3.32
), absorbing
\frac{1}{\max(\tau_{i},\tau_{j})^{2}}
into
\frac{1}{d_{ij}^{2}}
by the above remark.
∎

Lemma 3.10

(Errors from the upper bound)
.

Let
x_{i},x_{j},y_{i},y_{j}\in\Lambda,\tau>0
such that
\tau\geq 8\max(r_{i},\lambda)
for all
i
and
d_{ij}\geq\min(r_{i},r_{j})
.
Let
v_{ij}
be as in Definition
3.6
and
\tilde{v}_{ij}
be as in (
3.18
).
There exists
C>0
(depending only on the regularization
\mathsf{g}_{1}
) such that

(3.53)

|\tilde{v}_{ij}|\leq C\frac{r_{i}r_{j}}{\max(d_{ij},\lambda)\max(r_{i},r_{j},d_{ij})}\mathbf{1}_{d_{ij}\leq 2\tau}.

Moreover,

(3.54)

|\tilde{v}_{ij}-v_{ij}|\leq C\frac{r_{i}r_{j}}{\max(d_{ij},\lambda)\max(r_{i},r_{j},d_{ij})}\mathbf{1}_{d_{ij}>2\tau}+\frac{r_{i}r_{j}}{\tau^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{d_{ij}\leq 2\tau}

Proof.

First let us observe that if
d_{ij}>2\tau
, then by property of the barycenter,
B(z_{i},\tau)\cap B(z_{j},\tau)=\emptyset
, hence
\tilde{v}_{ij}=0
and the claim is true by (
3.32
).
Let us turn to the case
d_{ij}\leq 2\tau
and distinguish two cases.

\bullet
Case 1:
d_{ij}\leq\frac{1}{2}\tau
.
In that case the balls
B(z_{i},3\max(r_{i},\lambda))
and
B(z_{j},3\max(r_{j},\lambda))
are included in
\Omega\coloneqq B(z_{i},\tau)\cap B(z_{j},\tau).

Integrating by parts and using (
3.16
), we have

\displaystyle\tilde{v}_{ij}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}=\int_{\partial\Omega}\frac{\partial u_{j}}{\partial\nu}(u_{i}-\bar{u}_{i})-2\pi\int_{\Omega}u_{i}\,\mathrm{d}(\delta_{x_{j}}^{(\lambda)}-\delta_{y_{j}}^{(\lambda)})

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\Omega}\frac{\partial u_{j}}{\partial\nu}(u_{i}-\bar{u}_{i})-2\pi\int_{\Omega}\tilde{u}_{i}\,\mathrm{d}(\delta_{x_{j}}^{(\lambda)}-\delta_{y_{j}}^{(\lambda)})+v_{ij},

where
\bar{u}_{i}
is the average of
u_{i}
on
\partial\Omega
.
Using the geometry of this case and (
3.21
), we may bound the first term on the right-hand side by
C\tau^{2}\|\nabla u_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial\Omega)}\|\nabla u_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial\Omega)}\leq\frac{r_{i}r_{j}}{\tau^{2}}
, and using (
3.20
), we may bound the second term by the same.
The result (
3.54
) follows in that case, and we then also deduce (
3.53
) from (
3.32
).

\bullet
Case 2:
d_{ij}\geq\frac{1}{2}\tau\geq 4\max(r_{i},r_{j},\lambda)
. In that case
B(z_{i},2\max(r_{i},\lambda))
and
B(z_{j},2\max(r_{j},\lambda))
are disjoint. We partition
\Omega
as
\Omega_{i}\cup\Omega_{j}
where

\Omega_{i}=\{x\in\Omega,|x-z_{i}|\leq|x-z_{j}|\},\quad\Omega_{j}=\{x\in\Omega,|x-z_{i}|>|x-z_{j}|\}.

Because
\Omega
is the intersection of two discs centered at
z_{i}
and
z_{j}
,
\partial\Omega_{i}
consists of two pieces:
\partial B(z_{i},\tau)\cap B(z_{j},\tau)
and a segment
S
, respectively the same for
\partial\Omega_{j}
with the same segment
S
.
The points of
S
must be at distance
\geq\frac{1}{2}d_{ij}\geq\frac{1}{4}\tau
from both
z_{i}
and
z_{j}
, thus
\nabla u_{i}
is bounded by
Cr_{i}/\tau^{2}
on
S
. The points of
\Omega_{i}
are also at distance
\geq\frac{1}{4}\tau
from
z_{j}
, so that
\nabla u_{j}
is bounded by
Cr_{j}/\tau^{2}
there.
We may now write, by integration by parts and (
3.16
), that

(3.55)

\int_{\Omega_{i}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}=\int_{S}\frac{\partial u_{i}}{\partial\nu}(u_{j}-\bar{u}_{j})-\int_{\Omega_{i}}(u_{j}-\bar{u}_{j})\Delta u_{i}

where
\bar{u}_{j}
is a constant.

Assume first that
\int_{\Omega_{i}}\Delta u_{i}=0
. By the definition of
\delta_{0}^{(\lambda)}
, this can only happen if both
B(x_{i},\lambda)
and
B(y_{i},\lambda)
do not intersect
\Omega_{i}
, or if both
B(x_{i},\lambda)
and
B(y_{i},\lambda)
are included in
\Omega_{i}
. In that case, we let
\bar{u}_{j}
be the average of
u_{j}
over
\Omega_{j}
. Using (
3.21
) and the property of
S
, we find that

(3.56)

\left|\int_{S}\frac{\partial u_{i}}{\partial\nu}(u_{j}-\bar{u}_{j})\right|\leq|S|\tau\sup_{S}|\nabla u_{i}|\sup_{\Omega_{j}}|\nabla u_{j}|\leq\frac{Cr_{i}r_{j}}{\tau^{2}}.

Moreover, by (
3.21
) and the above remark on
\Omega_{i}
,

\left|\int_{\Omega_{i}}(u_{j}-\bar{u}_{j})\Delta u_{i}\right|\leq Cr_{i}\|\nabla u_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{j})}\leq\frac{Cr_{i}r_{j}}{\tau^{2}}

in both the cases that we have to consider. Inserting into (
3.55
), we have shown that

(3.57)

\left|\int_{\Omega_{i}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\right|\leq\frac{Cr_{i}r_{j}}{\tau^{2}}

in the case
\int_{\Omega_{i}}\Delta u_{i}=0
.
In the case
\int_{\Omega_{i}}\Delta u_{i}\neq 0
, we note that in view of the structure of
\Delta u_{i}
, we may always split it as
\mu_{n}+\mu_{s}
where
\int_{\Omega_{i}}\mu_{n}=0
and
\mu_{s}
has a distinguished sign, with both
\mu_{n}
and
\mu_{s}
supported in
(B(x_{i},\lambda)\cup B(y_{i},\lambda))\cap\Omega_{i}
. We then let

(3.58)

\bar{u}_{j}=\frac{\int u_{j}\mu_{s}}{\int\mu_{s}}

and check that
|u_{j}-\bar{u}_{j}|\leq C\tau\|\nabla u_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{i})}\leq C\frac{r_{j}}{\tau}
.
The first term in the right-hand side of (
3.55
) is thus bounded as above, and for the second term, we have

\int_{\Omega_{i}}(u_{j}-\bar{u}_{j})\Delta u_{i}=\int_{\Omega_{i}}u_{j}\mu_{n}+\int_{\Omega_{i}}(u_{j}-\bar{u}_{j})\mu_{s}=\int_{\Omega_{i}}u_{j}\mu_{n}

where the second term vanished by (
3.58
).
We then use the structure of
\mu_{n}
and its neutrality to find that this is again bounded by
Cr_{i}\|\nabla u_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{i})}\leq C\frac{r_{i}r_{j}}{\tau^{2}}
.
We have thus obtained that (
3.57
) holds in the case
\int_{\Omega_{i}}\Delta u_{i}\neq 0
as well.
We may then bound
\int_{\Omega_{j}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}
in the same way, reversing the roles of
i
and
j
, and we conclude that

|\tilde{v}_{ij}|\leq C\frac{r_{i}r_{j}}{\tau^{2}}

in Case 2. But in that case we also have
|v_{ij}|\leq C\frac{r_{i}r_{j}}{\tau^{2}},
so (
3.54
) holds, and (
3.53
) is true as well.

∎

Definition 3.11

(Interaction of the energy upper bound model)
.

From now on, we let
\tilde{v}_{ij}
be as in (
3.18
) with the choice

(3.59)

\tau=8\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}

where
\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}
is defined in Definition
2.17
, and
\varepsilon_{0}
is a small positive constant to be determined (in Definition
4.10
).
With that definition, we get from Lemma
3.4
the existence of a constant
L>0
depending only on
\beta
(and the norms of the regularization
\mathsf{g}_{1}
) such that

(3.60)

\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})\leq-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-y_{\sigma_{N}(i)})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\neq j}\tilde{v}_{ij}+\frac{L}{\beta}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{|x_{i}-y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}|^{2}}{(\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda})^{2}}\right).

4.
Large deviations lower bound

Now that we move to the probabilistic context, we need more details about the stable matching.

4.1.
Stable matching

We provide more details on the stable matching alluded to in the introduction. Recall that a matching is a random permutation of
[N]
, i.e. a way to assign to each
x_{i}
a match
y_{\sigma(i)}
. A matching is stable with respect to an ordering of preferences if there does not exist any pair of points for which it would be preferable to switch partners.

We let
\Sigma_{N}
denote the set of permutations on
[N]
.

Definition 4.1
.

Let us introduce a random matching to pair particles of opposite sign. We define a random variable
\sigma_{N}
from
(\Lambda^{2N},\mathcal{B}(\Lambda^{2N}))
to
\Sigma_{N}
using the following procedure:

•

Enlarge all points
x_{i},1\leq i\leq N
into
B(x_{i},\varepsilon)
for the same parameter
\varepsilon>0
.

•

Increase
\varepsilon
until some
B(x_{i},\varepsilon)
touches some negative charge
y_{j}
. Set
\sigma_{N}(i)=j
. (If it touches more than one point at the same
\varepsilon
, choose the smallest index).

•

Remove
x_{i}
and
y_{j}
from the configuration and repeat.

Almost surely, this procedure defines a permutation of
[N]
, which we sometimes denote
\sigma_{N}[\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N}]
.

Remark 4.2

(Uniqueness of the stable matching)
.

When preferences are ordered by (minimal) distances and are strict, there is a unique stable matching algorithm, which is given by Definition
4.1
. This algorithm is a special case of the Gale-Shapley algorithm.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})
be a point configuration in
\Lambda^{2N}
such that the distances
|x_{i}-y_{j}|
,
1\leq i,j\leq N
are all distinct. Then the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

\sigma_{N}[\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N}]=\mathrm{Id}
,

(2)

For every
1\leq i\neq j\leq N
,
\sigma_{2}[x_{i},x_{j},y_{i},y_{j}]=\mathrm{Id}_{\{i,j\}}
.

The above lemma means that
\sigma_{N}
is equal to the identity if and only if there is no “blocking pair”, i.e., if and only if for every pair of couples
1\leq i,j\leq N
with
i\neq j
,
x_{i}
is assigned to
y_{i}
and
x_{j}
is assigned to
y_{j}
when performing the stable matching between
x_{i},x_{j}
and
y_{i},y_{j}
only.

Definition 4.4
.

For every
i,j\in[N]
with
i\neq j
, we denote

(4.1)

\mathcal{A}_{ij}\coloneqq\{(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})\in\Lambda^{2N}:\sigma_{2}[x_{i},x_{j},y_{i},y_{j}]=\mathrm{Id}_{\{i,j\}}\}.

Proof of Lemma
4.3
.

We first prove that (1) implies (2). Let
(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})
such that
\sigma_{N}[\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N}]=\mathrm{Id}
. Let
i_{1}
be the index of the first ball
B(x_{i},\varepsilon)
that touches a negative charge. Then

(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})\in\bigcap_{j:j\neq i_{1}}\mathcal{A}_{i_{1}j}.

Now let
i_{2}
be the index of the second ball to touch. We have similarly

(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})\in\bigcap_{j:j\neq i_{2}}\mathcal{A}_{i_{2}j}.

Iterating until the last ball touches a negative charge shows that

(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})\in\bigcap_{i\neq j}\mathcal{A}_{ij}.

We now prove that (2) implies (1). Let
(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})\in\cap_{i\neq j}\mathcal{A}_{ij}
. Let
i_{1}
be the index of the first ball which touches. Then
\sigma_{N}[\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N}](i_{1})=i_{1}
because
(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})\in\cap_{j:j\neq i_{1}}\mathcal{A}_{i_{1},j}
. Let
i_{2}
be the index of the second ball which touches. Then
\sigma_{N}[\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N}](i_{2})=i_{2}
because
(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})\in\cap_{j:j\neq i_{1},i_{2}}\mathcal{A}_{i_{2},j}
. We iterate the argument until the last ball touches a negative point.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
r_{i}\coloneqq|x_{i}-y_{i}|,r_{j}\coloneqq|x_{j}-y_{j}|
. Suppose
r_{i}\leq r_{j}
. Then the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

\sigma_{2}(x_{i},y_{i},x_{j},y_{j})=\mathrm{Id}_{\{1,2\}},

(2)

|y_{j}-x_{i}|\geq r_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad|y_{i}-x_{j}|\geq r_{i}.

The proof is omitted.

4.2.
Abstract polymer system and resummation

We recall here the standard polymer expansion result, which will be useful later.

The following lemma gives a (formal) identity for the logarithm of a cluster expansion series.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
C
be a finite set with a symmetric, reflexive relation expressing the intersection of its elements. Let
K:C\to\mathbb{R}
. We say that
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in C
are connected if their connection graph
G(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
is connected. Let
\mathrm{I}
be the Ursell function defined in (
2.26
). We have

(4.2)

\log\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in C\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}K(X_{1})\ldots K(X_{n})=\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in C\\
\mathrm{connected}\end{subarray}}K(X_{1})\cdots K(X_{n})\mathrm{I}(G(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})),

as an identity between formal series in
K
.

Note that the equality holds as an identity between elements in
\mathbb{R}
if the series in the right-hand side of (
4.2
) converges absolutely.

The statement and the proof of Lemma
4.6
can be found in
[
Bau16
, Theorem 4.4]
.

Remark 4.7
.

In the grand canonical setting, the identity (
4.2
) assumes a more concise form.
As a concrete illustration, let

K:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})\longrightarrow\mathbb{R},\qquad K(\{i\})=0\quad\text{for every }i\in\mathbb{N},\quad K(\emptyset)=0

so that

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}V_{1},\ldots,V_{n}\subset[N]\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}K(V_{1})\ldots K(V_{n})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X\mathrm{partition}\\
\mathrm{of}\,[N]\end{subarray}}\prod_{S\in X}\tilde{K}(X),

where

\tilde{K}(X)=\begin{cases}K(X)&\text{if $|X|>1$}\\
0&\text{if $|X|\in\{0,1\}$}.\end{cases}

Then, regarding all sums below as formal power series, we have

\log\!\Biggl[\;\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{N}}{N!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X\mathrm{partition}\\
\mathrm{of}\,[N]\end{subarray}}\prod_{S\in X}K(S)\Biggr]=\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\frac{z^{N}}{N!}\,K\bigl([N]\bigr).

The identity above is an instance of the
exponential (or connected-components) formula
for exponential generating functions: whenever a class of labeled structures is built as a disjoint union of “components,” the exponential generating function (EGF) of all structures is the exponential of the EGF of the connected ones (see for instance
[
Joy81
, Thm. 7.2.1]
).

4.3.
Graph and partitions notions

We denote
G=(V,E)
for a graph
G
on
[N]
, with
V
denoting the vertices and
E
denoting the edges.
We will also denote
V(G)
for the vertices of
G
.

Given two graphs
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
, we will denote
G_{1}\cup G_{2}
the graph whose vertices, resp. edges, are the union of the vertices, resp. edges, of those of
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
.

Recall that we denote by
\mathcal{G}_{c}(V)
the set of collections of edges
E
on
V
such that
(V,E)
is connected. We also denote by
\mathcal{T}_{c}(V)
the set of collections of edges
E
on
V
such that
(V,E)
is a tree.

Definition 4.8

(More partition notions)
.

(1)

For every set
A
, we denote by
\mathbf{\Pi}(A)
the set of partitions of
A

and by
\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathrm{sub}}(A)
the set of subpartitions of
A
. Recall that
B\subset\mathcal{P}(A)
is a subpartition of
A
if there exists
C\subset A
such that
B
is a partition of
C
.

(2)

(Set of points) Let
X
be a subpartition of
[N]
. We denote

(4.3)

V_{X}=\bigcup_{S\in X}S.

(3)

Let
X
be a subpartition of
[N]
and
i\in V_{X}
. We denote by
[i]^{X}
the part
S\in X
such that
i\in S
.

(4)

(Set of edges between dipoles in distinct multipoles and in the same multipole). Let
X
be a subpartition of
[N]
. We let

(4.4)

\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(X)\coloneqq\bigcup_{S\neq S^{\prime}\in X}\bigcup_{i\in S,j\in S^{\prime}}\{ij\}.

Moreover, we let

(4.5)

\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{intra}}(X)\coloneqq\bigcup_{S\in X}\bigcup_{i\in S,j\in S:i<j}\{ij\}.

(5)

(Restriction of a subpartition). Let
X
be a subpartition of
[N]
. For each
E\subset\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(X)
, we let
\mathrm{Res}(X,E)
be the set of blocks
S\in X
such that there exists a vertex in
S
incident to an edge in
E
.

(6)

(Coarsening of a partition). Let
X
be a subpartition of
[N]
. Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
be disjoint subsets of
X
. We define the coarsening of
X
along
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
to be

(4.6)

\mathrm{Coa}_{X}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\coloneqq\left(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{n}\{V_{X_{i}}\}\right)\sqcup\bigsqcup_{S\in X,S\notin X_{1}\cup\cdots\cup X_{n}}\{S\},

Note that
\mathrm{Coa}_{X}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
is a partition of
V_{X}
. Each group
X_{i}
of blocks is replaced by a single block
\{V_{X_{i}}\}
and every other block of
X
is untouched, see Figure
3
below.

Figure 3
.
Left: the partition
X
with blocks
A,B,C,D,E
and
F
.
Let
X_{1}=\{A,B\}
and
X_{2}=\{C,D\}
.
Right: the coarsening
\mathrm{Coa}_{X}(X_{1},X_{2})
with blocks
A\cup B
,
C\cup D
,
E
, and
F
.

.

Definition 4.9

(More graph notions)
.

Let
X
be a subpartition of
[N]
.

(1)

(Quotient graph). Let
E\subset\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(X)
. We let
G=(V_{X},E)/X
be the graph given by
V(G)=X
and

SS^{\prime}\in E(G)\quad\text{if there exists $i\in S$ and $j\in S^{\prime}$ such that $ij\in E$}.

(2)

(Multigraph quotient) The
multigraph quotient

G^{\mathrm{multi}}
is the undirected multigraph with vertex set

V(G^{\mathrm{multi}})=X
and edge multiplicities

m_{E}(S,S^{\prime})\coloneqq\#\bigl\{\{ij\}\in E:\ i\in S,\ j\in S^{\prime}\bigr\},\qquad S\neq S^{\prime}\in X.

(3)

(
X
-connected-components). Let
E\subset\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(X)
. We call
X
-components the components of the augmented graph

(V_{X},E\cup\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{intra}}(X)).

(4)

(Connected graph relative to
X
). We define
\mathsf{E}^{X}
to be the set of
E\subset\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(X)
such that the augmented graph

\Bigr(V_{X},E\cup\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{intra}}(X)\Bigr)

is connected. If
E\in\mathsf{E}^{X}
, we say that
(V_{X},E)
is connected relative to
X
.

(5)

(Tree relative to
X
). We define
\mathsf{T}^{X}
to be the set of
E\in\mathsf{E}^{X}
such that
G\coloneqq(V_{X},E)/X
is a tree and such that for every edge
SS^{\prime}\in E(G)
, there exists a unique
i\in S
and a unique
j\in S^{\prime}
such that
ij\in E
.

(6)

Let
E\subset\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(X)
and let
S\in X
. We denote

\deg_{E}(S)\coloneqq\sum_{i\in S}\deg_{(V_{X},E)}(i).

Figure 4
.
A tree relative to
X\coloneqq\{S_{1},S_{2},S_{3},S_{4},S_{5}\}
. Notice that
T\in\mathsf{T}^{X}
implies that the quotient graph
(V_{X},T)/X
is a tree on
X
, but the converse is not true.

4.4.
Strategy for the lower bound

The aim of this section is to provide a lower bound on the partition function by performing a cluster expansion. We start by bounding the partition function from below by that of a simplified model, the lower bound model obtained by the energy upper bound of Lemma
3.4
.

We wish to rewrite the partition function of the lower-bound model in terms of a cluster expansion series, as on the left-hand side of (
4.2
). In order to obtain a formula for the logarithm as in (
4.2
), we need the cluster expansion series, i.e. the right-hand side of (
4.2
), to be absolutely convergent. For that, we expand only the interactions that correspond to distinct multipoles hence are “small enough”.

Restricting interactions to only those within each multipole defines what we call a hierarchical model. We then compute the error between our lower-bound model and the hierarchical model by considering the ratio of their partition functions, and expanding the interactions around
1
,
which amounts to performing a perturbative expansion around the hierarchical model. This is done after restricting to the event on which the stable matching
\sigma_{N}
is identity, the cardinality of each multipole is always smaller than
p
, the number of multipoles of cardinality
k\leq p
is fixed, and every dipole has length smaller than
\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}
where
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
is a small parameter.

Following the outline of the introduction, this provides a lower bound of the free energy in terms of a sum over graphs of the product of
activities
, presented in Lemma
4.16
.

The goal of the rest of the section is then to expand the logarithm of the cluster expansion series appearing in Lemma
4.16
. We define a
cluster
as a subpartition
X
of
[N]
, and the
size
of a cluster as the total number of points in the union of the elements of
X
. First, we aim to control the activity of clusters of bounded size, which is addressed in Section
4.7
. Suppose that
\beta\in(\beta_{p},\beta_{p+1}]
, where we recall that
\beta_{p}\coloneqq 4-\frac{2}{p}
. We show that the behavior of the activity of a cluster of size
k
undergoes a transition: if
k\leq p
, then typical clusters of size
k
have length scale
\lambda
, whereas if
k>p
, then typical clusters of size
k
have length scale
\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}
. We also control in Section
4.7
the error between the activities of the lower-bound model and the activities of the true model. The second main task of the section is to control the activity of large clusters in order to prove that the cluster expansion series in Lemma
4.16
is absolutely convergent, allowing us to apply the formula of Lemma
4.6
to its logarithm.

The second task is the most delicate. As we will see in Section
4.6
, the activity of a cluster
X
is defined as a sum over certain sets of edges
E
of an expectation under a certain measure of
\prod_{ij\in E}f_{ij}^{\tilde{v}}
, where the weights
f_{ij}^{\tilde{v}}
are the Mayer bonds and with
\tilde{v}=(\tilde{v}_{ij})_{i<j}
the interaction in the lower-bound model.
The ingredients that will be used were alluded to in the introduction:

•

cancellation
by parity that allows to reduce to Eulerian graphs

•

Penrose resummation
to rewrite an activity defined as a sum over connected graphs in terms of a sum over trees

•

a
peeling procedure
on Eulerian graphs to construct a spanning tree on which we can control the contributions of all edges.

With these ingredients, we complete the proof of the absolute convergence of the cluster expansion series in Section
4.18
. In Section
4.20
, we give a lower bound on the partition function of the true model in terms of the infimum of a multipole free energy. This will complete the proof of the lower bound in (
2.46
) and (
2.49
) in Theorem
2
.

4.5.
Definitions: hierarchical model and activities

For any
V\subset[N]
, we let
\Pi_{\mathrm{mult}}^{V}
be the partition of
V
into multipoles (computed among points in
V
only). When
V=[N]
, we let
\Pi_{\mathrm{mult}}\coloneqq\Pi_{\mathrm{mult}}^{V}
.
Let
X
be the value of
\Pi_{\mathrm{mult}}
. Recall that we say that
i
belongs to a
2k
-pole if
|[i]^{X}|=k
with
k\geq 2
or to a pure dipole if
|[i]^{X}|=1.

Definition 4.10

(Dipole measure)
.

Let
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
be a small fixed constant, the same as in Definition
3.11
.
The dipole measure is defined as the probability measure over
(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{2}
with density

(4.7)

\mathrm{d}\mu_{\beta,\lambda,\varepsilon_{0}}=\frac{1}{C_{\beta,\lambda,\varepsilon_{0}}N}e^{\beta\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x-y)}\mathbf{1}_{x,y\in\Lambda}\mathbf{1}_{|x-y|\leq\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y,

where
C_{\beta,\lambda,\varepsilon_{0}}
is the normalization constant

(4.8)

C_{\beta,\lambda,\varepsilon_{0}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{N}\int_{\Lambda^{2}}e^{\beta\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x-y)}\mathbf{1}_{|x-y|\leq\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
\beta\in(2,\infty)
and
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
. There exists
C>0
and
O
depending only on
\beta
such that

(4.9)

C_{\beta,\lambda,\varepsilon_{0}}=\lambda^{2-\beta}(\mathcal{Z}_{\beta}+O(\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda})^{-2})\quad\text{as}\ N\to\infty,

where
\mathcal{Z}_{\beta}
is as in (
2.17
).
Moreover, given
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, there exists
\lambda_{0}
depending on
\varepsilon_{0}
and
\beta
such that for
\lambda<\lambda_{0}
the following holds:

(4.10)

C_{\beta,\lambda,\varepsilon_{0}}\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{Z}_{\beta}\lambda^{2-\beta}

and

(4.11)

\frac{|C_{\beta,\lambda,\varepsilon_{0}}-C_{\beta,\lambda}|}{C_{\beta,\lambda}}\leq C(\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda})^{-2},

where
C_{\beta,\lambda}
is as in (
2.28
).

Proof.

First, it is straightforward to compute that

(4.12)

|C_{\beta,\lambda,\varepsilon_{0}}-C_{\beta,\lambda}|\leq C(\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda})^{2-\beta}

for
C>0
depending only on
\beta
.
Next, observe that

C_{\beta,\lambda}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{\beta\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(y)}V_{N}(y)\mathrm{d}y,

where

V_{N}(x)=\frac{1}{N}|\{x\in\Lambda:x+y\in\Lambda\}|.

Since
0\leq V_{N}\leq 1
, we thus get by dominated convergence that

(4.13)

\lim_{N\to\infty}C_{\beta,\lambda}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{\beta\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x)}\mathrm{d}x=\lambda^{2-\beta}\mathcal{Z}_{\beta}.

It follows in view of (
4.12
) that, as
N\to\infty
, we have

C_{\beta,\lambda,\varepsilon_{0}}=\lambda^{2-\beta}\left(\mathcal{Z}_{\beta}+O\left(\left(\frac{\lambda}{\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\right)^{\beta-2}\right)\right),

where
O
depends only on
\beta
.
Recall that

\Bigr(\frac{\lambda}{R}\Bigr)^{\beta-2}\leq R^{-2}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad R\leq R_{\beta,\lambda}.

Thus, since
\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}\leq R_{\beta,\lambda}
, we deduce (
4.9
), and (
4.10
) provided
\lambda
is small enough is then a direct consequence. The relation (
4.11
) is then also a direct consequence of (
4.9
), (
4.10
) and (
4.13
).
∎

We now introduce the multipole measure and partition function for our approximate model.

Definition 4.12

(Multipole measure)
.

For any subpartition
X
of
[N]
, define the probability measure on
(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{|V_{X}|}

(4.14)

\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{X}^{0,\varepsilon_{0}}=\frac{1}{\mathsf{M}^{0}_{\varepsilon_{0}}(X)}\prod_{S\in X}\Bigr(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{S}}\prod_{i,j\in S:i<j}e^{-\beta v_{ij}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{ij}}\Bigr)\prod_{i\in V_{X}}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\beta,\lambda,\varepsilon_{0}}(x_{i},y_{i}),

where we recall (
2.12
) and (
4.1
), and where
\mathsf{M}^{0}_{\varepsilon_{0}}(X)
is the normalization constant

(4.15)

\mathsf{M}^{0}_{\varepsilon_{0}}(X)\coloneqq\prod_{S\in X}\mathbb{E}_{(\mu_{\beta,\lambda,\varepsilon_{0}})^{\otimes|S|}}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{S}}\prod_{i,j\in S:i<j}e^{-\beta v_{ij}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{ij}}\right].

Let
L
be the positive constant in (
3.60
). For any subpartition
X
of
[N]
,
we let
\mathsf{P}_{X}^{-,\varepsilon_{0}}
be the probability measure

(4.16)

\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{X}^{-,\varepsilon_{0}}=\frac{1}{\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)}\prod_{S\in X}\Bigr(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{S}}\prod_{i,j\in S:i<j}e^{-\beta\tilde{v}_{ij}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{ij}}\Bigr)\prod_{i\in V_{X}}e^{-L\frac{|x_{i}-y_{i}|^{2}}{(\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda})^{2}}}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\beta,\lambda,\varepsilon_{0}}(x_{i},y_{i})

where
\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)
is the normalization constant

(4.17)

\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)\coloneqq\prod_{S\in X}\mathbb{E}_{(\mu_{\beta,\lambda,\varepsilon_{0}})^{\otimes|S|}}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{S}}\prod_{i,j\in S:i<j}e^{-\beta\tilde{v}_{ij}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{ij}}\prod_{i\in V_{X}}e^{-L\frac{|x_{i}-y_{i}|^{2}}{(\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda})^{2}}}\right].

Figure 5
.
Hierarchical model. Four multipoles are featured:
X=\{S_{1},S_{2},S_{3},S_{4}\}
. The hierarchical model is
P_{X}^{-}\coloneqq P_{S_{1}}^{-}\otimes P_{S_{2}}^{-}\otimes P_{S_{3}}^{-}\otimes P_{S_{4}}^{-}
. Only the interactions within each multipole are taken into account.

We introduce the corrections to the hierarchical multipole model approximation. First recall the notion of Mayer bond.

Definition 4.13

(Mayer bond)
.

Let
w=(w_{ij})
be a weight. We let

f_{ij}^{w}\coloneqq e^{-\beta w_{ij}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{ij}}-1.

Definition 4.14
.

Let
\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathrm{sub}}([N])
be as in Definition
4.8
. For every
X\in\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathrm{sub}}([N])
, recall
\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(X)
and
\mathrm{Coa}_{X}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
from Definition
4.8
, and
\mathsf{E}^{X}
as in Definition
4.9
. Let
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
.

Let
\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{0}:\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathrm{sub}}([N])\to\mathbb{R}
be defined for every
X\in\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathrm{sub}}([N])
by

(4.18)

\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{0}(X)\coloneqq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subset X\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\sum_{E_{1}\in\mathsf{E}^{X_{1}}}\cdots\sum_{E_{n}\in\mathsf{E}^{X_{n}}}\sum_{F\in\mathsf{E}^{\mathrm{Coa}_{X}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})}}\\
\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{P}_{X}^{0,\varepsilon_{0}}}\left[\prod_{ij\in E_{1}\cup\cdots\cup E_{n}}f^{v}_{ij}\prod_{ij\in\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(X_{1})\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(X_{n})}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}}\prod_{ij\in F}(-\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}})\right].

Let
\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}:\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathrm{sub}}([N])\to\mathbb{R}
be defined for every
X\in\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathrm{sub}}([N])
by

(4.19)

\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)\coloneqq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subset X\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\sum_{E_{1}\in\mathsf{E}^{X_{1}}}\cdots\sum_{E_{n}\in\mathsf{E}^{X_{n}}}\sum_{F\in\mathsf{E}^{\mathrm{Coa}_{X}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})}}\\
\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{P}_{X}^{-,\varepsilon_{0}}}\left[\prod_{ij\in E_{1}\cup\cdots\cup E_{n}}f^{\tilde{v}}_{ij}\prod_{ij\in\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(X_{1})\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(X_{n})}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}}\prod_{ij\in F}(-\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}})\right].

Notice that for
|X|\in\{0,1\}
,
\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{0}(X)=\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)=0
.

Remark 4.15
.

By the Möbius-inversion formula (see Lemma
4.55
), one can obtain a more compact expression for
\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)
. However, because the proof of the absolute convergence of the cluster-expansion series relies on the representation given in (
4.19
), we keep that expression for now.

4.6.
Perturbative expansion around a hierarchical model

We now perform the series of reductions announced in Section
4.4
.

For each
k\geq 1
, let us denote by
\mathcal{N}_{k}
the number of
2k
-poles as defined in Definition
2.2
, item (2). Let
n_{1},\ldots,n_{p(\beta)}
such that
n_{1}+2n_{2}+\cdots+p(\beta)n_{p(\beta)}=N
and such that

(4.20)

\forall k=2,\ldots,p(\beta),\quad n_{k}\leq\varepsilon_{0}^{-\alpha(\beta)}\lambda^{2(k-1)}N,

where

(4.21)

\alpha(\beta)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\frac{4-\beta}{2}&\text{if $\beta\in(2,4)$}\\
\frac{1}{2}&\text{if $\beta\geq 4$}.\end{cases}

Set

(4.22)

\mathcal{A}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{N}_{1}=n_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{N}_{p(\beta)}=n_{p(\beta)}\}\bigcap_{i=1}^{N}\{|x_{i}-y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}|\leq\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}\}.

Our aim is to give a lower bound on
\int_{\mathcal{A}}e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}}
based on the energy upper bound (
3.60
), restricting to events where there are no
k
-poles for
k
beyond
p(\beta)
, the number of
k
poles for
k\leq p(\beta)
is well-controlled, and the size of the dipoles does not exceed
\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}
. We start with the following cluster expansion form.

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
\beta\in(2,\infty)
and
p(\beta)
be as in Definition
2.7
. Let
n_{1},\ldots,n_{p(\beta)}
be such that
n_{1}+2n_{2}+\cdots+p(\beta)n_{p(\beta)}=N
and such that (
4.20
) holds, and let
\mathcal{A}
be as in (
4.22
).
Let
\pi
be a partition of
[N]
such that for every
k=1,\ldots,p(\beta)
,

(4.23)

|\{S\in\pi:|S|=k\}|=\begin{cases}n_{k}&\text{if}\ k\leq p(\beta)\\
0&\text{if}\ k\geq p(\beta)+1.\end{cases}

Recalling
\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}
from (
4.17
) and
\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}
from (
4.19
), we have

\int_{\mathcal{A}}\mathbf{1}_{\sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Id}}e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})}\mathrm{d}\vec{X}_{N}\mathrm{d}\vec{Y}_{N}\\
\geq\frac{N!}{1^{n_{1}}(2!)^{n_{2}}\cdots(p(\beta)!)^{n_{p(\beta)}}n_{1}!\cdots n_{p(\beta)}!}(C_{\beta,\lambda,\varepsilon_{0}}N)^{N}\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(\pi)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\pi)\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{1})\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{n}),

with the convention that for
n=0
, the sum over
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\pi)
disjoint is 1.

Proof.

For simplicity, let us write
p
for
p(\beta)
.

Step 1: fixing dipoles and multipoles.

Since particle labels are indistinguishable, one can first write

\int_{\mathcal{A}}e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}}=N!\int_{\mathcal{A}\cap\{\sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Id}\}}e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}},

where
\sigma_{N}
is the stable matching introduced in Definition
4.1
.
Let
\pi
be a partition of
[N]
satisfying (
4.23
).
Recall that the number of ways to partition
[N]
into
m
sets of cardinality
k_{1},\ldots,k_{m}
is given by

(4.24)

\frac{N!}{k_{1}!\cdots k_{m}!\prod_{i\geq 1}m_{i}!},

where for every
i\geq 1
,
m_{i}
stands for the number of sets of size
i
,
m=\sum_{i\geq 1}m_{i}
. The number of ways to choose the partition
\pi
is therefore given by

(4.25)

\frac{N!}{1^{n_{1}}(2!)^{n_{2}}\cdots(p!)^{n_{p}}n_{1}!\cdots n_{p}!}.

Therefore, recalling from the beginning of Section
4.5
that

\Pi_{\mathrm{mult}}
is the partition of
[N]
into multipoles (see Definition
2.2
for multipoles), we get

(4.26)

\int_{\mathcal{A}\cap\{\sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Id}\}}e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}}\\
=\frac{N!}{1^{n_{1}}(2!)^{n_{2}}\cdots(p!)^{n_{p}}n_{1}!\cdots n_{p}!}\int_{\Pi_{\mathrm{mult}}=\pi,\sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Id}}e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{|x_{i}-y_{i}|\leq\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\mathrm{d}\vec{X}_{N}\mathrm{d}\vec{Y}_{N}.

Step 2: rewriting the event
\{\Pi_{\mathrm{mult}}=\pi,\sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Id}\}
as an intersection of simpler events.

By Lemma
4.3
, one can write

(4.27)

\{\sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Id}\}=\bigcap_{i<j}\mathcal{A}_{ij},

where we recall from Definition
4.4
that

\mathcal{A}_{ij}
is the event where
x_{i}
is assigned to
y_{i}
and
x_{j}
to
y_{j}
when performing the stable matching
between
x_{i},y_{i}
and
x_{j},y_{j}
only.

Moreover, recalling
\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}
and
\mathcal{B}_{S}
from (
2.11
) and (
2.12
),

\{\Pi_{\mathrm{mult}}=\pi\}=\left\{\bigcap_{S\in\pi}\mathcal{B}_{S}\right\}\cap\left\{\bigcap_{S,S^{\prime}\in\pi:S\neq S^{\prime}}\bigcap_{i\in S,j\in S^{\prime}}\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}\right\}.

Indeed every connected component of
\Pi_{\mathrm{mult}}
is connected and there are no edges between distinct connected components. Therefore, in view of (
4.27
),

(4.28)

\begin{split}\{\Pi_{\mathrm{mult}}=\pi,\sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Id}\}&=\left\{\bigcap_{S\in\pi}\Bigr(\mathcal{B}_{S}\bigcap_{i\neq j\in S}\mathcal{A}_{ij}\Bigr)\right\}\cap\left\{\bigcap_{S\neq S^{\prime}\in\pi}\bigcap_{i\in S,j\in S^{\prime}}\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}\cap\mathcal{A}_{ij}\right\}.\end{split}

Step 3: incorporating the energy upper bound

Combining (
4.28
) and (
3.60
), and recalling the notation
\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(\pi)
from Definition
4.8
, we get

(4.29)

\displaystyle\int_{\Pi_{\mathrm{mult}}=\pi,\sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Id}}e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{|x_{i}-y_{i}|\leq\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\mathrm{d}\vec{X}_{N}\mathrm{d}\vec{Y}_{N}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\geq\int\Bigr(\prod_{ij\in\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(\pi)}e^{-\beta\tilde{v}_{ij}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{ij}}\Bigr)\prod_{ij\in\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(\pi)}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}}\prod_{S\in\pi}\Bigr(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{S}}\prod_{i,j\in S:i<j}e^{-\beta\tilde{v}_{ij}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{ij}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\prod_{i=1}^{N}e^{\beta\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-y_{i})-L(\frac{|x_{i}-y_{i}|}{\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}})^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{|x_{i}-y_{i}|\leq\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\mathrm{d}x_{i}\mathrm{d}y_{i}.

Step 4: expansion on interactions between dipoles in distinct multipoles

As explained in the introduction, our starting point is to expand the interaction between dipoles which do not belong to the same multipole. Expanding the product of the
1+f_{ij}^{\tilde{v}}
and recalling (
4.4
) and Definition
4.13
, we have

\prod_{ij\in\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(\pi)}e^{-\beta\tilde{v}_{ij}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{ij}}=\prod_{ij\in\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(\pi)}(1+f_{ij}^{\tilde{v}})=\sum_{E\subset\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(\pi)}\prod_{ij\in E}f_{ij}^{\tilde{v}}.

Inserting into (
4.29
), and letting
w=\tilde{v}
, it follows that

(4.30)

\displaystyle\int_{\Pi_{\mathrm{mult}}=\pi,\sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Id}}e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{|x_{i}-y_{i}|\leq\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\mathrm{d}\vec{X}_{N}\mathrm{d}\vec{Y}_{N}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\geq\sum_{E\subset\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(\pi)}\int\prod_{ij\in\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(\pi)}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}}\prod_{ij\in E}f_{ij}^{\tilde{v}}\prod_{S\in\pi}\Bigr(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{S}}\prod_{i,j\in S:i<j}e^{-\beta\tilde{v}_{ij}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{ij}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\prod_{i=1}^{N}e^{\beta\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-y_{i})-L(\frac{|x_{i}-y_{i}|}{\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}})^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{|x_{i}-y_{i}|\leq\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\mathrm{d}x_{i}\mathrm{d}y_{i}.

Step 5: summing according to connected components relative to
\pi
of
([N],E)

Next, we rewrite the above sum according to the connected components of the augmented graph (as in Definition
4.9
)

\Bigr([N],E\cup\bigcup_{S\in\pi}\prod_{i,j\in S:i<j}\{ij\}\Bigr)

that contain at least two multipoles.
This yields

(4.31)

\sum_{E\subset\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(\pi)}\prod_{ij\in E}f_{ij}^{\tilde{v}}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subset\pi\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\sum_{E_{1}\in\mathsf{E}^{X_{1}},\ldots,E_{n}\in\mathsf{E}^{X_{n}}}\prod_{l=1}^{n}\prod_{ij\in E_{l}}f_{ij}^{\tilde{v}}.

Notice that for
n=0
, the product over
l\in[n]
of the sum over
X_{l}\subset\pi
is empty, hence the contribution equals
1
. Notice that if
|X_{i}|=1
, the sum over
E_{i}\in\mathsf{E}^{X_{i}}
is zero. Hence, one can keep in mind that the sum in (
4.31
) can be restricted to
|X_{i}|\geq 2
.

Thus,

(4.32)

\displaystyle\int_{\Pi_{\mathrm{mult}}=\pi,\sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Id}}e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{|x_{i}-y_{i}|\leq\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\mathrm{d}\vec{X}_{N}\mathrm{d}\vec{Y}_{N}\geq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subset\pi\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\sum_{E_{1}\in\mathsf{E}^{X_{1}},\ldots,E_{n}\in\mathsf{E}^{X_{n}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\int\prod_{ij\in\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(\pi)}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}}\prod_{ij\in E_{1}\cup\cdots\cup E_{n}}f_{ij}^{\tilde{v}}\prod_{S\in\pi}\Bigr(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{S}}\prod_{i,j\in S:i<j}e^{-\beta\tilde{v}_{ij}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{ij}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\prod_{i=1}^{N}e^{\beta\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-y_{i})-L(\frac{|x_{i}-y_{i}|}{\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}})^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{|x_{i}-y_{i}|\leq\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\mathrm{d}x_{i}\mathrm{d}y_{i}.

Step 6: expanding the weights
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}}
over disjoint connected components

Fix
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subset\pi
which contains at least two multipoles and suppose that
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
are disjoint. In order to have a multiplicative activity, we need to expand the product of the
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}}
. To be able to control the products of the Mayer bonds
f_{ij}^{\tilde{v}}
inside each connected component
X_{l}
, we keep the weights
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}}
for every edge
ij\in\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(X_{l})
and expand the rest:

\prod_{ij\in\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(\pi)}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}}=\left(\prod_{l=1}^{n}\prod_{ij\in\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(X_{l})}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}}\right)\prod_{ij\in\mathrm{Coa}_{\pi}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}}.

We now expand the second product in the above display by writing
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}}=1-\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}}
. This gives

\prod_{ij\in\mathrm{Coa}_{\pi}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}}=\sum_{F\subset\mathrm{Coa}_{\pi}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})}\prod_{ij\in F}(-\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}}).

Inserting this into (
4.32
) gives

(4.33)

\displaystyle\int_{\Pi_{\mathrm{mult}}=\pi,\sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Id}}e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{|x_{i}-y_{i}|\leq\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\mathrm{d}\vec{X}_{N}\mathrm{d}\vec{Y}_{N}

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subset\pi\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\sum_{E_{1}\in\mathsf{E}^{X_{1}},\ldots,E_{n}\in\mathsf{E}^{X_{n}}}\sum_{F\subset\mathrm{Coa}_{\pi}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})}I(E_{1},\ldots,E_{n},F),

where

I(E_{1},\ldots,E_{n},F)\coloneqq\int\prod_{ij\in\cup_{l=1}^{n}\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(X_{l})}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}}\prod_{ij\in F}(-\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}})\prod_{ij\in E_{1}\cup\cdots\cup E_{n}}f_{ij}^{\tilde{v}}\prod_{S\in\pi}\Bigr(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{S}}\prod_{i,j\in S:i<j}e^{-\beta\tilde{v}_{ij}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{ij}}\Bigr)\\
\times\prod_{i=1}^{N}e^{\beta\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-y_{i})-L(\frac{|x_{i}-y_{i}|}{\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}})^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{|x_{i}-y_{i}|\leq\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\mathrm{d}x_{i}\mathrm{d}y_{i}.

Step 7: resumming over the connected components relative to
\pi
of
([N],E\cup F)

One can then resum (
4.33
) according to the connected components of the graph

\Bigr([N],E_{1}\cup\cdots\cup E_{n}\cup F\cup\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{intra}}(\pi)\Bigr)

that contain at least two multipoles. Denoting the collection of these nontrivial connected components as
\mathrm{Comp}_{\geq 2}([N],E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}\cup F\cup\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{intra}}(\pi))
, this yields

(4.34)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subset\pi\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\sum_{E_{1}\in\mathsf{E}^{X_{1}},\ldots,E_{n}\in\mathsf{E}^{X_{n}}}\sum_{F\subset\mathrm{Coa}_{\pi}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})}I(E_{1},\ldots,E_{n},F)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{m!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,X_{m}^{\prime}\subset\pi\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subset\pi\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\sum_{E_{1}\in\mathsf{E}^{X_{1}},\ldots,E_{n}\in\mathsf{E}^{X_{n}}}\sum_{F\subset\mathrm{Coa}_{\pi}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{Comp}_{\geq 2}([N],E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}\cup F\cup\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{intra}}(\pi))=\{X_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,X_{m}^{\prime}\}}I(E_{1},\ldots,E_{n},F).

Notice that again, we dropped the condition
|X_{i}^{\prime}|\geq 2
since for
|X_{i}^{\prime}|\in\{0,1\}
, the sum is empty; hence the contribution is zero.

We will next sum over the connected components of the Mayer clusters (i.e. of the set of edges receiving the weight
f_{ij}^{\tilde{v}}
) and then over the set of edges with weight
-\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}}
.

Let
X_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,X_{m}^{\prime}\subset\pi
be disjoint. Summing over the connected components of the graph
([N],E_{1}\cup\cdots\cup E_{n}\cup\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{intra}}(\pi))
with at least two multipoles, we get

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subset\pi\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\sum_{E_{1}\in\mathsf{E}^{X_{1}},\ldots,E_{n}\in\mathsf{E}^{X_{n}}}\sum_{F\subset\mathrm{Coa}_{\pi}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})}\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{Comp}_{\geq 2}([N],(E_{l}),\cup F\cup\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{intra}}(\pi))=\{X_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,X_{m}^{\prime}\}}I((E_{l}),F)\\
=\prod_{r\in[m]}\left(\sum_{n_{r}=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n_{r}!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1,r},\ldots,X_{n_{r},r}\subset X_{r}^{\prime}\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\sum_{E_{1,r}\in\mathsf{E}^{X_{1,r}}}\ldots\sum_{E_{n_{r},r}\in\mathsf{E}^{X_{n_{r},r}}}\sum_{F\subset\mathrm{Coa}_{X_{r}^{\prime}}(X_{1,r},\ldots,X_{n_{r},r})}\right.\\
\left.\mathbf{1}_{E_{1,r}\cup\cdots\cup E_{n_{r},r}\cup F\in\mathsf{E}^{X_{r}^{\prime}}}I(E_{1,r},\ldots,E_{n_{r},r},F)\right).

Figure 6
.
Two connected components
X_{1}^{\prime}
and
X_{2}^{\prime}
of

([N],E\cup F\cup\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{intra}}(\pi))
. Each node represents a multipole, here assumed to be reduced to a single point (
\pi
partition into singletons). Solid lines are edges in
E
(Mayer bonds), dotted lines are edges in
F

(from the expansion
1-\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}}
).

Given
X_{1,r},\ldots,X_{n_{r},r}\subset X_{r}^{\prime}
disjoint and
F\subset\mathrm{Coa}_{X_{r}^{\prime}}(X_{1,r},\ldots,X_{n_{r},r})
, we have

E_{1,r}\cup\cdots\cup E_{n_{r},r}\cup F\in\mathsf{E}^{X_{r}^{\prime}}\Longleftrightarrow F\in\mathsf{E}^{\mathrm{Coa}_{X_{r}^{\prime}}(X_{1,r},\ldots,X_{n_{r},r})}.

Therefore,

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subset\pi\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\sum_{E_{1}\in\mathsf{E}^{X_{1}},\ldots,E_{n}\in\mathsf{E}^{X_{n}}}\sum_{F\subset\mathrm{Coa}_{\pi}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})}\\
\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{Comp}_{\geq 2}([N],E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}\cup F\cup\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{intra}}(\pi))=\{X_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,X_{m}^{\prime}\}}I(E_{1},\ldots,E_{n},F)\\
=\prod_{r\in[m]}\left(\sum_{n_{r}=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n_{r}!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1,r},\ldots,X_{n_{r},r}\subset X_{r}^{\prime}\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\sum_{E_{1,r}\in\mathsf{E}^{X_{1,r}}}\ldots\sum_{E_{n_{r},r}\in\mathsf{E}^{X_{n_{r},r}}}\sum_{F\in\mathsf{E}^{\mathrm{Coa}_{X_{r}^{\prime}}(X_{1,r},\ldots,X_{n_{r},r})}}I(E_{1,r},\ldots,E_{n_{r},r},F)\right).

Combining this with (
4.34
) and (
4.33
), we get

(4.35)

\displaystyle\int_{\Pi_{\mathrm{mult}}=\pi,\sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Id}}e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{|x_{i}-y_{i}|\leq\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\mathrm{d}\vec{X}_{N}\mathrm{d}\vec{Y}_{N}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\geq\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{m!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,X_{m}^{\prime}\subset\pi\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\int F(X_{1}^{\prime})\cdots F(X_{m}^{\prime})\prod_{S\in\pi}\Bigr(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{S}}\prod_{i,j\in S:i<j}e^{-\beta\tilde{v}_{ij}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{ij}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\prod_{i=1}^{N}e^{\beta\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-y_{i})-L(\frac{|x_{i}-y_{i}|}{\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}})^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{|x_{i}-y_{i}|\leq\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\mathrm{d}x_{i}\mathrm{d}y_{i},

where for every
X\subset\pi
,

F(X)\coloneqq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subset X\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\sum_{E_{1}\in\mathsf{E}^{X_{1}}}\cdots\sum_{E_{n}\in\mathsf{E}^{X_{n}}}\sum_{F\in\mathsf{E}^{\mathrm{Coa}_{X}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})}}\prod_{ij\in\cup_{l=1}^{n}E_{l}}f_{ij}^{\tilde{v}}\prod_{ij\in\cup_{l=1}^{n}\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(X_{l})}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}}\prod_{ij\in F}(-\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}}).

Step 8: conclusion

Inserting the last display into (
4.30
), dividing by the normalization constant
\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(\pi)
introduced in Definition
4.12
, and using independence over disjoint components to obtain the multiplicativity of
\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}
over disjoint connected components, we obtain from (
4.35
)

\frac{1}{(NC_{\beta,\lambda,\varepsilon_{0}})^{N}\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(\pi)}\int_{\Pi_{\mathrm{mult}}=\pi,\sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Id}}e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{|x_{i}-y_{i}|\leq\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\mathrm{d}\vec{X}_{N}\mathrm{d}\vec{Y}_{N}\\
\geq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subset\pi\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{1})\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{n}),

where
\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}
is as in Definition
4.14
. In view of (
4.26
), we have proved the result.

∎

4.7.
Control of activities for bounded size clusters and limiting activities, statements

Let us introduce a truncated version of the dipole activity of Definition
2.4
that will be used later in the proof.

Definition 4.17

(Truncated dipole activity)
.

For all
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
and
V\subset[N]
, recalling (
4.7
), we let

(4.36)

\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda,\varepsilon_{0}}^{\mathrm{dip}}(V)\coloneqq\sum_{E\in\mathcal{G}_{c}(V)}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\beta,\lambda,\varepsilon_{0}}^{\otimes|V|}}\left[\prod_{ij\in E}f_{ij}^{v}\right].

Notice that for
|V|\in\{0,1\}
, one has
\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda,\varepsilon_{0}}^{\mathrm{dip}}(V)=0
.

The following transition illustrates a transition that occurs at
\beta_{p}\coloneqq 4-\frac{2}{p}
. Suppose
\beta\in(\beta_{p},\beta_{p+1}]
and
k>p
. Then, clusters of cardinality
k
lead to activities whose leading order is governed by the long distances: the weight of the integral is carried by configurations of dipoles with a typical size
\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}
and at a distance of order
\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}
. In contrast, for
k\leq p
, the dominant contribution to the activity is given by dipoles of length scale
\lambda
separated by a distance of order
\lambda
.

Definition 4.18
.

Let
\beta\in(2,+\infty)
. Recalling (
2.30
),
we define

\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,k}=\begin{cases}\lambda^{2(k-1)}&\text{if }k\leq p^{*}(\beta),\\[4.0pt]
\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}^{-2}&\begin{aligned} &\text{if }k>p^{*}(\beta)\ \text{and}\ \beta\in\big(\beta_{p^{*}(\beta)},\beta_{p^{*}(\beta)+1}\big)\\
&\text{or }\beta=\beta_{p^{*}(\beta)+1}\ \text{and}\ k>p^{*}(\beta)+1,\end{aligned}\\[8.0pt]
\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}^{-2}\lvert\log\lambda\rvert&\text{if }k=p^{*}(\beta)+1\ \text{and}\ \beta=\beta_{p^{*}(\beta)+1}.\end{cases}

Proposition 4.19

(Control on the activity of bounded size clusters)
.

Let
\beta\in(2,+\infty)
. Let
\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,n}
be as in Definition
4.18
. Let
S\subset[N]
with
|S|>1
, and let
X
be a subpartition of
[N]
with
|X|>1
. Recall the activities
\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}
,
\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{0}
,
\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}
and
\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{0}
from Definitions
4.12
and
4.14
and
\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{dip}}
from Definition
2.4
.

There exists
C>0
depending only on
\beta
,
M
and
|S|
such that for
\mathsf{M}\in\{\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-},\mathsf{M}^{0}_{\varepsilon_{0}}\}
,

(4.37)

\mathsf{M}(S)\geq\frac{\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,|S|}}{CN^{|S|-1}},

(4.38)

\mathsf{M}(S)\leq C\frac{\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,|S|}}{N^{|S|-1}}.

Moreover, there exists
C>0
depending only on
\beta
,
M
,
\varepsilon_{0}
and
|V_{X}|
such that for
\mathsf{K}\in\{\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-},\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{0}\}
, we have

(4.39)

|\mathsf{K}(X)|\leq C\frac{\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,|V_{X}|}}{\mathsf{M}^{0}_{\varepsilon_{0}}(X)N^{|V_{X}|-1}}.

If
V\subset[N]
is such that
1<|V|\leq p(\beta)
, where
p(\beta)
is as in Definition
2.7
, then there exists
C>0
depending only on
\beta
,
M
and
|V|
such that

(4.40)

|\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{dip}}(V)|\leq C\frac{\lambda^{2(|V|-1)}}{N^{|V|-1}}.

Proposition 4.20

(Control of activity errors)
.

Let
\beta\in(2,+\infty)
. Let
p^{*}(\beta)
be as in (
2.30
) and
M
as in Definition
2.2
. Let
S\subset[N]
be such that
1<|S|\leq p^{*}(\beta)
. Let
X
be a subpartition of
[N]
such that
|V_{X}|\leq p^{*}(\beta)
and
|X|>1
.

There exists
C>0
depending on
\beta
,
M
,
\varepsilon_{0}
and
|S|
such that

(4.41)

|\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(S)-\mathsf{M}^{0}_{\infty}(S)|\leq\frac{C}{N^{|S|-1}}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}^{-2}.

Moreover, there exists
C>0
depending on
\beta
,
M
,
\varepsilon_{0}
and
|S|
such that

(4.42)

|\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda,\varepsilon_{0}}^{\mathrm{dip}}(S)-\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{dip}}(S)|\leq\frac{C}{N^{|S|-1}}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}^{-2}.

Besides, there exists
C>0
depending on
\beta
,
M
,
\varepsilon_{0}
and
|V_{X}|
such that

(4.43)

|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)-\mathsf{K}_{\infty}^{0}(X)|\leq\frac{C}{\mathsf{M}_{\infty}^{0}(X)N^{|V_{X}|-1}}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}^{-2}

and

(4.44)

|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{0}(X)-\mathsf{K}_{\infty}^{0}(X)|\leq\frac{C}{\mathsf{M}_{\infty}^{0}(X)N^{|V_{X}|-1}}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}^{-2}.

Proposition
4.19
and
4.20
are proved in Sections
4.12
and
4.13
.

Remark 4.21

(On activity sizes and assumption
4.20
)
.

Let
n_{1},\ldots,n_{p(\beta)}
be such that
n_{1}+2n_{2}+\cdots+p(\beta)n_{p(\beta)}>p(\beta)+1
and set
k_{0}\coloneqq n_{1}+\cdots+n_{p(\beta)}
. Suppose that
X
has
n_{i}
multipoles of cardinality
i
for every
i\in[p(\beta)]
. Then as indicated by (
4.39
) and (
4.37
),

|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|\approx\frac{\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}^{-2}}{N^{k_{0}-1}}\prod_{k=1}^{p(\beta)}\frac{1}{\lambda^{2(k-1)n_{k}}}.

If we do not impose (
4.20
), then the number of choices of
X
with
\#_{i}(X)=n_{i}
for all
i
is at most
\binom{|\pi|}{k_{0}}\leq N^{k_{0}}/k_{0}!
, hence

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):\\
\forall i,\#_{i}(X)=n_{i}\end{subarray}}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|\lesssim N\frac{\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}^{-2}}{\lambda^{2(k-1)n_{k}}}.

This is too large to conclude, since in Theorems
1
and
2
the contribution of clusters strictly larger than
p(\beta)
must be
O(N\delta_{\beta,\lambda})
.
This explains the necessity of assumption (
4.20
).
