\mathcal{E}_{1}(s,t)\coloneqq\{j\in V_{X}:r_{i}\leq r_{j},s<r_{j}<2s,\max(Mr_{i},r_{j})\leq d_{ij}\leq 16\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda},t<d_{ij}<2t\}.

\mathcal{N}(s,t)\coloneqq|\mathcal{E}_{1}(s,t)|.

Note that in view of the constraints
d_{ij}\in(Mr_{i},16\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda})
and
r_{j}\in[r_{i},\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}]
, the function
\mathcal{N}(s,t)
vanishes identically for
2t\leq\max(Mr_{i},s)
, for
t\geq 16\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}
, and for
s\leq\frac{r_{i}}{2}
.

We can give a geometric bound on
\mathcal{N}
:
assume that
j_{1},j_{2},\dots j_{q}
are
q
elements of
\mathcal{E}_{1}(s,t)
, which in addition belong to different multipoles. By the definition of multipoles, and since their sizes are larger than
s
, their distance must exceed
Ms
. Thus there are
q
balls of radius
(M-1)s
which are disjoint and with centers included in the ball centered at
x_{i}
and of outer radius
2t+r_{i}
. The sum of areas of these balls thus cannot exceed the area of the ball of radius
2t+r_{i}+Ms
which implies that

q((M-1)s)^{2}\leq(t+(M+1)s)^{2}
, unless
q=1
.

Since on the event
\mathcal{D}
, multipoles are of cardinality bounded by
p
, adding over different multipoles gives

(4.111)

\mathcal{N}(s,t)\leq C\left(\frac{t^{2}}{M^{2}s^{2}}+1\right)\mathbf{1}_{\frac{1}{2}\max(Mr_{i},s)\leq t\leq 16\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\mathbf{1}_{s\geq\tfrac{r_{i}}{2}}.

We may now write

\sum_{j\in V_{X}}\frac{r_{j}}{d_{ij}^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{\max(r_{j},Mr_{i})<d_{ij}<16\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\mathbf{1}_{r_{j}\geq r_{i}}\\
\leq C\sum_{j\in V_{X}}\mathbf{1}_{\max(r_{j},Mr_{i})<d_{ij}<16\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\mathbf{1}_{r_{j}\geq r_{i}}\iint\frac{s}{t^{2}}\frac{1}{ts}\mathbf{1}_{(t,2t)}(d_{ij})\mathbf{1}_{(s,2s)}(r_{j})\mathbf{1}_{\frac{1}{2}\max(Mr_{i},s)\leq t\leq 16\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\mathbf{1}_{s\geq\tfrac{r_{i}}{2}}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t.

Therefore, inverting the order of summation and integration gives

\sum_{j\in V_{X}}\frac{r_{j}}{d_{ij}^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{\max(r_{j},Mr_{i})<d_{ij}<16\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\mathbf{1}_{r_{j}\geq r_{i}}\leq C\iint\frac{1}{t^{3}}\mathcal{N}(s,t)\mathbf{1}_{\frac{1}{2}\max(Mr_{i},s)\leq t\leq 16\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\mathbf{1}_{s\geq\tfrac{r_{i}}{2}}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t.

Inserting the estimate (
4.111
), we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in V_{X}}\frac{r_{j}}{d_{ij}^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{\max(r_{j},Mr_{i})<d_{ij}<16\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\mathbf{1}_{r_{j}\geq r_{i}}

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{t=\tfrac{M}{2}r_{i}}^{16\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\int_{s=\tfrac{r_{i}}{2}}^{2t}\frac{1}{t^{3}}\left(\frac{t^{2}}{M^{2}s^{2}}+1\right)\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{M^{2}r_{i}}\log\frac{16\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}{r_{i}}+\frac{1}{Mr_{i}}\right).

Inserting into (
4.107
) yields

(4.112)

|A_{1}|\leq\frac{C}{M}\left(\log\frac{16\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}{r_{i}}+1\right).

We next turn to the right-hand side of (
4.108
). Notice that

A_{2}=\sum_{j\in V_{X}:j\neq i}\frac{r_{i}}{d_{ij}}\mathbf{1}_{Mr_{i}\leq d_{ij}\leq r_{j}}\mathbf{1}_{r_{j}\geq r_{i}}.

Define

\mathcal{E}_{2}(t)=\left\{j\in V_{X}:\max(t,Mr_{i})<d_{ij}<\min(2t,r_{j})\right\}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{N}(t)\coloneqq|\mathcal{E}_{2}(t)|.

We observe that if
j_{1},j_{2}\in\mathcal{E}_{2}(t)
, then by the triangle inequality and since
M>10
,

d_{j_{1}j_{2}}\leq 4t\leq 4\min(r_{j_{1}},r_{j_{2}})\leq M\min(r_{j_{1}},r_{j_{2}}).

Therefore,
j_{1}
and
j_{2}
are in the same multipole. Since on the event
\mathcal{D}
multipoles are of cardinality bounded by
p
, we deduce that
\mathcal{N}(t)\leq p
.

Applying the same reasoning as for
A_{1}
, we obtain

(4.113)

\begin{split}\sum_{j\in V_{X}}\frac{1}{d_{ij}}\mathbf{1}_{Mr_{i}\leq d_{ij}\leq r_{j}}\mathbf{1}_{r_{j}\geq r_{i}}&\leq C\sum_{j\in V_{X}}\mathbf{1}_{Mr_{i}\leq d_{ij}\leq r_{j}}\mathbf{1}_{r_{j}\geq r_{i}}\int_{\tfrac{M}{2}r_{i}}^{16\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\frac{1}{t^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{d_{ij}\in(t,2t)}\mathrm{d}t\\
&\leq C\int_{\tfrac{M}{2}r_{i}}^{16\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\frac{1}{t^{2}}\mathcal{N}(t)\mathrm{d}t\leq\frac{Cp}{Mr_{i}}.\end{split}

Inserting into (
4.108
), we obtain

|A_{2}|\leq\frac{C}{M}.

Combined with (
4.112
), this proves (
4.105
).

We finally prove (
4.106
). By definition (
4.66
), one may write

\sum_{j\in V_{X}:j\neq i}g_{ij}\mathbf{1}_{r_{j}\geq r_{i}}\leq 1+\sum_{j\in V_{X}:d_{ij}\geq r_{j}\geq r_{i}}\Bigr(\frac{r_{j}}{d_{ij}}\Bigr)^{2}\mathbf{1}_{d_{ij}\leq 16\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}}+\sum_{j\in V_{X}:r_{j}>d_{ij},r_{j}\geq r_{i}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}}.

Arguing as above, we deduce that

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in V_{X}:d_{ij}\geq r_{j}\geq r_{i}}\Bigr(\frac{r_{j}}{d_{ij}}\Bigr)^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}}\mathbf{1}_{d_{ij}\leq 16\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}

\displaystyle\leq C\int^{16\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}_{t=\frac{M}{2}r_{i}}\int_{s=\tfrac{r_{i}}{2}}^{2t}\frac{1}{ts}\frac{s^{2}}{t^{2}}\mathcal{N}(s,t)\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\int^{16\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}_{t=\frac{M}{2}r_{i}}\int_{s=\tfrac{r_{i}}{2}}^{2t}\frac{s}{t^{3}}\left(\frac{t^{2}}{M^{2}s^{2}}+1\right)\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\frac{1}{M^{2}}\log^{2}\Bigr(\frac{16\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}{r_{i}}\Bigr)+1\right).

On the other hand,

\sum_{j\in V_{X}:r_{j}>d_{ij},r_{j}\geq r_{i}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}}=\sum_{j\in V_{X}:r_{j}>d_{ij}\geq Mr_{i},r_{j}\geq r_{i}}1.

This proves (
4.106
).

∎

4.17.
Simplified bound on the activity

Observe that both the tree
T^{b}
in (
4.89
) and the trees
\tilde{T}^{b}
in each
\mathcal{L}_{C_{0}}(X_{l})
carry the weight
b_{ij}^{\mathrm{abs}}
. Thus, using the bound on the interaction provided by Lemma
4.51
and the result of Lemma
4.50
gives a much simpler bound on the activity, by summing first over the connected components of the Eulerian graph.

Lemma 4.52
.

Let
\beta\in(2,\infty)
.
Let
X
be a subpartition of
[N]
such that for every
S\in X
,
|S|\leq p(\beta)
, with
p(\beta)
as in Definition
2.7
. There exists
C>0
depending only on
\beta
and
M
and
C_{0}>0
depending only on
\beta
such that

|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|\leq\frac{e^{C|V_{X}|}}{(N\lambda^{(2-\beta)})^{|V_{X}|}\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{0}(X)}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subset X\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\prod_{l=1}^{n}\sum_{T_{l}^{a}\in\mathsf{T}^{X_{l}}}\sum_{T^{b}\in\mathsf{T}^{\mathrm{Coa}_{X}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})}}\mathcal{J}_{C_{0}}(\cup_{l=1}^{n}T_{l}^{a},T^{b}),

where for every
T_{1}^{a}\in\mathsf{T}^{X_{1}},\ldots,T_{n}^{a}\in\mathsf{T}^{X_{n}}
,
T^{b}\in\mathsf{T}^{\mathrm{Coa}_{X}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})}
,

(4.114)

\mathcal{J}_{C_{0}}(\cup_{l=1}^{n}T_{l}^{a},T^{b})\coloneqq\int_{(\Lambda^{2})^{|V_{X}|}}\prod_{ij\in T^{b}}b_{ij}^{\mathrm{abs}}\prod_{l=1}^{n}\Bigr(\prod_{S\in X_{l},S\neq\hat{S}_{l}}r_{S}^{2}\Bigr)\prod_{ij\in\cup_{l=1}^{n}T_{l}^{a}}\Bigr(\frac{1}{d_{ij}^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{d_{ij}\leq 16\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}}\Bigr)\\
\times\prod_{S\in X}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{S}}\prod_{i\in V_{X}}\Bigr(\Bigr(\frac{\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}{r_{i}}\Bigr)^{\frac{C_{0}}{M}}e^{\beta\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-y_{i})}\mathbf{1}_{|x_{i}-y_{i}|\leq\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\mathrm{d}x_{i}\mathrm{d}y_{i}\Bigr).

Proof.

By combining Lemmas
4.49
,
4.50
and
4.51
, there exists
C>0
depending only on
\beta
such that

(4.115)

|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|\leq C^{|V_{X}|}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subset X\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\sum_{T^{b}\in\mathsf{T}^{\mathrm{Coa}_{X}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})}}\\
\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{P}_{X}^{-,\varepsilon_{0}}}\left[\prod_{l=1}^{n}\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(X_{1})\cdots\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(X_{n})\prod_{ij\in T^{b}}b_{ij}^{\mathrm{abs}}\prod_{i\in V_{X}}\Bigr(\frac{\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}{r_{i}}\Bigr)^{\frac{C}{M}}\right],

where

\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(X^{\prime})\coloneqq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,X_{k}^{\prime}\subset X^{\prime}\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\sum_{T_{1}^{a}\in\mathsf{T}^{X_{1}^{\prime}}}\ldots\sum_{T_{k}^{a}\in\mathsf{T}^{X_{k}^{\prime}}}\sum_{\tilde{T}^{b}\in\mathsf{T}^{\mathrm{Coa}_{X^{\prime}}(X_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,X_{k}^{\prime})}}\\
\prod_{l=1}^{k}\prod_{S\in X_{l}^{\prime}:S\neq\hat{S}_{l}}r_{S}^{2}\prod_{ij\in T_{1}^{a}\cup\cdots\cup T_{k}^{a}}\frac{1}{d_{ij}^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{d_{ij}\leq 16\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}}\prod_{ij\in\tilde{T}^{b}}b_{ij}^{\mathrm{abs}}.

The expression (
4.115
) can be simplified by permuting the sum over
n
and the sum over
k
. Proceeding as in the proof of Lemma
4.16
(Step 7) yields

|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|\leq C^{|V_{X}|}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subset X\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\sum_{T_{1}^{a}\in\mathsf{T}^{X_{1}}}\ldots\sum_{T_{n}^{a}\in\mathsf{T}^{X_{n}}}\sum_{T^{b}\in\mathsf{T}^{\mathrm{Coa}_{X}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})}}\\
\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{P}_{X}^{-,\varepsilon_{0}}}\left[\prod_{ij\in T^{b}}b_{ij}^{\mathrm{abs}}\prod_{l=1}^{n}\prod_{S\in X_{l},S\neq\hat{S}_{l}}r_{S}^{2}\prod_{ij\in\cup_{l=1}^{n}T_{l}^{a}}\Bigr(\frac{1}{d_{ij}^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{d_{ij}\leq 16\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}}\Bigr)\prod_{i\in V_{X}}\Bigr(\frac{\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}}{r_{i}}\Bigr)^{\frac{C}{M}}\right].

Using the definition of
\mathsf{P}_{X}^{-,\varepsilon_{0}}
, this shows that there exists
C>0
depending only on
\beta
and
M
and
C_{0}>0
depending only on
\beta
such that

|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|\leq\frac{e^{C|V_{X}|}}{(N\lambda^{(2-\beta)})^{|V_{X}|}\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subset X\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\prod_{l=1}^{n}\sum_{T_{l}^{a}\in\mathsf{T}^{X_{l}}}\sum_{T^{b}\in\mathsf{T}^{\mathrm{Coa}_{X}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})}}\mathcal{J}_{C_{0}}(\cup_{l=1}^{n}T_{l}^{a},T^{b}),

Finally, applying Proposition
4.19
to
\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}
and
\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{0}
, there exists
C>0
depending on
\beta
and
M
such that

\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)\geq C^{-|V_{X}|}\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{0}(X).

Inserting this into the last display concludes the proof.
∎

In Section
A.5
, we prove the following lemma:

Lemma 4.53

(Integration and summation over trees)
.

Let
\beta\in(2,\infty)
and
p(\beta)
be as in Definition
2.7
. Let
X
be a subpartition of
[N]
such that for every
S\in X
,
|S|\leq p(\beta)
. Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subset X
be disjoint. For every
T_{1}^{a}\in\mathsf{T}^{X_{1}},\ldots,T_{n}^{a}\in\mathsf{T}^{X_{n}}
,
T^{b}\in\mathsf{T}^{\mathrm{Coa}_{X}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})}
, let
\mathcal{J}_{C_{0}}(\cup_{l=1}^{n}T_{l}^{a},T^{b})
be as in (
4.114
). Suppose that

|V_{X}|>\begin{cases}\frac{4}{4-\beta}&\text{if $\beta\in(2,4)$}\\
2p_{0}&\text{if $\beta\geq 4$}.\end{cases}

Then, for
M
large enough with respect to
\beta
and
p(\beta)
, and
\lambda
small enough with respect to
\beta,p(\beta)
and
M
, there exists a constant
C>0
depending on
\beta
,
M
and
p(\beta)
such that

(4.116)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T_{1}^{a}\in\mathsf{T}^{X_{1}},\ldots,T_{n}^{a}\in\mathsf{T}^{X_{n}}\\
T^{b}\in\mathsf{T}^{\mathrm{Coa}_{X}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{J}_{C_{0}}(\cup_{l=1}^{n}T_{l}^{a},T^{b})\\
\leq C^{|V_{X}|}N|\mathrm{Coa}_{X}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})|^{|\mathrm{Coa}_{X}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})|}\prod_{l=1}^{n}|X_{l}|^{|X_{l}|}\varepsilon_{0}^{2\alpha(\beta)|V_{X}|-2}\lambda^{(2-\beta)|V_{X}|}\delta_{\beta,\lambda},

where
\alpha(\beta)
is as in (
4.21
).

Let us briefly comment on the proof of Lemma
4.53
.
Suppose to simplify that
\beta\in(2,4)
and that
X
is made of pure dipoles only. Integrating the distances
d_{ij}
for
ij\in\cup_{l}T_{l}^{a}\cup T^{b}
reduces the problem to controlling

\int\frac{\prod_{i\in V_{X}}r_{i}^{2}}{\max_{j\in V_{X}}r_{j}^{2}}\Bigr(\frac{2\varepsilon_{0}R_{\beta,\lambda}}{r_{i}}\Bigr)^{\frac{C}{M}}\prod_{i\in V_{X}}e^{\beta\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(r_{i})}\mathbf{1}_{r_{i}\leq\varepsilon_{0}R_{\beta,\lambda}}\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{i}.

Switching to polar coordinates and denoting
i_{0}
the index of the largest
r_{i}
, we are led to integrating

\prod_{i\in V_{X}:i\neq i_{0}}\Bigr(r_{i}^{3-\beta}\Bigr(\frac{\varepsilon_{0}R_{\beta,\lambda}}{r_{i}}\Bigr)^{\frac{C}{M}}\mathbf{1}_{r_{i}\leq r_{i_{0}}}\Bigr)r_{i_{0}}^{1-\beta}\Bigr(\frac{2\varepsilon_{0}R_{\beta,\lambda}}{r_{i_{0}}}\Bigr)^{\frac{C}{M}}.

The point is that, since
\beta\in(2,4)
, we can take
M
large enough so that
3-\beta-\frac{C}{M}>-1
, forcing each
r_{i}
to concentrate at its upper limit, i.e.
\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}
. In turn, the “many-body interaction term”

\prod_{i\in V_{X}}\frac{2\varepsilon_{0}R_{\beta,\lambda}}{r_{i}}

disappears upon integration. This argument depends critically on the fact that we expand around the multipole model; otherwise, we could not make the exponent in front of that error arbitrarily small.

4.18.
Absolute convergence of the cluster series

We may now assemble all the above steps and sum over the number of multipoles of given cardinality. Let
\pi
be a partition of
[N]
and
X
a subpartition.
If
X
contains only pure dipoles, i.e. is a subpartition into singletons, then,

(4.117)

\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{0}(X)\geq C^{-|V_{X}|}N^{|V_{X}|},

for some constant
C>0
. Combining Lemma
4.52
and (
4.116
) yields that

|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|\leq C^{|V_{X}|}|V_{X}|^{|V_{X}|}\frac{1}{N^{|V_{X}|-1}}\varepsilon_{0}^{(4-\beta)|V_{X}|-2}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}^{-2}.

Therefore, supposing for simplicity that
\beta\in(2,4)
, we have

(4.118)

\sum_{X:|X|=|V_{X}|>\frac{4}{4-\beta}}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|=\sum_{k>\frac{4}{4-\beta}}\sum_{X\subset\pi:|X|=|V_{X}|=k}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|\leq\sum_{k>\frac{4}{4-\beta}}\Bigr(C^{k}k^{k}\frac{1}{N^{k-1}}\varepsilon_{0}^{(4-\beta)k}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}^{-2}\Bigr)\sum_{X\subset\pi:|X|=|V_{X}|=k}1.

Moreover, one can write

\sum_{X\subset\pi:|X|=|V_{X}|=k}1\leq\binom{|\pi|}{k}\leq\frac{|\pi|^{k}}{k!}\leq\frac{N^{k}}{k!}.

Inserting this into (
4.118
) and using Stirling’s formula, we deduce that for
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
small enough, the series in the right-hand side of (
4.118
) is absolutely convergent.

When
X
is a general partition with
n_{i}^{\prime}
multipoles of cardinality
i
for every
i=1,\ldots,p
, the lower bound (
4.117
) is replaced, in view of the estimate (
4.37
) of Proposition
4.19
, by

\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{0}(X)\geq\frac{1}{C^{|V_{X}|}}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\lambda^{2(i-1)n_{i}^{\prime}}N^{|X|}.

Combining this with (
4.89
) gives

|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|\lesssim C^{k}|X|^{|X|}\frac{1}{N^{|X|-1}}\varepsilon_{0}^{(4-\beta)k}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\lambda^{-|X|2(i-1)\gamma_{i}^{\prime}}.

By computing the number of ways to choose a subpartition
X
of
\pi
having
n_{1}^{\prime}
pure dipoles,
\ldots
,
n_{p}^{\prime}

2p
-poles, and using the crucial assumption (
4.54
), we deduce from the last display that, for
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
small enough, the cluster series converges absolutely.

Let us now prove this completely.

Lemma 4.54
.

Let
\beta\in(2,\infty)
. Let
\pi
be a partition of
[N]
such that for every
S\in\pi
, one has
|S|\leq p(\beta)
where
p(\beta)
is as in Definition
2.7
. For every
k\in[p(\beta)]
, let
n_{k}
be the number of parts of
\pi
of cardinality
k
. Assume that these
n_{k}
’s satisfy (
4.20
).

Then, for
M
large enough with respect to
\beta
and
p(\beta)
,
\varepsilon_{0}
small enough with respect to
\beta,M
and
p(\beta)
, and
\lambda
small enough, there exists
C>0
depending only on
\beta,M
,
p(\beta)
, and
\varepsilon_{0}
such that

(4.119)

\sum_{X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):|X|>1}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|20^{|X|}\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X}|>p(\beta)}\leq CN\delta_{\beta,\lambda}

where
\delta_{\beta,\lambda}
is as in (
2.34
).
In particular, the series
\sum_{X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi)}\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)
is convergent.

Moreover, for
\varepsilon_{0}
small enough, there exists
C>0
depending on
\beta,M
,
p(\beta)
and
\varepsilon_{0}
such that

(4.120)

\max_{S_{0}\in\pi}\sum_{X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):S_{0}\in X,|X|>1}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|20^{|X|}\leq C\Bigr(\lambda^{2}+\lambda^{2\frac{\beta-2}{4-\beta}-2(p^{*}(\beta)-1)}\mathbf{1}_{\beta\in(2,4)}\Bigr).

Proof.

Let
p\coloneqq p(\beta)
and

(4.121)

q_{0}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\frac{4}{4-\beta}&\text{if $\beta\in(2,4)$}\\
2p_{0}&\text{if $\beta\geq 4$}.\end{cases}

Step 1: summing over multipole sizes
.
Recall that for every supartition
X
of
[N]
and every
i\geq 1
,
\#_{i}(X)
stands for the number of blocks of cardinality
i
in
X
.
We begin by summing the cluster series (
4.119
) according to the number of multipoles of cardinality
i
, for every
i\in[p]
. We have

\begin{split}\sum_{X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):|V_{X}|>p}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,n_{p}^{\prime}:\\
n_{1}^{\prime}+2n_{2}^{\prime}+\cdots+pn_{p}^{\prime}>p\end{subarray}}\sum_{X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):\forall i\in[p],\#_{i}(X)=n_{i}^{\prime}}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|\\
&\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,n_{p}^{\prime}:\\
n_{1}^{\prime}+2n_{2}^{\prime}+\cdots+pn_{p}^{\prime}>p\end{subarray}}|\{X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):\forall i,\#_{i}(X)=n_{i}^{\prime}\}|\max_{X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):\forall i\in[p],\#_{i}(X)=n_{i}^{\prime}}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|.\end{split}

Fix
n_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,n_{p}^{\prime}\geq 0
such that

k\coloneqq n_{1}^{\prime}+2n_{2}^{\prime}+pn_{p}^{\prime}>p.

Set

k_{0}\coloneqq n_{1}^{\prime}+\cdots+n_{p}^{\prime}\leq k.

Since there are
n_{i}
multipoles of cardinality
i
in
\pi
and since
X
contains
n_{i}^{\prime}
multipoles of cardinality
i
, we have
\binom{n_{i}}{n_{i}^{\prime}}
choices for each
i\in[p]
, in other words,

|\{X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):\forall i\in[p],\#_{i}(X)=n_{i}^{\prime}\}|=\prod_{i=1}^{p}\binom{n_{i}}{n_{i}^{\prime}}.

For every
i\in[p]
, set
\gamma_{i}\coloneqq\frac{n_{i}}{N}
and
\gamma_{i}^{\prime}\coloneqq\frac{n_{i}^{\prime}}{k_{0}}
.
By the standard inequality
\binom{n}{m}\leq n^{m}/m!
and Stirling’s formula, we have

(4.122)

\binom{n}{m}\leq e^{Cm}\frac{n^{m}}{m^{m}}.

Hence,

\prod_{i=1}^{p}\binom{n_{i}}{n_{i}^{\prime}}\leq e^{Ck}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\frac{n_{i}^{n_{i}^{\prime}}}{(n_{i}^{\prime})^{n_{i}^{\prime}}}\leq e^{Ck}\Bigr(\frac{N}{k_{0}}\Bigr)^{k_{0}}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\Bigr(\frac{\gamma_{i}}{\gamma_{i}^{\prime}}\Bigr)^{\gamma_{i}^{\prime}k_{0}}.

Therefore,

(4.123)

\sum_{X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):\forall i\in[p],\#_{i}(X)=n_{i}^{\prime}}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|\leq e^{Ck}\Bigr(\frac{N}{k_{0}}\Bigr)^{k_{0}}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\Bigr(\frac{\gamma_{i}}{\gamma_{i}^{\prime}}\Bigr)^{\gamma_{i}^{\prime}k_{0}}\max_{X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):\forall i\in[p],\#_{i}(X)=n_{i}^{\prime}}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|.

Fix
X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi)
such that for every
i\in[p]
,
\#_{i}(X)=n_{i}^{\prime}
. Recall that in this setting
k=|V_{X}|
and
k_{0}=|X|
.

Step 2: summing over
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}

Suppose first that
k>q_{0}
, where
q_{0}
is as in (
4.121
).

Recall from Lemma
4.52
that there exists
C_{0}>0
depending on
\beta
and
C>0
depending only on
\beta
and
M
such that

(4.124)

|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|\leq\frac{e^{C|V_{X}|}}{(N\lambda^{(2-\beta)})^{|V_{X}|}\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{0}(X)}\\
\times\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subset X\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\sum_{T_{1}^{a}\in\mathsf{T}^{X_{1}},\ldots,T_{n}^{a}\in\mathsf{T}^{X_{n}}}\sum_{T^{b}\in\mathsf{T}^{\mathrm{Coa}_{X}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})}}\mathcal{J}_{C_{0}}(\cup_{l=1}^{n}T_{l}^{a},T^{b}),

where
\mathcal{I}
is controlled in Lemma
4.53
.

Observe that
|\mathrm{Coa}_{X}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})|=k_{0}-k_{0}^{\prime}+n
.
We now sum (
4.124
) according to the cardinality of
X_{1}\cup\dots\cup X_{n}\subset X
, which we denote by
k_{0}^{\prime}
(notice
n\leq k_{0}^{\prime}\leq k_{0}
). Choosing the elements of
\pi
that belong to
X_{1}\cup\dots\cup X_{n}
gives
\binom{k_{0}}{k_{0}^{\prime}}
choices.
Then, we partition these
k_{0}^{\prime}
elements into
X_{1}\cup\dots\cup X_{n}
and sum according to the cardinality
m_{1},\dots,m_{n}
of
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
. There are
\frac{(k_{0}^{\prime})!}{m_{1}!\dots m_{n}!}
ways to choose the
X_{i}
’s accordingly.

Thus, in view of (
4.116
), there exists a constant
C>0
depending only on
\beta
,
p(\beta)
and
M
such that

(4.125)

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subset X\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T_{1}^{a}\in\mathsf{T}^{X_{1}},\ldots,T_{n}^{a}\in\mathsf{T}^{X_{n}}\\
T^{b}\in\mathsf{T}^{\mathrm{Coa}_{X}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{J}_{C_{0}}(\cup_{l=1}^{n}T_{l}^{a},T^{b})\\
\leq C^{k}N\varepsilon_{0}^{2\alpha(\beta)|V_{X}|-2}\lambda^{(2-\beta)|V_{X}|}\delta_{\beta,\lambda}\\
\times\sum_{k_{0}^{\prime}=0}^{k_{0}}\binom{k_{0}}{k_{0}^{\prime}}\sum_{n=0}^{k_{0}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{m_{1}+\ldots+m_{n}=k_{0}^{\prime},m_{i}\geq 1}\frac{(k_{0}^{\prime})!}{m_{1}!\ldots m_{n}!}m_{1}^{m_{1}}\ldots m_{n}^{m_{n}}(k_{0}-k_{0}^{\prime}+n)^{k_{0}-k_{0}^{\prime}+n}.

By Stirling’s formula and since
m_{1}+\dots+m_{n}=k_{0}^{\prime}\leq k
, the factor

\frac{m_{1}^{m_{1}}\ldots m_{n}^{m_{n}}}{m_{1}!\ldots m_{n}!}
can be absorbed into
e^{Ck}
.
On the other hand, by the stars and bars theorem and (
4.122
), we have

\sum_{m_{1}+\ldots+m_{n}=k_{0}^{\prime},m_{i}\geq 1}1=\binom{k_{0}^{\prime}-1}{n-1}\leq e^{Ck_{0}}\frac{(k_{0}^{\prime})^{n}}{n^{n}}.

Therefore,

\sum_{k_{0}^{\prime}=0}^{k_{0}}\binom{k_{0}}{k_{0}^{\prime}}\sum_{n=0}^{k_{0}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{m_{1}+\ldots m_{n}=k_{0}^{\prime},m_{i}\geq 1}\frac{(k_{0}^{\prime})!}{m_{1}!\ldots m_{n}!}m_{1}^{m_{1}}\ldots m_{n}^{m_{n}}(k_{0}-k_{0}^{\prime}+n)^{k_{0}-k_{0}^{\prime}+n}\\
\leq e^{Ck}\sum_{k_{0}^{\prime}=0}^{k_{0}}\binom{k_{0}}{k_{0}^{\prime}}\sum_{n=0}^{k_{0}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{n!}(k_{0}^{\prime})!(k_{0}-k_{0}^{\prime}+n)^{k_{0}-k_{0}^{\prime}+n}\frac{(k_{0}^{\prime})^{n}}{n^{n}}.

By Stirling’s formula and since
k_{0}^{\prime}\leq k_{0}
and
k_{0}-k_{0}^{\prime}+n\leq k_{0}
,

\frac{1}{n!}(k_{0}^{\prime})!(k_{0}-k_{0}^{\prime}+n)^{k_{0}-k_{0}^{\prime}+n}\frac{(k_{0}^{\prime})^{n}}{n^{n}}\leq C^{k_{0}}\frac{1}{n^{2n}}k_{0}^{k_{0}+2n}.

It follows that

\sum_{n=0}^{k_{0}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{n!}(k_{0}^{\prime})!(k_{0}-k_{0}^{\prime}+n)^{k_{0}-k_{0}^{\prime}+n}\frac{(k_{0}^{\prime})^{n}}{n^{n}}\leq C^{k_{0}}k_{0}^{k_{0}}.

Thus

(4.126)

\sum_{k_{0}^{\prime}=0}^{k_{0}}\binom{k_{0}}{k_{0}^{\prime}}\sum_{n=0}^{k_{0}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{m_{1}+\ldots+m_{n}=k_{0}^{\prime},m_{i}\geq 1}\frac{(k_{0}^{\prime})!}{m_{1}!\ldots m_{n}!}m_{1}^{m_{1}}\ldots m_{n}^{m_{n}}(k_{0}-k_{0}^{\prime}+n)^{k_{0}-k_{0}^{\prime}+n}\\
\leq C^{k_{0}}k_{0}^{k_{0}}\sum_{k_{0}^{\prime}=0}^{k_{0}}\binom{k_{0}}{k_{0}^{\prime}}=(2C)^{k_{0}}k_{0}^{k_{0}}.

Inserting this into (
4.125
), and recalling that
k_{0}=|X|
, yields

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subset X\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T_{1}^{a}\in\mathsf{T}^{X_{1}},\ldots,T_{n}^{a}\in\mathsf{T}^{X_{n}}\\
T^{b}\in\mathsf{T}^{\mathrm{Coa}_{X}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{J}_{C_{0}}(\cup_{l=1}^{n}T_{l}^{a},T^{b})\leq C^{k}N\varepsilon_{0}^{2\alpha(\beta)|V_{X}|-2}\lambda^{(2-\beta)|V_{X}|}\delta_{\beta,\lambda}|X|^{|X|}.

Hence, by (
4.124
), if
k>q_{0}
, then there exists
C>0
depending on
\beta
,
M
, and
p(\beta)
such that

(4.127)

|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|\leq e^{Ck}\frac{N}{N^{k}\mathsf{M}^{0}_{\varepsilon_{0}}(X)}\varepsilon_{0}^{2\alpha(\beta)k-2}\delta_{\beta,\lambda}|X|^{|X|}.

On the other hand, by the estimate (
4.37
) of Proposition
4.19
, there exists
C>0
depending on
\beta
,
M
and
p(\beta)
such that for every
S\in X
,

\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{0}(S)\geq\frac{1}{CN^{|S|-1}}\lambda^{2(|S|-1)}.

Therefore, there exists
C>0
depending on
\beta
,
M
, and
p(\beta)
such that

\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{0}(X)=\prod_{S\in X}\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{0}(S)\geq\frac{1}{C^{|X|}N^{k-|X|}}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\lambda^{2(i-1)n_{i}^{\prime}}.

Inserting this into (
4.127
) and using
k_{0}=|X|
, there exists
C>0
depending only on
\beta
,
p(\beta)
and
M
such that

(4.128)

|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|\leq e^{Ck}\frac{N}{N^{k_{0}}}\varepsilon_{0}^{2\alpha(\beta)k-2}\delta_{\beta,\lambda}k_{0}^{k_{0}}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\lambda^{-2(i-1)n_{i}^{\prime}}.

Next, let us treat the case of small clusters
k\in\{p+1,\ldots,q_{0}\}
. Then, by Proposition
4.19
, there exists
C>0
depending on
\beta,p(\beta),M
and
\varepsilon_{0}
such that

|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|\leq e^{Ck}\frac{N}{N^{k}\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{0}(X)}\delta_{\beta,\lambda}.

Therefore, there exists
C>0
depending on
\beta
,
p(\beta)
,
M
and
\varepsilon_{0}
such that

(4.129)

|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|\leq e^{Ck}\frac{N}{N^{k_{0}}}\delta_{\beta,\lambda}k_{0}^{k_{0}}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\lambda^{-2(i-1)n_{i}^{\prime}}.

Step 3: proof of (
4.119
).

Inserting (
4.128
) into (
4.123
), and using
n_{i}^{\prime}=k_{0}\gamma_{i}^{\prime}
, we obtain that if
k>q_{0}
, then there exists
C>0
depending on
\beta,M
and
p(\beta)
such that

\sum_{X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):\forall i,\#_{i}(X)=n_{i}^{\prime}}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|20^{|X|}\leq e^{Ck}\varepsilon_{0}^{2\alpha(\beta)k-2}N\delta_{\beta,\lambda}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\Bigr(\frac{\gamma_{i}}{\gamma_{i}^{\prime}}\lambda^{-2(i-1)}\Bigr)^{\gamma_{i}^{\prime}k_{0}}.

By assumption (
4.20
), for every
i=2,\ldots,p
, we have
\gamma_{i}\leq\varepsilon_{0}^{-\alpha(\beta)}\lambda^{2(i-1)}
. Therefore,

\varepsilon_{0}^{2\alpha(\beta)k}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\Bigr(\frac{\gamma_{i}}{\gamma_{i}^{\prime}}\lambda^{-2(i-1)}\Bigr)^{\gamma_{i}^{\prime}k_{0}}\leq\varepsilon_{0}^{\alpha(\beta)k}\prod_{i=1}^{p}(\gamma_{i}^{\prime})^{-\gamma_{i}^{\prime}k_{0}}.

Moreover, by the convexity of
x\mapsto x\log x
and
\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}^{\prime}=1
, we check that

\prod_{i}(\gamma_{i}^{\prime})^{-\gamma_{i}^{\prime}k_{0}}\leq C^{k_{0}}
.

Thus, there exists
C>0
depending on
\beta
,
p(\beta)
and
M
such that for every
k>q_{0}
,

(4.130)

\sum_{X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):|V_{X}|=k}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|20^{|X|}\leq e^{Ck}\varepsilon_{0}^{\alpha(\beta)k-2}N\delta_{\beta,\lambda}.

Similarly, there exists
C>0
depending on
\beta
,
p(\beta)
,
M
and
\varepsilon_{0}
such that for every
k\in\{p+1,\ldots,q_{0}\}
,

(4.131)

\sum_{X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):|V_{X}|=k}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|20^{|X|}\leq e^{Ck}N\delta_{\beta,\lambda}.

Choosing
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
small enough, we see that the series (
4.130
) in
k
is convergent. Using this along with (
4.131
) concludes the proof of (
4.119
).

Step 4: proof of the anchored estimates.

Fix
S_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\pi)
and let
r\coloneqq|S_{0}|
.

Let
n_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,n_{p}^{\prime}\geq 0
with
n_{r}^{\prime}\geq 1
and set
k_{0}\coloneqq n_{1}^{\prime}+\cdots+n_{p}^{\prime}
. Suppose that
k\coloneqq n_{1}^{\prime}+2n_{2}^{\prime}+\cdots+pn_{p}^{\prime}>q_{0}
.
The number of ways to choose
X
with
n_{i}^{\prime}
multipoles of cardinality
i
for every
i\in[p]
, and such that
S_{0}\in X
, is given by

\binom{n_{r}-1}{n_{r}^{\prime}-1}\prod_{i\in[p]:i\neq r}\binom{n_{i}}{n_{i}^{\prime}}=\frac{n_{r}^{\prime}}{n_{r}}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\binom{n_{i}}{n_{i}^{\prime}}

By Stirling’s formula, there exists
C>0
such that

\frac{n_{r}^{\prime}}{n_{r}}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\binom{n_{i}}{n_{i}^{\prime}}\leq C^{k_{0}}\Bigr(\frac{n_{r}}{n_{r}^{\prime}}\Bigr)^{n_{r}^{\prime}-1}\prod_{i\in[p]:i\neq r}\Bigr(\frac{n_{i}}{n_{i}^{\prime}}\Bigr)^{n_{i}^{\prime}}.

Combining (
4.128
) and the above, there exists
C>0
depending on
\beta,p
and
M
such that

(4.132)

\sum_{X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):\forall i,\#_{i}(X)=n_{i}^{\prime},S_{0}\in X}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|20^{|X|}\\
\leq C^{k}\varepsilon_{0}^{2\alpha(\beta)k-2}N^{1-k_{0}}k_{0}^{k_{0}}\Bigr(\frac{n_{r}}{n_{r}^{\prime}\lambda^{2(r-1)}}\Bigr)^{n_{r}^{\prime}-1}\frac{1}{\lambda^{2(r-1)}}\prod_{i\in[p]:i\neq r}\Bigr(\frac{n_{i}}{n_{i}^{\prime}\lambda^{2(i-1)}}\Bigr)^{n_{i}^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta,\lambda}.

By (
4.20
), for every
i\in\{2,\ldots,p\}
,

n_{i}\leq N\varepsilon_{0}^{-\alpha(\beta)}\lambda^{2(i-1)}.

Inserting this into (
4.132
) and using that
n_{1}^{\prime}+\cdots+n_{p}^{\prime}=k_{0}
, we deduce that there exists
C>0
depending on
\beta,p
and
M
such that

\sum_{X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):\forall i,\#_{i}(X)=n_{i}^{\prime},S_{0}\in X}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|20^{|X|}\leq C^{k}\varepsilon_{0}^{\alpha(\beta)k-2}N^{1-k_{0}}N^{k_{0}-1}\frac{k_{0}^{k_{0}}}{\prod_{i\in[p]}(n_{i}^{\prime})^{n_{i}^{\prime}}}\frac{1}{\lambda^{2(r-1)}}\delta_{\beta,\lambda}.

Summing over the
n_{i}^{\prime}
, we get

\sum_{X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):|V_{X}|=k,S_{0}\in X}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|20^{|X|}\leq C^{k}\varepsilon_{0}^{\alpha(\beta)k-2}\frac{1}{\lambda^{2(r-1)}}\delta_{\beta,\lambda}.

For
\varepsilon_{0}
small enough, the above series in
k
is convergent. Therefore, there exists
C>0
depending on
\beta,p
,
M
and
\varepsilon_{0}
such that

(4.133)

\sum_{X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):|V_{X}|>q_{0},S_{0}\in X}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|20^{|X|}\leq C\frac{1}{\lambda^{2(r-1)}}\delta_{\beta,\lambda}.

Using the estimate (
4.129
), proceeding similarly we obtain that there exists
C>0
depending on
\beta,p
,
M
and
\varepsilon_{0}
such that

(4.134)

\sum_{X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):|V_{X}|>p,S_{0}\in X}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|20^{|X|}\leq C\frac{1}{\lambda^{2(r-1)}}\delta_{\beta,\lambda}.

Similarly, using the bounded cluster estimate of Proposition
4.19
for clusters smaller than
p^{*}(\beta)
, we get that there exists
C>0
depending on
\beta,p
,
M
and
\varepsilon_{0}
such that

(4.135)

\sum_{X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):|V_{X}|\leq p,S_{0}\in X,|X|>1}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|20^{|X|}\leq C\lambda^{2}.

Combining (
4.135
), (
4.134
) and (
4.133
) yields

\max_{S_{0}\in\pi}\sum_{X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):S_{0}\in X,|X|>1}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|20^{|X|}\leq C\Bigr(\lambda^{2}+\frac{1}{\lambda^{2(r-1)}}\delta_{\beta,\lambda}\Bigr).

For
\beta\in(2,4)
, we have

\frac{1}{\lambda^{2(r-1)}}R_{\beta,\lambda}^{-2}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda^{2(p^{*}(\beta)-1)}}R_{\beta,\lambda}^{-2}=\lambda^{2\frac{\beta-2}{4-\beta}-2(p^{*}(\beta)-1)}.

For
\beta\geq 4
, we have

\frac{1}{\lambda^{2(r-1)}}\lambda^{2p_{0}}\leq\lambda^{2}.

This concludes the proof of (
4.120
).
∎

We next prove the absolute convergence of the series (
4.56
) using the Kotecký-Preiss
[
KP86
]
anchored-norm criterion, following roughly the presentation of the proof of Theorem 4.9 in
[
Bau16
]
.

Proof of Proposition
4.23
.

Denote
p\coloneqq p(\beta)
.
In view of the previous lemma, it remains to prove (
4.56
).

Since
\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)=0
if
|X|\in\{0,1\}
, we have

(4.136)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subset\pi\\
\mathrm{connected}\\
\exists i,|V_{X_{i}}|>p\end{subarray}}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{1})\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{n})\mathrm{I}(G(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}))|\leq n\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subset\pi\\
\forall i,|X_{i}|>1\\
\mathrm{connected},|V_{X_{1}}|>p\end{subarray}}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{1})\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{n})\mathrm{I}(G(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}))|.

Then, by Rota’s theorem, (
4.88
), for every connected graph
G
on
[n]
, we have

|\mathrm{I}(G)|\leq\sum_{T\subset G}1,

where the sum runs over every spanning tree of
G
. Fix a spanning tree
T
of
G
. We have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subset\pi\\
\mathrm{connected},|V_{X_{1}}|>p\end{subarray}}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{1})\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{n})\mathrm{I}(G(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}))|

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\,\mathrm{tree}\\
\mathrm{on}\,[n]\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subset\pi\\
\mathrm{connected}\\
\forall i,|X_{i}|>1,|V_{X_{1}}|>p\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}_{T\subset G(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{1})\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{n})|

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\,\mathrm{tree}\\
\mathrm{on}\,[n]\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subset\pi\\
\mathrm{connected}\\
\forall i,|X_{i}|>1,|V_{X_{1}}|>p\end{subarray}}\prod_{ij\in T}\mathbf{1}_{X_{i}\cap X_{j}\neq\emptyset}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{1})\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{n})|.

We then sum according to the cardinality of the
X_{i}
’s:

(4.137)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\\
\mathrm{connected}\\
|V_{X_{1}}|>p\end{subarray}}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{1})\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{n})\mathrm{I}(G(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}))|\\
\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\ \mathrm{tree}\\
\mathrm{on}\,[n]\end{subarray}}\sum_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}\geq 2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}:\\
\forall i,|X_{i}|=k_{i}\\
|V_{X_{1}}|>p\end{subarray}}\prod_{ij\in T}\mathbf{1}_{X_{i}\cap X_{j}\neq\emptyset}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{1})\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{n})|.

Let
T
be a rooted tree on
[n]
with root equal to
1
. Let
k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}\geq 2
. Let
i_{1}\in[n]
be a leaf of
T
and let
p(i_{1})
be the unique neighbor of
i_{1}
in
T
. Since
i_{1}p(i_{1})\in T
, we have
X_{i_{1}}\cap X_{p(i_{1})}\neq\emptyset
. The only term in the right-hand side of (
4.137
) that depends on
X_{i_{1}}
is
\mathbf{1}_{X_{i_{1}}\cap X_{p(i_{1})}\neq\emptyset}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{i_{1}})|
. Summing it, we get

\sum_{X_{i_{1}}\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):|X_{i_{1}}|=k_{i_{1}}}\mathbf{1}_{X_{i_{1}}\cap X_{p(i_{1})}\neq\emptyset}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{i_{1}})|\leq\left(\sum_{S_{i_{1}}\in X_{p(i_{1})}}1\right)\sup_{S_{0}\in\pi}\sum_{X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):S_{0}\in X,|X|=k_{i_{1}}}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|.

Let us denote by
\mathrm{Anch}
the anchored quantity introduced in Lemma
4.54
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Anch}\coloneqq\sup_{S_{0}\in\pi}\sum_{X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):S_{0}\in X,|X|>1}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|20^{|X|}.

By the definition of
\mathrm{Anch}
, we have

\sup_{S_{0}\in\pi}\sum_{X:S_{0}\in X,|X|=k_{i_{1}}}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|\leq\mathrm{Anch}\times 20^{-k_{i_{1}}}.

Since there are at most
k_{p(i_{1})}
choices for an element in
X_{i_{1}}\cap X_{p(i_{1})}
(called the anchor at the vertex
i_{1}
), we get by combining the above displays that

\sum_{X_{i_{1}}:|X_{i_{1}}|=k_{i_{1}}}\mathbf{1}_{X_{i_{1}}\cap X_{p(i_{1})}\neq\emptyset}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{i_{1}})|\leq\mathrm{Anch}\times k_{p(i_{1})}20^{-k_{i_{1}}}.

We deduce that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}:\\
\forall i,|X_{i}|=k_{i}\\
|V_{X_{1}}|>p\end{subarray}}\prod_{ij\in T}\mathbf{1}_{X_{i}\cap X_{j}\neq\emptyset}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{1})\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{n})|\\
\leq\mathrm{Anch}\times k_{p(i_{1})}20^{-k_{i_{1}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(X_{i})_{i\in[n]\setminus\{i_{1}\}}:\\
\forall i,|X_{i}|=k_{i}\end{subarray}}\prod_{ij\in T\setminus\{i_{1}p(i_{1})\}}\mathbf{1}_{X_{i}\cap X_{j}\neq\emptyset}\prod_{i\in[n]\setminus\{i_{1}\}}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{i})|.

Choose a leaf
i_{2}
of the residual tree
T\setminus\{i_{1}p(i_{1})\}
and repeat the previous step. Iterating leaf removal, the tree eventually reduces to its root (the vertex
1
). We obtain

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}:\\
\forall i,|X_{i}|=k_{i}\\
|V_{X_{1}}|>p\end{subarray}}\prod_{ij\in T}\mathbf{1}_{X_{i}\cap X_{j}\neq\emptyset}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{1})\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{n})|\\
\leq\mathrm{Anch}^{n-1}\prod_{i\in[n]}k_{i}^{\deg_{T}(i)}\prod_{i\in[n]}20^{-k_{i}}\sum_{X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):|V_{X}|>p}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|20^{k_{i}}.

Inserting this into (
4.136
) gives

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\\
\mathrm{connected}\\
\exists i,|V_{X_{i}}|>p\end{subarray}}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{1})\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{n})\mathrm{I}(G(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}))|\\
\leq Nn\mathrm{Anch}^{n-1}\sum_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}\geq 2}\prod_{i=1}^{n}20^{-k_{i}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\ \mathrm{tree}\\
\mathrm{on}\,[n]\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}k_{i}^{\deg_{T}(i)}\sum_{X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):|V_{X}|>p}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|20^{k_{i}}.

By Cayley’s formula, the number of trees with degree sequence
(d_{1},\ldots,d_{n})
on a set of
n
points is given by
\frac{(n-2)!}{\prod_{i}(d_{i}-1)!}
. Therefore,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\ \mathrm{tree}\\
\mathrm{on}\,[n]\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}k_{i}^{\deg_{T}(i)}\leq\sum_{d_{1},\ldots,d_{n}}\frac{(n-2)!}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}(d_{i}-1)!}\prod_{i=1}^{n}k_{i}^{d_{i}}\leq(n-2)!\prod_{i=1}^{n}k_{i}e^{k_{i}}.

Combining the last two displays yields

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\\
\mathrm{connected}\\
\exists i,|V_{X_{i}}|>p\end{subarray}}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{1})\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{n})\mathrm{I}(G(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}))|\\
\leq nn!\mathrm{Anch}^{n-1}\sum_{X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):|V_{X}|>p}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|20^{k_{i}}\left(\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}e^{2k}20^{-k}\right)^{n}.

Hence,

(4.138)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\\
\mathrm{connected}\\
\exists i,|V_{X_{i}}|>p\end{subarray}}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{1})\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{n})\mathrm{I}(G(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}))|\\
\leq n!n\Bigr(\frac{1}{20e^{-2}-1}\Bigr)^{n}\mathrm{Anch}^{n-1}\sum_{X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):|V_{X}|>p}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|20^{k_{i}}.

By (
4.120
),

(4.139)

\mathrm{Anch}=O_{\beta,M,p,\varepsilon_{0}}\Bigr(\lambda^{2}+\lambda^{2\frac{\beta-2}{4-\beta}-2(p^{*}(\beta)-1)}\mathbf{1}_{\beta\in(2,4)}\Bigr).

For
\beta\in(2,4)
, by definition of
p^{*}(\beta)
(
2.30
) ,we have
2\frac{\beta-2}{4-\beta}-2(p^{*}(\beta)-1)>0
. Therefore, combining (
4.138
) and (
4.139
), we conclude that for
\lambda
small enough, there exists
C>0
depending on
\beta,M,p
and
\varepsilon_{0}
such that

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\\
\mathrm{connected}\\
\exists i,|V_{X_{i}}|>p\end{subarray}}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{1})\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{n})\mathrm{I}(G(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}))|\leq C\sum_{X\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):|V_{X}|>p}|\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X)|20^{k_{i}}.

Inserting (
4.119
) concludes the proof of (
4.56
).
∎

4.19.
Simplifying the multipole activity via Möbius inversion

Let us recall the standard Möbius inversion on the partition lattice; see
[
Sta11
, Prop. 3.7.1; Ex. 3.9.2, (3.37)]
for a modern exposition and
[
Rot64
]
for the original source.

Lemma 4.55

(Möbius inversion on the partition lattice)
.

Let
E
be a finite set. Let
g:\mathbf{\Pi}(E)\to\mathbb{R}
.

(1)

Define

f:\pi\in\mathbf{\Pi}(E)\mapsto\sum_{\sigma\leq\pi}g(\sigma),

where the sum is over refinements
\sigma
of
\pi
.
Then, for every
\sigma\in\mathbf{\Pi}(E)
, we have

g(\sigma)=\sum_{\pi\leq\sigma}\mu(\pi,\sigma)f(\pi),

where
\mu
is the Möbius function of the partition lattice defined as follows: if
\pi\leq\sigma
and for each block
B\in\sigma
, let
k_{B}
be the number of blocks of
\pi
contained in
B
. Then,

(4.140)

\mu(\pi,\sigma)=\prod_{B\in\sigma}(-1)^{k_{B}-1}(k_{B}-1)!.

If
\pi\nleq\sigma
, then
\mu(\pi,\sigma)=0
.

(2)

Define

f:\pi\in\mathbf{\Pi}(E)\mapsto\sum_{\sigma\geq\pi}g(\sigma),

where the sum is over coarsenings
\sigma
of
\pi
. Then, for every
\sigma\in\mathbf{\Pi}(E)
, we have

(4.141)

g(\sigma)=\sum_{\pi\geq\sigma}\mu(\pi,\sigma)f(\pi),

where
\mu
is as in (
4.140
).

As a corollary of Lemma
4.55
, we equate the activity
\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}
from Definition
2.12
with the activity
\mathsf{K}^{0}_{\infty}(X)
of Definition
4.14
.

Corollary 4.56
.

For every subpartition
X
of
[N]
, we have

\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X)=\mathsf{K}_{\infty}^{0}(X).

Proof.

Fix a partition
\pi
of
[N]
. Recall that
\mathbf{\Pi}(\pi)
stands for the set of
\pi
(into metablocks).

For every
P\in\mathbf{\Pi}(\pi)
with blocks
B_{1},\ldots,B_{k}
, we let
\tilde{\pi}^{P}
be the coarsening of
\pi
along
P
, i.e.
\tilde{\pi}^{P}=\mathrm{Coa}_{\pi}(B_{1},\ldots,B_{k})
. Note that
\tilde{\pi}^{P}
is a partition of
[N]
and that
\pi
is a refinement of
\tilde{\pi}^{P}
.

For every
P\in\mathbf{\Pi}(\pi)
, we set

f(P)=\frac{1}{(C_{\beta,\lambda}N)^{N}\mathsf{M}_{\infty}^{0}(\tilde{\pi}^{P})}\int_{\sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Id},\Pi^{\mathrm{mult}}=\tilde{\pi}^{P}}e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})}\mathrm{d}\vec{X}_{N}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{d}\vec{Y}_{N}.

Proceeding as in the proof of Lemma
4.16
shows that for every
P\in\mathbf{\Pi}(\pi)
,

(4.142)

f(P)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subset\tilde{\pi}^{P}\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\mathsf{K}_{\infty}^{0}(X_{1})\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\infty}^{0}(X_{n}).

Define

\tilde{\mathsf{K}}_{\infty}^{0}(X)=\begin{cases}{\mathsf{K}}_{\infty}^{0}(X)&\text{if $|X|\geq 2$}\\
1&\text{if $|X|=1$}.\end{cases}

One can rewrite (
4.142
) as

(4.143)

f(P)=\sum_{Q\geq P}g_{1}(Q),

where for every
Q\in\mathbf{\Pi}(\pi)
,

g_{1}(Q)\coloneqq\prod_{X\in Q}\tilde{\mathsf{K}}_{\infty}^{0}(X).

Let us now prove a second expansion of the form (
4.143
). Fix
P\in\mathbf{\Pi}(\pi)
. Recall that

\{\sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Id},\Pi^{\mathrm{mult}}=\tilde{\pi}^{P}\}=\bigcap_{i,j\in[N]:i\neq j}\mathcal{A}_{ij}\cap\bigcap_{S\in\tilde{\pi}^{P}}\mathcal{B}_{S}\bigcap_{ij\in\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(\tilde{\pi}^{P})}\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}.

Moreover, since
\mathcal{A}_{ij}\subset\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}
,

\{\sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Id},\Pi^{\mathrm{mult}}=\tilde{\pi}^{P}\}=\bigcap_{S\in\tilde{\pi}^{P}}\left(\mathcal{B}_{S}\bigcap_{i,j\in S:i\neq j}\mathcal{A}_{ij}\right)\bigcap_{ij\in\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(\tilde{\pi}^{P})}\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}.

It follows that

\int_{\sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Id},\Pi^{\mathrm{mult}}=\tilde{\pi}^{P}}e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})}\mathrm{d}\vec{X}_{N}\mathrm{d}\vec{Y}_{N}\\
=\int\prod_{ij\in\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(\tilde{\pi}^{P})}e^{-\beta v_{ij}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}}\prod_{S\in\tilde{\pi}^{P}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{S}}\prod_{i,j\in S:i\neq j}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{ij}}\prod_{i\in[N]}e^{\beta\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}(x_{i}-y_{i})}\mathrm{d}x_{i}\mathrm{d}y_{i}.

For every
ij\in\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(\pi)
, write
e^{-\beta v_{ij}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}}=1+(e^{-\beta v_{ij}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}}-1)
. Then, normalizing by the hierarchical multipole partition function, we obtain

f(P)=\sum_{E\subset\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{inter}}(\tilde{\pi}^{P})}\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{P}_{\beta,\lambda}^{X}}\left[\prod_{ij\in E}(e^{-\beta v_{ij}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{ij}^{c}}-1)\right].

Splitting according to the connected components of the augmented graph
([N],E\cup\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{intra}}(\tilde{\pi}^{P}))
yields

(4.144)

f(P)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\subset\tilde{\pi}^{P}\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X_{1})\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X_{n}).

Define

\tilde{\mathsf{K}}_{\beta,\lambda}(X)=\begin{cases}{\mathsf{K}}_{\beta,\lambda}(X)&\text{if $|X|\geq 2$}\\
1&\text{if $|X|=1$}.\end{cases}

Then, one can rewrite (
4.144
) as

(4.145)

f(P)=\sum_{Q\geq P}g_{2}(Q),

where for every
Q\in\mathbf{\Pi}(\pi)
,

g_{2}(Q)\coloneqq\prod_{X\in Q}\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X).

Since (
4.143
) and (
4.145
) hold for every
P\in\mathbf{\Pi}(\pi)
, we get by applying Möbius inversion (
4.141
) to
E=\pi
that

g_{1}\equiv g_{2}.

Let
X\subset\pi
. Taking
P
to be the partition of
\pi
with blocks
X
and blocks
\{S\}
for every
S\in\pi\setminus X
, we get

g_{1}(P)=\tilde{\mathsf{K}}_{\infty}^{0}(X)=\tilde{\mathsf{K}}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X)=g_{2}(P),

hence for every
|X|\geq 2
, we have
\mathsf{K}_{\infty}^{0}(X)=\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X)
. Recalling that for
|X|\in\{0,1\}
, we have
\mathsf{K}_{\infty}^{0}(X)=\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X)=0
, this concludes the proof of the corollary.
∎

4.20.
Proof of the LDP lower bound

We conclude the proof of the lower bound.

Proposition 4.57

(Large deviations lower bound)
.

Let
\beta\in(2,\infty)
and let
p(\beta)
be as in Definition
2.7
. Let
\mathcal{Z}_{\beta}
be as in (
2.17
) and
\mathcal{I}_{\beta,p(\beta),\lambda}
be as in (
2.14
). Let
n_{1},\ldots,n_{p(\beta)}\in\mathbb{N}
be such that

n_{1}+2n_{2}+\cdots+p(\beta)n_{p(\beta)}=N.

Assume that for every
k\in\{2,\ldots,p(\beta)\}
the assumption (
4.20
) holds.

For every
k\in\{1,\ldots,p(\beta)\},
let
\mathcal{N}_{k}
be the number of
2k
-poles in
[N]
. The event
\mathcal{A}
being as in (
4.22
), we have

(4.146)

\log\int_{\mathcal{A}}e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}}\mathrm{d}\vec{X}_{N}\mathrm{d}\vec{Y}_{N}\geq\log N!+(2-\beta)N\log\lambda+N\log\mathcal{Z}_{\beta}-N\mathcal{I}_{\beta,p(\beta),\lambda}(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{p(\beta)})\\
+O_{\beta,M,p_{0},\varepsilon_{0}}(N\delta_{\beta,\lambda}).

Proof.

Denote
p\coloneqq p(\beta)
.

Step 1: expanding the multipole terms

By Lemma
4.16
, we have

(4.147)

\int_{\mathcal{A}}\mathbf{1}_{\sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Id}}e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}}\geq\frac{N!}{1^{n_{1}}(2!)^{n_{2}}\cdots(p!)^{n_{p}}n_{1}!\cdots n_{p}!}(C_{\beta,\varepsilon_{0}}N)^{N}\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(\pi)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{1})\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{n}).

By Proposition
4.20
and Lemma
4.22
, for every
S\subset\pi
,

\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(S)=\frac{1}{N^{|S|-1}}\Bigr(\mathsf{m}_{\beta,\lambda}(|S|)+O_{\beta,M,p,\varepsilon_{0}}(\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}^{-2})\Bigr).

Using that
\mathsf{m}_{\beta,\lambda}(1)=1
, it follows that

\prod_{S\in\pi}\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(S)=\frac{N^{n_{1}+\ldots+n_{p}}}{N^{N}}\prod_{k=2}^{p}\mathsf{m}_{\beta,\lambda}(k)^{n_{k}}\prod_{k=2}^{p}e^{\frac{n_{k}}{\mathsf{m}_{\beta,\lambda(k)}}O_{\beta,M,p,\varepsilon_{0}}(\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}^{-2})}.

By the lower bound on the multipole partition function of Proposition
4.23
and the fact that
C_{\beta,\lambda}\leq C\lambda^{2-\beta}
, we get that for every
k\in\{1,\ldots,p\}
, there exists a constant
C>0
depending on
\beta
,
M
, and
p
such that

\mathsf{m}_{\beta,\lambda}(k)\geq\frac{1}{C}\lambda^{2(k-1)}.

Therefore, by the assumption (
4.20
),

(4.148)

\prod_{S\in\pi}\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(S)=\frac{N^{n_{1}+\ldots+n_{p}}}{N^{N}}\left(\prod_{k=2}^{p}\mathsf{m}_{\beta,\lambda}(k)^{n_{k}}\right)e^{O_{\beta,M,p,\varepsilon_{0}}(N\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}^{-2})}.

Combining (
4.147
) and (
4.148
), we obtain

\frac{1}{(NC_{\beta,\lambda,\varepsilon_{0}})^{N}}\int_{\mathcal{A}}\mathbf{1}_{\sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Id}}e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}}=\frac{N!}{1^{n_{1}}(2!)^{n_{2}}\cdots(p!)^{n_{p}}n_{1}!\cdots n_{p}!}\frac{N^{n_{1}+\cdots+n_{p}}}{N^{N}}\prod_{k=2}^{p}\mathsf{m}_{\beta,\lambda}(k)^{n_{k}}\\
\times\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\pi)\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{1})\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{n})e^{O_{\beta,M,p,\varepsilon_{0}}(N\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}^{-2})}.

Therefore, by Stirling’s formula,

(4.149)

\frac{1}{(NC_{\beta,\lambda,\varepsilon_{0}})^{N}}\int_{\mathcal{A}}\mathbf{1}_{\sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Id}}e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\lambda}}=e^{-N}\prod_{k=1}^{p}\Bigr(\frac{Ne}{k!n_{k}}\Bigr)^{n_{k}}\prod_{k=2}^{p}\mathsf{m}_{\beta,\lambda}(k)^{n_{k}}\\
\times\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\pi)\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{1})\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{n})e^{O_{\beta,M,p,\varepsilon_{0}}(N\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}^{-2})}.

In view of (
4.9
) and of Definition
2.14
, we will have proved the result once we show that

(4.150)

\log\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\pi)\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{1})\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{n})\\
=-N\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n!}\sum_{m_{1},\ldots,m_{p}\in\mathbb{N}}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\frac{\gamma_{i}^{m_{i}}}{m_{i}!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\in\mathrm{Htrees}_{n}(Y(m_{1},\ldots,m_{p}))\\
X_{1}\cup\cdots\cup X_{n}=Y(m_{1},\ldots,m_{p})\end{subarray}}\mathsf{k}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(\#X_{1})\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{1}}|\leq p}\cdots\mathsf{k}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(\#X_{n})\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{n}}|\leq p}\\
+O_{\beta,M,p,\varepsilon_{0}}\left(N\delta_{\beta,\lambda}\right),

with
\mathrm{Htrees}_{n}
defined in (
2.32
) and
Y(m_{1},\ldots,m_{p})
as in Definition
2.13
.

Step 2: expansion of the perturbative term

By Proposition
4.23
, the series

\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\pi)\\
\mathrm{connected}\end{subarray}}\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{1})\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{n})\mathrm{I}(G(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}))

is absolutely convergent, which allows one to write by Lemma
4.6
,

\log\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\pi)\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{1})\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{n})\\
=\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\pi)\\
\mathrm{connected}\end{subarray}}\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{1})\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{n})\mathrm{I}(G(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})).

Moreover, by (
4.56
),

\log\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\pi)\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{1})\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{n})\\
=\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\pi)\\
\mathrm{connected}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{1}}|\leq p}\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{1})\cdots\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{n}}|\leq p}\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{n})\mathrm{I}(G(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}))+O_{\beta,M,p,\varepsilon_{0}}(N\delta_{\beta,\lambda}).

Then, by Proposition
4.20
, one can check that

\log\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\pi)\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{1})\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{n})\\
=\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\pi)\\
\mathrm{connected}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{1}}|\leq p}\mathsf{K}_{\infty}^{0}(X_{1})\dots\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{n}}|\leq p}\mathsf{K}_{\infty}^{0}(X_{n})\mathrm{I}(G(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}))+O_{\beta,M,p,\varepsilon_{0}}\left(N\delta_{\beta,\lambda}\right).

Using Corollary
4.56
to equate
\mathsf{K}_{\infty}^{0}
with
\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}
, we thus get

(4.151)

\log\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\pi)\\
\mathrm{disjoint}\end{subarray}}\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{1})\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{-}(X_{n})\\
=\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\pi)\\
\mathrm{connected}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{1}}|\leq p}\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X_{1})\dots\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{n}}|\leq p}\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X_{n})\mathrm{I}(G(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}))+O_{\beta,M,p,\varepsilon_{0}}\left(N\delta_{\beta,\lambda}\right).

Step 3: summing over unlabeled quantities

First, we show that only hypertrees contribute an order
N
term in (
4.151
). Let
n\geq 1
and recall
\mathrm{Htrees}_{n}
from (
2.32
).
Let us prove that the contribution of non-hypertrees is negligible. Summing according to the cardinality of
X_{1}\cup\cdots\cup X_{n}
gives

(4.152)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\pi)\\
\mathrm{connected}\\
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\notin\mathrm{Htrees}_{n}(\pi)\end{subarray}}\Bigr|\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X_{1})\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{1}}|\leq p}\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X_{n})\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{n}}|\leq p}\mathrm{I}(G(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}))\Bigr|\\
\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{N^{k}}{k!}\max_{Y\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):|Y|=k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\pi)\\
\mathrm{connected}\\
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\notin\mathrm{Htrees}_{n}(\pi)\\
X_{1}\cup\cdots\cup X_{n}=Y\end{subarray}}\Bigr|\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X_{1})\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{1}}|\leq p}\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X_{n})\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{n}}|\leq p}\mathrm{I}(G(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}))\Bigr|.

If
|V_{X_{i}}|\leq p
, then by the scaling of
\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}
in Lemma
4.22
,

(4.153)

\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X_{i})=N^{1-|X_{i}|}(\mathsf{k}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(\#_{1}(X_{i}),\ldots,\#_{p}(X_{i}))+o_{N}(1)).

Therefore, the term
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
contributes to the limit after dividing by
N
if and only if

(4.154)

\Bigr|\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\Bigr|+n-\sum_{i=1}^{n}|X_{i}|=1.

(Notice that the left-hand side is always smaller than
1
). One can check by induction that (
4.154
) is equivalent to
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\in\mathrm{Htrees}_{n}(\pi)
. Thus,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\pi)\\
\mathrm{connected}\\
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\notin\mathrm{Htrees}_{n}(\pi)\end{subarray}}\Bigr|\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X_{1})\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{1}}|\leq p}\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X_{n})\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{n}}|\leq p}\mathrm{I}(G(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}))\Bigr|=o(N).

Hence, recalling that if
G(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
is a tree, then
\mathrm{I}(G(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}))=(-1)^{n-1}
, we obtain

(4.155)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\pi)\\
\mathrm{connected}\end{subarray}}\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X_{1})\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{1}}|\leq p}\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X_{n})\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{n}}|\leq p}\mathrm{I}(G(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}))\\
=(-1)^{n-1}\sum_{(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\in\mathrm{Htrees}_{n}(\pi)}\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X_{1})\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{1}}|\leq p}\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X_{n})\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{n}}|\leq p}.

We now sum the above display according to the number of multipoles of cardinality
i
in
X_{1}\cup\cdots\cup X_{n}
:

(4.156)

\sum_{(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\in\mathrm{Htrees}_{n}(\pi)}\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X_{1})\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{1}}|\leq p}\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X_{n})\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{n}}|\leq p}\\
=\sum_{m_{1},\ldots,m_{p}\geq 0}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Y\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):\\
\forall i\in[p],\#_{i}(Y)=m_{i}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\pi)\\
X_{1}\cup\cdots\cup X_{n}=Y\end{subarray}}\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X_{1})\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{1}}|\leq p}\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X_{n})\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{n}}|\leq p}.

Given
m_{1},\ldots,m_{p}
notice that the number of ways to choose
Y\subset\pi
with
m_{i}
parts of cardinality
i
for every
i\in[p]
is given by

\prod_{i=1}^{p}\binom{n_{i}}{m_{i}}.

Moreover, recalling that
n_{i}=N\gamma_{i}
and using Stirling’s formula,

\log\prod_{i=1}^{p}\binom{n_{i}}{m_{i}}=\log\left(N^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{p}}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\frac{\gamma_{i}^{m_{i}}}{m_{i}!}\right)+O(\log N).

Recall from Definition
2.13

Y(m_{1},\ldots,m_{p})
the canonical partition of
[m_{1}+2m_{2}+\cdots+km_{p}]
with
m_{i}
blocks of cardinality
i
for every
i\in[p]
. Then, combining the two above displays with (
4.153
), we obtain

(4.157)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Y\in\mathcal{P}(\pi):\\
\forall i\in[p],\#_{i}(Y)=m_{i}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\in\mathrm{Htrees}_{n}(\pi)\\
X_{1}\cup\cdots\cup X_{n}=Y\end{subarray}}\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X_{1})\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{1}}|\leq p}\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X_{n})\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{n}}|\leq p}\\
=N\prod_{i=1}^{p}\frac{\gamma_{i}^{m_{i}}}{m_{i}!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\in\mathrm{Htrees}_{n}(Y(m_{1},\ldots,m_{p}))\\
X_{1}\cup\cdots\cup X_{n}=Y(m_{1},\ldots,m_{p})\end{subarray}}\mathsf{k}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(\#X_{1})\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{1}}|\leq p}\cdots\mathsf{k}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(\#X_{n})\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{n}}|\leq p}+o(N).

Combining (
4.155
), (
4.156
) and (
4.157
) gives

(4.158)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\pi)\\
\mathrm{connected}\end{subarray}}\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X_{1})\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{1}}|\leq p}\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(X_{n})\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{n}}|\leq p}\mathrm{I}(G(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}))\\
=N(-1)^{n-1}\sum_{m_{1},\ldots,m_{p}}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\frac{\gamma_{i}^{m_{i}}}{m_{i}!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\in\mathrm{Htrees}_{n}(Y(m_{1},\ldots,m_{p}))\\
X_{1}\cup\cdots\cup X_{n}=Y(m_{1},\ldots,m_{p})\end{subarray}}\mathsf{k}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(\#X_{1})\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{1}}|\leq p}\cdots\mathsf{k}_{\beta,\lambda}^{\mathrm{mult}}(\#X_{n})\mathbf{1}_{|V_{X_{n}}|\leq p}\\
+o(N).

Summing over
n
and combining this with (
4.151
) concludes the proof of (
4.150
), hence the result.
∎

5.
Large deviations upper bound

In this section, we establish an upper bound on the partition function. Unlike in the case of the lower bound, we cannot assume that all dipoles have size smaller than
\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}
and that all multipoles have cardinality smaller than
p(\beta)
. We must consider all configurations. However, the absolute convergence of the cluster expansion series in Proposition
4.23
crucially relied on the bounded (by
\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}
) size of dipoles and the bounded cardinality of multipoles.

5.1.
Strategy for the upper bound

The key idea of the proof is to isolate a family of “good dipoles” and to perform a cluster expansion on the partition function of this subsystem, following the approach of Section
4
.
A major difficulty is that good dipoles do not form an isolated system: they still interact with bad dipoles.
A dipole is declared good if it satisfies the following three conditions:

(1)

its length is smaller than
\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}
;

(2)

it belongs to a multipole of cardinality smaller than
p(\beta)
, and the number of such multipoles is not too large;

(3)

it contains no “internal” bad dipole of either of the two previously defined types whose size is smaller than its own.

In Section
5.2
we introduce a reduced model by applying the energy lower bound of Proposition
3.3
with carefully chosen radii.
For a good point, the radius is taken to be a fixed fraction of its distance to the closest bad point, with a cut-off at
\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}
.
Furthermore, the radii of bad points are chosen small enough to prevent overcrowding of too many same-sign particles.

In Section
5.4
we isolate the good dipoles and write their partition function as a cluster-expansion series, within the framework of Section
4.6
.
The corresponding activity estimates are stated in Section
5.5
.

A key fact in Section
4
was that the graphs weighted by
a_{ij}^{\tilde{v}}
contributing to the activity are Eulerian, hence 2-edge-connected.
Here, because of the interaction with bad dipoles, the analogous graphs with odd weights are not always Eulerian or 2-edge-connected.
We decompose these graphs into a 2-core consisting of a tree of 2-edge-connected blocks, to which pendant trees are attached.
By a parity argument, every leaf of a pendant tree interacts with bad points.
Using fine geometric properties of the configuration and our choice of radii, we show that the smallness of the good-bad interactions compensates for the loss of 2-edge-connectivity.
This is done in Sections
5.7
and
5.8
.
Since the contributing graphs obey exactly the same bound as in Corollary
4.43
, we can import the analysis of Section
4
to establish the activity estimates.

Thanks to the choice of radii for bad points, we may bound their energy from below, allowing us to reduce to a system of non-interacting dipoles.
Sections
5.12
,
5.13
and
5.14
control the total number of bad points.
Together with the cluster-expansion estimate for good dipoles, this yields the desired LDP upper bound.

Combining this upper bound with the LDP lower bound of Proposition
4.57
proves the full LDP of Theorem
2
.
As a corollary, we obtain an expansion of the partition function expressed through the infimum of the rate function
\mathcal{I}_{\beta,p(\beta),\lambda}
of Definition
2.14
.
Our final aim is to simplify that expression and derive the expansion of Theorem
1
. By the LDP and the activity estimates, the partition function can be approximated by a sum over clusters whose graphs have small connected components and small multipoles.
A Möbius inversion transform shows that this auxiliary truncated activity coincides with the dipole activity of Definition
2.4
, which in turn identifies the limiting free energy.

5.2.
Definition of bad points and of a reduced model

We first define clusters of positive charges and clusters of negative charges.

Roughly speaking, we grow balls of equal radii equal to
\tau
around all the positive charges. As soon as
\tau
equals
r_{i}
(the distance from
x_{i}
to its match
y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}
), we remove the point and its associated ball. Moreover, as soon as a ball intersects
q(\beta)
(a fixed parameter) other balls, then remove
B(x_{i},\tau)
and all the balls touching it. The erased points are declared to be a cluster. Stop the process when
\tau
reaches
\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}
and declare each remaining charge to be a (singleton) cluster.

Definition 5.1

(
q
-clustering of same-sign particles)
.

Let
q\geq 2
be a fixed parameter.
Let
(x_{i})_{1\leq i\leq N}\subset\Lambda^{N}
and
(y_{i})_{1\leq i\leq N}\subset\Lambda^{N}
. For each
i
, let
r_{i}=|x_{i}-y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}|
where
\sigma_{N}
is the stable matching from Definition
4.1
. A
q
-clustering of
\{x_{i}\}
is a partition of
[N]
and a list of radii given by the following iteration:

•

Define
I_{0}\coloneqq[N]

and
\mathrm{Clus}_{0}^{+}\coloneqq\emptyset.

•

Having defined
I_{n-1}
and
\mathrm{Clus}_{n-1}^{+}
,

(1)

If
I_{n-1}=\emptyset
, stop and let
\mathrm{Clus}_{n-1}^{+}
be the final cluster; otherwise

(2)

Let
\tau
be given by the minimum of
\frac{1}{4}\min\{r_{i}:i\in I_{n-1}\}
and the minimal
t
such that some ball
B(x_{i},t)
for
i\in I_{n-1}
intersects at least
q
other balls
B(x_{j},\tau)
for
j\in I_{n-1}
.

–

If
\tau\geq\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}
, then set
\mathrm{Clus}_{n}^{+}\coloneqq\mathrm{Clus}_{n-1}^{+}\sqcup\{\{i\}:i\in I_{n-1}\}
and
I_{n}\coloneqq\emptyset
.

–

If
\tau=\frac{1}{4}r_{i}
for some
i\in I_{n-1}
, set
\mathrm{Clus}_{n}^{+}\coloneqq\mathrm{Clus}_{n-1}^{+}\sqcup\{i\}
,
\tau_{i}^{+}\coloneqq\frac{1}{4}r_{i}
and
I_{n}\coloneqq I_{n-1}\setminus\{i\}
.

–

If
\tau
is such that
B(x_{i},\tau)
intersects at least
q
other balls
B(x_{j},\tau)
, set
\mathrm{Clus}_{n}^{+}=\mathrm{Clus}_{n-1}^{+}\sqcup J
and
I_{n}=I_{n-1}\setminus J

where
J
is the set of
j\in I_{n-1}
such that
B(x_{j},\tau)
intersects
B(x_{i},\tau)
. (Notice
i\in J
).

•

Denote
\mathrm{Clus}_{N}^{+}
as the set of clusters obtained and for every
i\in[N]
, denote
\tau_{i}^{+}
as the maximum between
\lambda
and the value of
\tau
at which
B(x_{i},\tau)
has been removed. By exchanging the roles of the positive and negative charges above, define a
q
-clustering of negative charges, denoted
\mathrm{Clus}_{N}^{-}
with an associated list of radii
\tau_{i}^{-}
.

Up to removing a set of measure
0
we will assume that at the removal time
\tau
,
B(x_{i},\tau)
intersects exactly
q
other balls.

We first declare bad the dipoles containing one point that has disappeared before its radius has reached
\max(\frac{1}{4}r_{i},\lambda)
.

Definition 5.2

(Bad points of type 1)
.

Let us define

(5.1)

q(\beta)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\frac{3}{2-\beta/2}-1&\text{if $\beta\in(2,4)$}\\
p_{0}&\text{if $\beta\geq 4$}.\end{cases}

Let
\tau_{i}^{+}
and
\tau_{i}^{-}
be the radii given by the
q(\beta)
-clustering algorithm of Definition
5.1
.

We define

J_{\mathrm{bad}}^{1,+}\coloneqq\{i\in[N]:\tau_{i}^{+}<\tfrac{1}{4}\max(r_{i},\lambda)\}\quad\text{and}\quad J_{\mathrm{bad}}^{1,-}\coloneqq\{i\in[N]:\tau_{i}^{-}<\tfrac{1}{4}\max(r_{i},\lambda)\}.

We also let

I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{1}\coloneqq\{i\in[N]:i\in J_{\mathrm{bad}}^{1,+}\text{ or }\sigma_{N}(i)\in J_{\mathrm{bad}}^{1,-}\}.

Next, we include every dipole that belongs to a multipole whose size exceeds
p(\beta)
and all small-cardinality multipoles whenever their total count surpasses the permitted threshold.

Definition 5.3

(Bad points of type
2
)
.

Recall
p(\beta)
from Definition
2.7
. We let
I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{2,1}
be the set of
i\notin I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{1}
such that
|[i]^{\Pi_{\mathrm{mult}}}|>p(\beta)
.

Moreover, for every
k\in\{1,\ldots,p(\beta)\}
, we let

I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{2,2,k}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\{i\notin I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{1}\cup I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{2,1}:|[i]^{\Pi_{\mathrm{mult}}}|=k\}&\text{if $|\{i\notin I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{1}\cup I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{2,1}:|[i]^{\Pi_{\mathrm{mult}}}|=k\}|>\varepsilon_{0}^{-\alpha(\beta)}N\lambda^{2(k-1)}$}\\
\emptyset&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Set

I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{2,2}\coloneqq\bigcup_{k\in[p(\beta)]}I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{2,2,k}

and

I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{2}\coloneqq I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{2,1}\cup I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{2,2}.

Finally, we discard the dipoles having an “internal” bad dipole and take the closure by multipole of this set.

Definition 5.4

(Bad points of type
3
)
.

We let
J_{\mathrm{bad}}^{3}
be the set of
i\in[N]\setminus(I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{1}\cup I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{2})
such that there exists
j\in I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{1}\cup I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{2}
such that

(5.2)

d_{ij}\leq M\max(r_{i},\lambda).

We then let

I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{3}\coloneqq\Bigr\{i\in[N]\setminus(I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{1}\cup I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{2}):[i]^{\Pi_{\mathrm{mult}}}\cap J_{\mathrm{bad}}^{3}\neq\emptyset\Bigr\}.

Definition 5.5

(Good and bad points)
.

We let

I_{\mathrm{bad}}\coloneqq I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{1}\cup I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{2}\cup I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{3}

and finally set

I_{\mathrm{good}}\coloneqq[N]\setminus I_{\mathrm{bad}}.

Remark 5.6
.

(1)

Note that for the definition of the bad indices, we have taken the closure under the multipole relation, so that
i\in I_{\mathrm{good}}
and
j\in I_{\mathrm{bad}}
implies that
i
and
j
are not in the same multipole.

(2)

Since
q(\beta)\geq p(\beta)
, we have
i\notin I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{1}\cup I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{2}
if and only if
i\notin I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{2}
and
r_{i}\leq\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}
.

Definition 5.7
.

We let
Z_{\mathrm{bad}}=\{\{x_{i},y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}\},i\in I_{\mathrm{bad}}\}
and
for every
i\in I_{\mathrm{good}}
, we let

(5.3)

d_{i,\mathrm{bad}}\coloneqq\min(\mathrm{dist}(\{x_{i},y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}\},Z_{\mathrm{bad}}),4\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}).

For every
S\subset I_{\mathrm{good}}
, we let

(5.4)

d_{S,\mathrm{bad}}=\inf_{i\in S}d_{i,\mathrm{bad}}.

We next apply the result of the ball growth method of Proposition
3.3
by choosing the enlargement radii as follows:

Definition 5.8

(Interaction for the energy lower bound)
.

Let
Z=Z_{\mathrm{bad}}
, let
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
.

•

For every
i\in I_{\mathrm{good}}
, we let

(5.5)

\tau_{i}^{+,Z}=\tau_{i}^{-,Z}=\max(\tfrac{1}{4}d_{i,\mathrm{bad}},\lambda).

•

For every
i\in I_{\mathrm{bad}}
, we let

\tau_{i}^{+,Z}=\tau_{i}^{+}\text{and}\quad\tau_{i}^{-,Z}=\tau_{i}^{-}

where
\tau_{i}^{+}
and
\tau_{i}^{-}
are as in Definition
5.1
. Note that
\tau_{i}^{\pm}\leq\varepsilon_{0}\bar{R}_{\beta,\lambda}
.

For every
i,j\in[N]
with
i\neq j
, define

(5.6)

v_{i,j,+}^{Z}\coloneqq\Bigr(\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}-\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{i}^{+,Z})}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{j}^{+,Z})}\Bigr)(x_{i}-x_{j})-\Bigr(\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}-\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{i}^{-,Z})}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{j}^{+,Z})}\Bigr)(y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}-x_{j})

and

(5.7)

v_{i,j,-}^{Z}\coloneqq\Bigr(\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}-\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{i}^{+,Z})}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{j}^{-,Z})}\Bigr)(x_{i}-y_{\sigma_{N}(j)})-\Bigr(\mathsf{g}_{\lambda}-\mathsf{g}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{i}^{-,Z})}*\delta_{0}^{(\tau_{j}^{-,Z})}\Bigr)(y_{\sigma_{N}(i)}-y_{\sigma_{N}(j)}),

and

(5.8)

v_{ij}^{Z}\coloneqq v_{i,j,+}^{Z}-v_{i,j,-}^{Z}.

Recalling that
v_{ij}^{Z}=v_{ij}^{\prime}
, we see that
v_{i,j,+}^{Z}
is nonzero only if
B(x_{i},\tau_{i}^{+,Z})\cup B(y_{\sigma_{N}(i)},\tau_{i}^{-,Z})
intersects
B(x_{j},\tau_{j}^{+,Z})
. The definition of the radii will ensure that the good points do not interact too much with bad points, and that each bad point does not have an attractive interaction with more than a bounded number of charges per scale.

Lemma 5.9

(Geometric properties)
.

Recall that the constant
M
in Definition
2.2
satisfies
M>10
. Let
\Pi_{\mathrm{mult}}
be the partition of
I_{\mathrm{good}}
into multipoles. Suppose that for every
S\in\Pi_{\mathrm{mult}}
, we have
|S|\leq p(\beta)
.

(1)

Let
i\in I_{\mathrm{good}}
. We have

(5.9)

d_{i,\mathrm{bad}}\geq\max(r_{i},\lambda).

(2)

Let
i\in I_{\mathrm{good}}
and
j\in I_{\mathrm{good}}
be such that
i
and
j
belong to the same multipole. Then, there exists
C>0
depending on
M
and
p(\beta)
such that

(5.10)

d_{i,\mathrm{bad}}\leq Cd_{j,\mathrm{bad}}.

(3)

Let
i\in I_{\mathrm{good}}
and
j\in I_{\mathrm{bad}}
be such that
v_{i,j,+}^{Z}\neq 0
. Then,

(5.11)

d_{i,j,+}\leq 2\tau_{j}^{+,Z}\quad\text{and}\quad\max(r_{i},\lambda)\leq\max(r_{j},\lambda).

(4)

Let
i\in I_{\mathrm{good}}
and
j\in I_{\mathrm{bad}}
be such that
v_{i,j,-}^{Z}\neq 0
. Then,

(5.12)

d_{i,j,-}\leq 2\tau_{j}^{-,Z}\quad\text{and}\quad\max(r_{i},\lambda)\leq\max(r_{j},\lambda).

(5)

Let
i_{1},\ldots,i_{K}\in I_{\mathrm{good}}
. Suppose that

v_{i_{1}i_{2}}^{Z}\cdots v_{i_{K-1}i_{K}}^{Z}\neq 0.

Then,

(5.13)

d_{i_{K},\mathrm{bad}}\leq 4^{K}d_{i_{1},\mathrm{bad}}.

(6)

Let
i_{1},\ldots,i_{K}\in I_{\mathrm{good}}
and
i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{K}^{\prime}\in I_{\mathrm{good}}
all distinct. Suppose that for every
l\in\{1,\ldots,K-1\}
,
[i_{l}^{\prime}]^{\Pi_{\mathrm{mult}}}=[i_{l+1}]^{\Pi_{\mathrm{mult}}}
. Suppose that

v_{i_{1}i_{1}^{\prime}}^{Z}\ldots v_{i_{K}i_{K}^{\prime}}^{Z}\neq 0.

Then, there exists a constant
C>0
depending on
\beta
and
p(\beta)
such that

(5.14)

d_{i_{K}^{\prime},\mathrm{bad}}\leq C^{K}d_{i_{1},\mathrm{bad}}.

(7)

Let
t\geq 0
and
A\geq\frac{1}{2}
. Then, there exists a constant
C>0
depending on
q(\beta)
and
A
such that for every
z\in\Lambda
,

(5.15)

|\{i\in I_{\mathrm{bad}}:x_{i}\in B(z,At),\tau_{i}^{+,Z}\geq t\text{ and }\tau_{i}^{+,Z}>\lambda\}|\leq C,

(5.16)

|\{i\in I_{\mathrm{bad}}:y_{i}\in B(z,At),\tau_{i}^{-,Z}\geq t\text{ and }\tau_{i}^{-,Z}>\lambda\}|\leq C.

Remark 5.10
.

In particular, (
5.9
) and (
5.5
) imply that for every
i\in I_{\mathrm{good}}
,

\tau_{i}^{+,Z}=\tau_{i}^{-,Z}\geq\frac{r_{i}}{4},

and thus
r_{i}\leq 2(\tau_{i}^{+,Z}+\tau_{i}^{-,Z})
.

If
i\in I_{\mathrm{bad}}
, then by construction, we always have
\tau_{i}^{\pm,Z}\leq\max(\frac{1}{4}r_{i},\lambda)
. Thus, if
i\in I_{\mathrm{bad}}
,
either
\frac{1}{4}r_{i}<\lambda
in which case by construction
\tau_{i}^{+,Z}=\tau_{i}^{-,Z}=\lambda

and
r_{i}\leq 2(\tau_{i}^{+,Z}+\tau_{i}^{-,Z})
, or
\frac{1}{4}r_{i}\geq\lambda
in which case

2(\tau_{i}^{+,Z}+\tau_{i}^{-,Z})\leq 4\max(\frac{1}{4}r_{i},\lambda)\leq r_{i}
.

Proof.

We begin by proving (
5.9
). Let
i\in I_{\mathrm{good}}
and
j\in I_{\mathrm{bad}}
be such that

(5.17)

d_{ij}\leq\max(r_{i},\lambda)\quad\text{and}\quad\max(r_{j},\lambda)\leq\frac{1}{3}\max(r_{i},\lambda).

\bullet
Since
i\notin J_{\mathrm{bad}}^{3}
, by definition (
5.2
),
j\notin I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{1}\cup I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{2}
, hence
j\in I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{3}
. Now, suppose by contradiction that
j\in J_{\mathrm{bad}}^{3}
. Then, there exists
k\in I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{1}\cup I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{2}
such that
d_{jk}\leq M\max(r_{j},\lambda)
. Hence, by the triangle inequality,

d_{ik}\leq d_{ij}+d_{jk}\leq\max(r_{i},\lambda)+M\max(r_{j},\lambda)\leq(1+\tfrac{M}{3})\max(r_{i},\lambda)<M\max(r_{i},\lambda).

Hence,
i\in J_{\mathrm{bad}}^{3}
, which is a contradiction. Therefore
j\in I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{3}\setminus J_{\mathrm{bad}}^{3}
. It follows that there exists
l\in J_{\mathrm{bad}}^{3}
such that
j
and
l
are in the same multipole.

\bullet
We have

d_{jl}\leq M\max(\min(r_{j},r_{l}),\lambda)\leq M\max(r_{j},\lambda)\leq\frac{M}{3}\max(r_{i},\lambda).

Now, suppose by contradiction that
r_{l}\geq\frac{r_{i}}{2}
.
We also have
d_{ij}\leq\max(r_{i},\lambda)
and therefore

d_{il}\leq d_{ij}+d_{jl}\leq(1+\tfrac{M}{3})\max(r_{i},\lambda)\leq(1+\tfrac{M}{3})2\max(r_{l},\lambda)\leq M\max(r_{l},\lambda).

The above display shows that

d_{il}\leq M\max(\min(r_{i},r_{l}),\lambda)

and therefore
i
and
l
belong to the same multipole, which is impossible since
i\in I_{\mathrm{good}}
and
l\in I_{\mathrm{bad}}
. Thus,
r_{l}\leq\frac{r_{i}}{2}
.

\bullet
Since
l\in J_{\mathrm{bad}}^{3}
, there exists
k\in I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{1}\cup I_{\mathrm{bad}}^{2}
such that
d_{lk}\leq M\max(r_{l},\lambda).
It follows that

d_{ik}\leq d_{ij}+d_{jl}+d_{lk}\leq\max(r_{i},\lambda)+M\max(r_{j},\lambda)+M\max(r_{l},\lambda)\leq(1+\tfrac{M}{3}+\tfrac{M}{2})\max(r_{i},\lambda)\leq M\max(r_{i},\lambda).

Hence
i\in J_{\mathrm{bad}}^{3}
which is impossible. Thus, we deduce that the set of
j
’s satisfying (
5.17
) is empty.

Hence, if
d_{ij}\leq\max(r_{i},\lambda)
, then
\max(r_{j},\lambda)\geq\frac{1}{3}\max(r_{i},\lambda)
and therefore

d_{ij}\leq 3\max(\min(r_{i},r_{j}),\lambda),

implying that
i
and
j
are in the same multipole. This proves (
5.9
).

We turn to the proof of (
5.10
). One can suppose without loss of generality that
d_{i,\mathrm{bad}}\geq d_{j,\mathrm{bad}}
. By the triangle inequality,

|d_{i,\mathrm{bad}}-d_{j,\mathrm{bad}}|\leq d_{ij}.

Since
i
and
j
are in the same multipole, there exists a path
P
in
S
from
i
to
j
such that for every
ab\in P
,
d_{ab}\leq M\max(\min(r_{a},r_{b}),\lambda)
. Using the triangle inequality, we deduce that

d_{ij}\leq M(|S|-1)\max(\min(r_{i},r_{j}),\lambda).

By (
5.9
) and since
|S|\leq p(\beta)
,

d_{ij}\leq 4M(p(\beta)-1)\min(d_{i,\mathrm{bad}},d_{j,\mathrm{bad}})=4M(p(\beta)-1)d_{j,\mathrm{bad}}.

It follows that

d_{i,\mathrm{bad}}\leq d_{j,\mathrm{bad}}(1+4M(p(\beta)-1)),

which concludes the proof.

We now prove (
5.11
). By (
3.46
) (or Newton’s theorem),
v^{Z}_{i,j,+}\neq 0
implies that

(5.18)

d_{i,j}^{+}\leq\tau_{i}^{Z}+\tau_{j}^{+,Z}.

By definition, we have

\tau_{i}^{Z}\leq\max(\frac{1}{4}d_{ij},\lambda)\leq\max(\frac{1}{4}d_{i,j}^{+},\lambda)
. Therefore, by (
5.18
),

d_{i,j}^{+}\leq\max(\tfrac{1}{4}d_{i,j}^{+},\lambda)+\tau_{j}^{+,Z}.

Hence, since
\tau_{j}^{+,Z}\geq\lambda
,

\max(d_{i,j}^{+},\lambda)\leq\tfrac{4}{3}\tau_{j}^{+,Z}.

This proves the first part of the statement. In particular, since
j\in I_{\mathrm{bad}}
,
d_{i,j}^{+}\leq 4\max(r_{j},\lambda)
. If
\max(r_{i},\lambda)\geq\max(r_{j},\lambda)
, then this would imply that
i
and
j
are in the same multipole, hence the contradiction. This proves (
5.11
). The proof of (
5.12
) is similar.

Let us prove (
5.13
). Let
k\in\{1,\ldots,K-1\}
. By the triangle inequality,

(5.19)

d_{i_{k+1},\mathrm{bad}}\leq d_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}+d_{i_{k},\mathrm{bad}}.

Since
v^{Z}_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}\neq 0
, by Lemma
3.9
, we have

(5.20)

d_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}\leq\tau_{i_{k}}^{\pm,Z}+\tau_{i_{k+1}}^{\pm,Z}.

Recall that

\tau_{i_{k+1}}^{\pm,Z}\leq\max(\tfrac{1}{4}d_{i_{k+1},\mathrm{bad}},\lambda).

Hence, by the triangle inequality,

\tau_{i_{k+1}}^{\pm,Z}\leq\max(\tfrac{1}{4}d_{i_{k},\mathrm{bad}},\lambda)+\frac{1}{4}d_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}.

Using that

\tau_{i_{k}}^{\pm,Z}\leq\max(\tfrac{1}{4}d_{i_{k},\mathrm{bad}},\lambda),

we therefore get from (
5.20
) that

(5.21)

d_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}\leq\frac{8}{3}\max(\tfrac{1}{4}d_{i_{k},\mathrm{bad}},\lambda)\leq 3\max(d_{i_{k},\mathrm{bad}},\lambda).

Therefore, using (
5.19
), we get

(5.22)

d_{i_{k+1},\mathrm{bad}}\leq 4\max(d_{i_{k},\mathrm{bad}},\lambda),

therefore concluding the proof of (
5.13
) in view of (
5.9
).

Let us prove (
5.14
). By (
5.10
), there exists
C_{0}>0
depending on
M
and
p(\beta)
such that for every
i,j
in the same multipole,

(5.23)

d_{i,\mathrm{bad}}\leq C_{0}d_{j,\mathrm{bad}},

Let
k\in\{1,\ldots,K-1\}
. By (
5.22
),

d_{i_{k}^{\prime},\mathrm{bad}}\leq 4\max(d_{i_{k},\mathrm{bad}},\lambda)=4d_{i_{k},\mathrm{bad}}.

Therefore, by (
5.23
),

d_{i_{k+1},\mathrm{bad}}\leq 4C_{0}d_{i_{k},\mathrm{bad}}.

We deduce that

d_{i_{K},\mathrm{bad}}\leq(4C_{0})^{K}d_{i_{1},\mathrm{bad}}

and that

d_{i_{K}^{\prime},\mathrm{bad}}\leq C_{0}(4C_{0})^{K}d_{i_{1},\mathrm{bad}},

which proves the result.

Finally, we turn to the proof of (
5.15
). By covering the ball
B(z,tA)
with
O(A^{2})
balls of radius
t
, it is enough to prove the result for
A=\frac{1}{2}
. Let
z\in\Lambda
and
t\geq 0
. Set

I\coloneqq\{i\in I_{\mathrm{bad}}:x_{i}\in B(z,\tfrac{1}{2}t),\tau_{i}^{+,Z}\geq t,\tau_{i}^{+,Z}>\lambda\}.

Note that for
i\in I
, since
\tau_{i}^{+,Z}>\lambda
, the radius
\tau_{i}^{+,Z}
corresponds to the
\tau
at which
B(x_{i},\tau)
disappears in the clustering algorithm of Definition
5.1
. Suppose by contradiction that
|I|>q(\beta)+1
. Then, let

\tau\coloneqq\min_{i\in I}\tau_{i}^{+,Z}.

By the definition of
I
, we have
\tau\geq t
. Moreover, if
x_{i},x_{j}\in I
then
B(x_{i},\tau)\cap B(x_{j},\tau)\neq\emptyset
since
|x_{i}-x_{j}|\leq t
and
\tau\geq t
. It follows that there exists
i\in I
and
q(\beta)+1
other distinct points
j\in I
such that
B(x_{i},\tau)
intersects each of the
B(x_{j},\tau)
. Since
\tau=\min_{i\in I}\tau_{i}^{+,Z}
and since
\tau>\lambda
, this is a contradiction when
t
approaches
\tau
from below. We conclude that
|I|\leq q(\beta)+1
, which proves the result.
∎

Lemma 5.11

(Control of good-bad interactions)
.

Let
(\vec{X}_{N},\vec{Y}_{N})\in\Lambda^{2N}
. Suppose that the
r_{i}
are all distinct. Let
d_{i,\mathrm{bad}}
be as in (
5.3
).

Then, there exists a constant
C>0
depending on
q(\beta)
such that for every
i\in I_{\mathrm{good}}
,

(5.24)

\sum_{j\in I_{\mathrm{bad}}}|v^{Z}_{ij}|\leq C\frac{r_{i}}{d_{i,\mathrm{bad}}}.

Proof.

First, we write

(5.25)

\sum_{j\in I_{\mathrm{bad}}}|v^{Z}_{ij}|=\sum_{j\in I_{\mathrm{bad}}}|v^{Z}_{i,j,+}|+\sum_{j\in I_{\mathrm{bad}}}|v^{Z}_{i,j,-}|,

where
v_{i,j,+}^{Z}
and
v_{i,j,-}^{Z}
are as in (
5.6
) and (
5.7
).

Let
j\in I_{\mathrm{bad}}
. By Lemma
5.9
, if
v_{i,j,+}^{Z}\neq 0
, then
d_{i,j}^{+}\leq 2\tau_{j}^{Z,+}
and
\max(r_{i},\lambda)\leq\max(r_{j},\lambda)
. Moreover, since dipoles in
I_{\mathrm{good}}
and
I_{\mathrm{bad}}
are not in the same multipole, we have
d_{i,\mathrm{bad}}\geq M\max(r_{i},\lambda)
.
Moreover,
d_{i,j}^{+}\geq d_{i,\mathrm{bad}}
. Finally, since
i\in I_{\mathrm{good}}
, we have
\tau_{i}^{+}=\tau_{i}^{-}
. We may thus apply Lemma
3.9
and in particular (
3.46
), to find

(5.26)
